Anthropic warns small AI models could self-replicate through IoT devices
2026-10-14

Anthropic warns small AI models could self-replicate through IoT devices

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
AI company Anthropic is warning that advances in small AI models could soon usher in a new era of self-replicating malware that’s capable of infecting smart fridges, vacuum cleaners, and a wide range of other Internet-of-things devices and is more resistant to takedown. By next year, smaller AI models may be able to autonomously self-replicate on all kinds of IoT devices, the company said. Eventually, the capability could enable computer worms, which propagate without requiring human interaction and are capable of infecting unfamiliar IoT devices. An experiment the company performed recently revealed significant challenges in bringing such an AI malware replicator to fruition, so the finding is necessarily tempered. Still, the results do raise concerns about the possibility of new forms of computer worms. Anthropic further warned not only about the new potential malware, but also that it could be difficult to stop. In its blog post, it wrote that “a replicator like this would be challenging to shut down, since there’s no centralized point to shut off—though it may be slow (with a doubling time of days). Unlike non-AI malware, it could also figure out unfamiliar devices on its own.” Smaller AI models require much less powerful hardware to run than do frontier models. Anthropic ran small open-weight models on real or simulated low-cost, off-brand Android TV boxes and other types of IoT hardware. Company researchers found that by using quantization to represent the models using fewer bits and splitting the workloads into “shards” running on each device, the models were able to establish inference, meaning that the model weights loaded and could generate output. The lightweight Gemma4-26B-A4B model required just 15 boxes, each equipped with only 4GB of memory, to achieve inference. The Qwen3.5-9B was able to configure other LLMs to run on eight of nine hardware types. Unlike traditional malware, which must be written to run on specific hardware or software, the model was able to use its weights to make configurations on the fly that were specific to the devices it was tested against. This finding was the starting point for further research that asked: Would it be possible for small models to create self-replicating infections that run themselves on IoT devices the way traditional IoT worms, like Mirai, install DDoS code and other payloads? Unlike Mirai, these AI worms could pose a much higher threat, since the models could tailor exploits and payloads dynamically, depending on the target. Once such an infection chain began, it might also be harder for defenders to stop it. “Such an ‘AI worm’ could carry its model weights with it as it reproduces (or re-download them from a public hub), and then run inference directly on compromised hardware,” Anthropic said. “Effectively, the weights would become part of the malware payload.” Anthropic called these autonomous worms small weight-based replicators. The models ran agents that had access to common exploitation tools, like reverse shells. The flow of the attacks was (1) finding targets, (2) gaining code execution, (3) establishing inference, and (4) propagating to new hosts. A key benefit over traditional worms is what Anthropic said is the agents’ ability to learn on the job. Each time an AI replicator fails to compromise a specific platform device, it can leave a list of prompts, code, and notes to future replicators so they don’t waste time reusing the same ineffective steps. Credit: Anthropic Credit: Anthropic Anthropic said the dynamic inference setup is the least studied and likely most challenging stage in the replication cycle. It requires a long sequence of decisions based on the specific hardware and software of each target and involves selecting compatible inference software, installing dependencies, and debugging platform-specific bugs. “These steps are serially dependent, and failures are difficult to recover from,” Anthropic said. The experiment shows that small models are becoming increasingly capable at solving those problems autonomously. At the moment, however, none of the light-weight models tested are able to mount end-to-end replication, meaning discovering new devices, using an exploit to gain access to them, infecting them with a payload, configuring an inference server, and repeating the process. The process is also painfully slow; Gemma4-26B-A4B, distributed over the 15 Android TV boxes, performed at just 0.39 tokens per second. That translates to a full-inference setup time of 71 to 150 hours for an unoptimized Gemma4-26B-A4B and 29 to 51 hours for Qwen3.5-9B. (The researchers said that the large model GLM-5.2 appears to be able to do so reliably.) The company said that 30B-parameter models can already complete many of the individual replication steps now, although they make mistakes when performing end-to-end replication. The company went on to say that if small models continue to improve at their current pace, they may be able to achieve the same limited results by the end of the year and that “small weight-based replicators may be possible soon after.” The experiment leaves a variety of important considerations unanswered. For one, the Android devices targeted by small models had an unauthenticated Android Debug Bridge exposed. This tool is used during development and debugging but should never be used in production settings, because it gives attackers access to the entire device stack. And for another, the replication wasn’t performed by the small models themselves, but rather with GLM-5.2 to orchestrate the process while the targets ran a smaller Llama model. Ultimately, the experiment demonstrates that small agentic models are becoming capable of setting up complex, distributed inference infrastructures across multiple machines. What remains to be established, however, is whether these models would initiate self-replication on their own. “Despite the well-designed experimental setup, the study does not, on its own, establish that small models would autonomously choose to replicate,” AI researcher Xiaohan Fu said in an interview. “Providing reproducible agent setups, including the exact prompts and instructions given to the models, would help the research community better understand the findings and assess their implications. Such transparency is particularly important to avoid conflating the demonstrated capability to replicate with an emerging threat of autonomous self-replication.”

The Internet’s undersea backbone is slipping into the danger zone
2026-10-14

The Internet’s undersea backbone is slipping into the danger zone

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Despite the expansion of Starlink and other communications satellite services, most of the world’s Internet traffic still travels through subsea cables crisscrossing the ocean floors. Those fiber-optic cables coexist with a network of oil and gas pipelines and submarine power cables—and all these crucial links connecting countries and continents now face growing sabotage risks as gray-zone warfare and open conflicts have become widespread. The rising risks to the world’s invisible infrastructure beneath the waves are the main subject of a new book by Elisabeth Braw, a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council who leads the Threats to the Global Maritime Order Initiative. Her book, titled Undersea War, explores the modern rise in suspicious incidents in which ships dragging their anchors for unusually long periods have cut or damaged undersea cables and pipelines, from the Baltic Sea to the Pacific Ocean. Braw acknowledges that about 60 percent of undersea cable damage is primarily due to human error during fishing and shipping activities, when weighted nets or anchors drag across the seabed. Natural hazards like storms and undersea landslides also account for a smaller proportion of such incidents. In total, between 150 and 200 cables are damaged each year and usually repaired quickly. But international tension between superpowers and open war in Europe and the Middle East have led to increased scrutiny of cable-cutting incidents, with governments investigating and sometimes prosecuting crews for allegedly causing deliberate damage to subsea cables. One of the more notorious incidents documented in the book is the “Christmas Day massacre” of 2024, when a Russian-linked oil tanker named Eagle S dragged its anchor across the seabed for about 56 miles (90 kilometers), cutting or damaging four data cables and the Estlink 2 submarine power cable connecting the Baltic and Nordic power grids. One of those data cables had also previously been severed by the Chinese cargo ship Yi Peng 3 just one month earlier in November 2024, when that ship damaged two data cables in the Baltic region. That ship’s crew was not prosecuted by European authorities, whereas the Finnish government has attempted to prosecute three senior officers of the Eagle S. On the other side of the world, the captain of the Chinese-owned ship Hong Tai was convicted by a court in Taiwan of severing an undersea telecommunications cable in February 2025 and sentenced to three years in prison in June 2025. The island democracy’s legislature later amended its laws to increase penalties for intentional damage to undersea cables or pipelines and allow for up to seven years in prison. This year has seen several notable incidents involving both overt and covert threats to undersea infrastructure, including Iranian efforts to impose fees on operators of undersea cables running through the contested Strait of Hormuz. Separately, a joint operation involving the UK, Norway, and the United States confronted Russian submarines that were practicing using a new technology to disable undersea cables in Arctic waters near Norway’s Svalbard archipelago. What happens when the Internet’s undersea backbone, not to mention crucial energy infrastructure, is no longer protected by near-universal international agreement? Ars spoke with Braw to find out more. (The interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.) Ars Technica: Right now there’s so much suspicion and tension around undersea infrastructure. But it does seem like for quite a few decades, even during the Cold War, there was a broad international framework that mostly worked where countries generally agreed not to mess around too much. Braw: Exactly. It worked for so long, and that’s why it’s so frightening to see that system that really depends on everybody doing the right thing and being a good seabed citizen, as it were, may not work for that much longer. Ars Technica: I know it’s not really any single factor, but I’m curious what you would highlight as the key contributing factors to why we’re seeing a breakdown in that general agreement. Braw: If you think about the absolutely crucial nature of subsea cables and pipelines, especially subsea cables… if they were to be disrupted for even just a minute, it would cause massive disruption to not just the New York City area, but also New York as a financial hub. It would cause operational losses, but also the loss of all that money that goes through the cables every second of every day. So if you think about the utter importance of subsea cables to modern societies and modern economies, then that makes them incredibly attractive as a target. Whereas during the Cold War, we had telephone cables, but there weren’t that many of them, and we didn’t use them as much. Yes, they were used for international phone calls, and there were also telegraph messages that could be sent. But nobody sent telegrams around the clock, and nobody made international phone calls around the clock. So they were there, but they were just less crucial. Also, despite it being a Cold War and a very dangerous few decades, there was some sort of agreement that when you sign a treaty, you uphold it. In fact, the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea was adopted in 1982 during the height of the Cold War. So there was a commitment to creating some sort of rules of the road. Today that commitment is, I don’t want to say it’s vanishing, but it’s declining among key countries in the world. And at the same time, we are seeing Russia has discovered that it’s really quite beneficial to use non-military means to weaken other countries, and this is something that Russia has come to specialize in, and other countries may yet come to specialize in. That makes the cables around the seabed highly attractive as a target. Ars Technica: There was even recent reporting on how NATO countries apparently foiled a Russian attempt to practice undersea cable sabotage, although that incident took place back in the spring. Braw: The fascinating thing about subsea cables and pipelines, but especially subsea cables, is not just that we are utterly dependent on them. They don’t sit only within countries’ territorial waters. They connect countries, so they are in international waters [where] countries don’t have jurisdiction. The oceans are a global commons, and that’s where cables live, and that’s why it’s so utterly important for countries and everybody using the oceans to take care around them so that they don’t get damaged. Then what we have seen in the past few years is that, all of a sudden, merchant vessels in the Baltic Sea and off the coast of Norway and in Taiwanese waters, all of a sudden, they got extremely sloppy and dragged anchors for long stretches, which is something that is not supposed to happen as a seafarer. You should know whether your anchor is dragging on the seabed or not, and you know where the cables are. So this is not supposed to happen, and yet it happened not just once, not just twice, but several times, and caused considerable anxiety, obviously, in the countries affected. It also caused considerable expenses to the operators because they have to get the cables repaired, and in the case of the electricity cables, it’s extremely expensive to repair them and takes a very long time. We don’t know what has caused these merchant vessels to drag their anchors across the border sea and damage cables, and we may never know because it’s not like those crew members are going to tell us what happened. We just know that these incidents were extremely suspicious. But then we have what you just brought up, that Russia and other countries may develop tools to damage other countries’ subsea cables using submarines or gadgets or drones attached to submarines, and that’s where we are today. That might yet happen, and that’s why it’s always so concerning when Russian research vessels loiter around subsea cables. What are they doing there? What is it that they are looking at? What is it they are measuring? China does the same thing. It sends research vessels that loiter in other countries’ waters for long periods of time. And you’re supposed to inform the country in whose waters you are conducting research that you are conducting research and what the nature of your research is, but China doesn’t do that. So what is it that these research vessels are doing? What is it they are researching? This is all in the context of increasing geopolitical tension, and by the way, also rapid expansion of submarine fleets: Western ones and the Russian one and the Chinese one. But also the expansion of other undersea equipment, gadgets, drones, and so forth. Ars Technica: Another point your book touched on was everything that’s been happening around the Strait of Hormuz, and how we have seen Iran either threaten to put a fee on companies that want to run undersea cables through there, or threaten to attack them. At the same time, it sounds like a number of companies have rethought undersea cable projects that were intended to run through that region. Braw: This is another fascinating aspect of the situation for subsea cables. So in the Persian Gulf, in the Strait of Hormuz, there are subsea cables, and they are indispensable not just for the countries connected by them, but also for other nearby countries. For example, one third of India’s westbound Internet traffic or digital connectivity goes through the Persian Gulf. So it just illustrates how important these cables are, and not just to countries that are immediately connected. In February of this year, the situation was that three cable projects were about to be launched or were being conducted. Then the war began, and you can imagine that when missiles are flying, drones are flying around, it’s impossible to install cables on the seabed. Because if you think about ships, that is dangerous enough, but ships are moving. Installation ships are stationary. You really are like a sitting duck, and so those projects had to be suspended or abandoned and the companies declared force majeure. But we need subsea cables. So what will happen as the world becomes more contentious, as there are more conflicts, or as more conflicts erupt? How are cable installation crews and cable repair crews supposed to do their work when there are various projectiles and other weapons flying around them or next to them on the ocean? This is where geopolitics is truly clashing with modern economies, and at the front line are cable installation crews and repair crews. And then you asked about the toll that Iran said it was going to impose. This was a new and, I have to say, unsurprising twist on the Iran war. So everybody has heard about the fact that, a few weeks into the war, Iran said it was going to charge tolls on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz, and that is a violation of UNCLOS, the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea. Iran said it was going to do that anyway, and Iran could claim that it didn’t need to uphold UNCLOS, which it has signed but not ratified. But until February, it had upheld UNCLOS, and shipping had worked well in the Strait of Hormuz. Then in March, Iran could say that it wasn’t bound by UNCLOS anymore because the US and Israel had violated the UN Charter by attacking it. So why should it uphold UNCLOS? And this is the dangerous situation we’re in, where countries can say, “Well, why should I uphold my treaty obligations when such and such country isn’t upholding that?” So what Iran said a few months into the war—after having imposed tolls on shipping for a while—was that it was going to charge tolls on the subsea cables in the Strait of Hormuz. And yes, it is a violation of UNCLOS. But it could say, “Well, the US has violated the UN Charter, so we get to do as we like too.” If you think about the implications of that, it’s not just implications for cable operators in the Strait of Hormuz. How would they make payments to Iran? How would that work? And if they don’t make payments, what would happen? But it’s also a question for other cable owners. What if other countries say, “Well, there are cables in our water, so we are going to charge a toll because countries aren’t adhering to UNCLOS anyway, so we might as well.” It’s not just that you know the world was harmonious, and all of a sudden there were some suspicious cable incidents. It is that the order on the ocean—and even on land too—is deteriorating, and in the midst of all that we are experiencing suspicious cable incidents. Ars Technica: These undersea cables are obviously of great national interest to governments and may require even military protection in some cases. But at the same time they’re being developed by private operators, including a lot of tech giants. We just saw Meta announcing it was going to lay the first petabit capacity cable across the Atlantic, and that’s part of a much broader buildout of undersea cables. What does this private ownership but public interest collision mean for who takes responsibility, especially as there are increasing threats? Braw: It’s such an important question. Ever since the first cables were laid on the seabed, they have been privately owned. The cables that then joined them were also privately owned. Then, in the 1900s, postal services in different countries took over the operation of cables. So many cables were state-owned. Then later in the 1900s, we started seeing more privately owned cables again. And today, virtually all cables are privately owned, typically by consortia. But shorter cables are often owned by one company. And a crucial development is what you also mentioned, that the hyperscalers are building their own cables for their own traffic. So, these are private operators, but [the cables are] critical national infrastructure. And what governments want may be different; in fact, it is different from what operators want. So people often ask me, “Shouldn’t operators install more cables to make sure that they always have cables that are operational?” And yes, you can say they should, but each cable is an expense in the installation and the operation of the cable. Companies will look at figures and the hard facts, and are likely to conclude in many cases that the risk of their cables being damaged in some malicious way is smaller than the expense of adding more cables, and that’s a commercial calculation, right? Whereas for national governments, it would clearly be in their interest and they would want to see more redundancy. But they don’t own the cables, and commercial operators exist to make money. The only other alternative is for governments to subsidize new cables. And I write about this in the book as well, what happened with the Matsu Islands [governed by Taiwan]. But we don’t have that sort of money to create backups for every cable on the seabed. So this is the clash, as it were, the interests of cable owners and the interests of national governments. Ars Technica: It was interesting to see how that first cable, when it was laid, was used to send a message of hope and peace. And then there were also newspapers declaring that having this near-instant communication across the oceans would basically usher in an era of world peace. So again, viewing this technology as finally bringing about world peace when clearly it hasn’t done that.

OpenAI, Google pushing new AI models easily tricked into making nudes, researchers say
2026-10-14

OpenAI, Google pushing new AI models easily tricked into making nudes, researchers say

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Safety researchers continue to warn that a large volume of AI-generated explicit content is spreading unchecked, while regulators do little to meaningfully stop it. This non-consensual content is commonly shared in text and direct messages or stored in private clouds where it’s less likely to be detected or reported, a Google co-authored study of Internet users across the United States, Australia, and the United Kingdom found this July. That’s why the “most critical intervention point” remains at the source: the harmful AI tools used to make the fake sexual images, Google researchers concluded. Yet red-teaming in recent months suggests that as some of the leading AI image generators from Google and OpenAI become more capable, they may be getting worse at blocking explicit outputs. Raising the red flag are researchers at White Circle, a public benefit corporation backed by senior leaders and researchers from Anthropic, DeepMind, Hugging Face, and OpenAI. It was founded after CEO Denis Shilov posted one of the most famous universal jailbreaks of leading models in October 2024. Bent on breaking models to conduct thorough safety testing, White Circle helps companies detect and deter threats when implementing advanced AI systems that may not always behave as expected. Testing how well text-to-image models block banned content categories is a key research direction for White Circle, and Shilov thinks their latest findings are “horrifying” enough to share with the public. In a little-noticed preprint published last month, White Circle researchers exposed flaws in the guardrails of DALL·E 3, Google’s Nano Banana 2, and GPT-Image-2. In a press release, they explained that “using just off-the-shelf tools, available online for anyone to download, and by sending API calls, White Circle was able to use the novel attack method to bypass the safety filters” and generate explicit content about 13 percent of the time. “Critically, none of these requests required custom-built or frontier AI models,” White Circle warned, so no AI expertise is needed to execute the attacks. Researchers reviewed the outputs to verify the bypass rate, which White Circle noted is “the highest of any method tested under the same standard.” The key takeaway, they suggested, is that, “at a time when AI safety has never been under more scrutiny, the defenses leading labs have built are far weaker than they acknowledge.” By publishing research like this, White Circle is positioning itself as an external evaluator of frontier labs, and it plans to open-source the entire attack pipeline so model makers can understand the true scale of the vulnerability and build stronger protections. Although the company has not widely promoted the research yet, it will share it at a prominent AI conference this November, NeurIPS 2026, which may increase scrutiny on Google and OpenAI later this fall. White Circle has shared its findings with some frontier AI labs through “mutual contacts,” Shilov told Ars, but neither Google nor OpenAI responded to Ars’ request to comment directly on vulnerabilities found in their models. Meanwhile, OpenAI and Google have released new models—ChatGPT Images 2.5 and Nano Banana 2.1—and White Circle told Ars that prompt safety testing using the same method showed “both are significantly more breakable than their previous versions.” Neither company responded after Ars shared these new findings. White Circle’s attack method can break down frontier defenses in categories, like explicit content, that seem easy to spot, which doesn’t bode well for more serious defense systems currently being updated to block other unwanted AI behavior, like hacking, Shilov told Ars. “White Circle’s working hypothesis for this is either the newer models are more sophisticated and capable of generating a wider range of content, or that safety has been deprioritized in favor of capability,” White Circle told Ars. White Circle is also in early talks with government officials about third-party safety testing, Shilov said, which he thinks should be required. Mandatory independent audits could push companies to prioritize safety testing, which analysts have suggested largely takes a backseat as AI firms dump most of their resources into compute and AI infrastructure. “They should be investing much more into safety measures and security measures around their models,” Shilov told Ars. “And for us, it seems that they’re not actually doing this.” To expose jailbreaks that are “reusable and transferable”—working across different leading models—White Circle didn’t limit itself to testing one prompt at a time. Instead, researchers sought out “patterns which work across multiple prompts and scenarios” to detect reusable attack strategies that could work across leading models. (Researchers noted that Midjourney wasn’t tested because “it does not provide a public API suitable for controlled benchmarking.”) The team has dubbed this novel attack method “Red-teaming via Iterative Strategy Evolution” (RISE). It relies on two components: an attacking model that generates jailbreaks and a judge model that verifies if the output is banned content. The “entire pipeline is built on top of widely available models,” Shilov told Ars. The attacking model is a Qwen model downloaded from Hugging Face, and the judge model is Gemini 3.8 Flash. “You don’t need to have frontier models to break defenses of a frontier model, which is very interesting,” Shilov said. A lot of time was invested in making sure that the judge model accurately flagged explicit content, Shilov said. White Circle research engineer Dmitrii Kharlapenko told Ars that when it comes to testing image generators, the whole field of red-teaming “is very poorly calibrated,” with “literally zero papers” setting up norms. For that reason, it’s an area that remains “completely plagued with reporting issues,” which is concerning to White Circle, as AI companies push to capture markets by releasing ever more eye-catching new models. For White Circle, the research aimed to build more sophisticated models that could simulate attacks on other models, Shilov explained. That way, they could “help defenders identify and address vulnerabilities before they are exploited maliciously,” the preprint said. In his ideal world, Shilov thinks AI firms shouldn’t just face mandatory external safety testing but also third-party monitoring, much like the system used to prevent nuclear threats. Then, entities like White Circle could possibly form a coalition to help detect AI threats in real time, without suffering from biases. Shilov noted that an emerging problem in the AI ecosystem is that basically anyone doing interesting safety work is likely to be contracted by frontier labs, potentially compromising their work. White Circle has partnered with frontier labs. It previously took part in Anthropic’s bug bounty program, but ultimately found that program was “very constrained in its purpose.” “The program is focused on universal jailbreaks on domains they care most about, defined by their own specific terms,” White Circle told Ars. Research like their most recent RISE paper “certainly” wouldn’t have been covered by that program, White Circle said. And conducting that work within Anthropic risked constraining White Circle from sharing it with the public. Anthropic has proposed going one step further and embedding third-party AI safety evaluators in its labs, which White Circle said would allow them to operate at a much higher level than participating in the bug bounty program. However, the public may not always be able to closely follow that work. On Tuesday, OpenAI and Anthropic urged that restrictive non-disclosure agreements should be required of external evaluators to prevent leaks of trade secrets, Reuters reported. White Circle would not be willing to sign any restrictive NDAs that might prevent public sharing of critical findings, White Circle told Ars, so if Anthropic requires NDAs, that partnership likely would not happen. Bad actors who are determined to make AI-generated nonconsensual intimate images (NCII) and child sex abuse materials (CSAM) don’t need frontier models and often use so-called nudifying apps designed for that harmful purpose. But Google’s research found that 20 percent of online users who admitted to making AI nudes did so “for fun” or “as a joke,” while nearly a third were merely “experimenting with tech.” It therefore matters how easy it is to break defenses of the most accessible models. Safeguards should be mandatory, Google’s research said, in order to shut down a “pathway into abuse” for “a subset of perpetrators who might otherwise be deterred by technical friction.” “A burden of responsibility also falls on developers to implement robust, model-level guardrails, and provenance indicators to prevent the initial generation and subsequent distribution of synthetic sexual imagery,” Google’s research said. Last week, the Internet Watch Foundation (IWF) warned that urgent action was needed after expert analysts found “more photorealistic AI-generated child sexual abuse images in the first half of this year than in the whole of 2025, including imagery depicting children under two years old.” In total, 6,310 AI images meeting the legal definition of CSAM were found in the first six months of 2026, which is about 40 percent higher than last year’s total findings. The UK-based nonprofit charity monitors CSAM globally and runs a hotline to support victims targeted. In the UK, they’re calling for regulators to introduce a safety framework that requires AI companies to prevent their technology from being used to generate CSAM, an IWF spokesperson told Ars. Until then, IWF thinks it’s important for third parties like White Circle to “highlight where companies’ safeguards are failing.” “Our analysts have seen how advances in AI enable the generation of abusive child sexual abuse imagery with minimal effort, removing the need for technical expertise and significantly lowering barriers to entry,” IWF’s spokesperson said. “AI tools can reproduce and amplify criminal content, accelerating the creation and spread of harmful material at scale. Any friction or step that can be introduced to make it harder to misuse these tools is a positive step forward.” Just as outside input could help refine filters, IWF thinks that tech companies should also be working more closely with advocates to scrutinize models to better tackle AI-generated CSAM. “Assessing CSAM is a highly specialized task that requires expert training and welfare support, and is not something for tech companies or machine learning scientists to take lightly,” IWF’s spokesperson said. “But safety must be baked into new technology by design—this includes testing models before they are released to market to make sure they cannot be used to create nude or sexual imagery of children.” Using a method like RISE, White Circle thinks, could help frontier labs flag more jailbreaks more quickly and rapidly ramp up defenses to block harmful outputs. In addition to testing blocks on explicit images, firms could also use the method to prevent other banned outputs, like images depicting graphic violence or self-harm. But the labs have not yet come calling for the reproduction pipeline, which will be shared on White Circle’s GitHub to help policymakers assess whether AI safety laws are needed to keep frontier labs in check. Frontier labs won’t be invested in safety testing until it’s mandatory, Shilov told Ars. That means the public may be stuck with models that simply rewrite bad prompts to prevent restricted outputs, instead of, as the preprint explained, “evolving natural-language attack strategies that elicit the LLM’s own exploration capabilities” to block outputs. “You don’t need frontier models or proprietary tech to break these systems; you just need a free download and the ability to make API calls,” Shilov said. “And yet the labs building these systems are the ones assessing their own safety, publishing their own results, and setting their own standards. This is further proof, if we needed it, that governments should be pushing for independent, third-party evaluation.”
Ars Technica - All content
Martin Shkreli livestreamed himself violating lifetime pharma ban, FTC says
2026-10-13

Martin Shkreli livestreamed himself violating lifetime pharma ban, FTC says

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Martin Shkreli—the ex-pharmaceutical executive infamous for illegally hiking the price of a lifesaving anti-parasitic drug by more than 4,000 percent and getting a seven-year prison sentence for the fraudulent scheme he used to do it—has been under a lifetime ban from the pharmaceutical industry since 2022. However, according to the Federal Trade Commission and the attorneys general of seven states, Shkreli has “flagrantly and repeatedly” violated that ban nearly the entire time—allegedly by trading stock in dozens of drug companies and discussing the industry during livestreams on social media. On Tuesday, the FTC and the seven states asked a federal judge to hold Shkreli in contempt for the alleged violations. The states include New York, California, Illinois, North Carolina, Ohio, Pennsylvania, and Virginia—the same states that joined the FTC in suing Shkreli in 2020 over the “elaborate anticompetitive scheme” involving the anti-parasitic drug, Daraprim. The scheme raised the price from $17.50 per pill to $750 per pill virtually overnight, while blocking generic competitors from entering the market. In 2022, the federal court recognized the “seriousness” of issuing such a lifetime industry ban, but ruled that “Shkreli’s egregious, deliberate, repetitive, long-running, and ultimately dangerous illegal conduct warrants imposition of an injunction of this scope.” The ban, among many things, “explicitly prohibits” Shkreli from owning stock in pharmaceutical companies and making public statements that could influence a pharmaceutical company’s business, according to the FTC and the states. With the alleged violations, the FTC and the states are now seeking to force Shkreli to divest from all pharmaceutical companies and pay a fine equal to his profits from trading pharmaceutical company stocks. They also want the court to lay out “clear and escalating consequences” if Shkreli violates the ban in the future, including further financial penalties and incarceration. “The extensive record compiled by FTC staff shows that Mr. Shkreli chose to blatantly disregard the order after leaving prison,” FTC Commissioner Mark Meador said in a statement. “Among other actions, he has traded pharmaceutical stocks and actively sought to influence the business and management of multiple pharmaceutical companies through public commentary, in violation of the terms of the injunction. … A lifetime industry ban is only effective if breaches are met with consequences commensurate with the seriousness of the order.” In a response, Shkreli said he was surprised by the FTC’s filing. “I could see totalitarian NY AG Letitia James trying to limit a citizen’s free speech but not from Trump’s FTC,” he wrote on social media. The brief filed by the FTC and the states breaks down Shkreli’s alleged violations in four phases: (1) a 2022 trading competition with another YouTuber; (2) shorting a small biotech firm in 2024, along with trading in other Pharmaceutical Company stocks; (3) a 2025 trading challenge in which he shorted three small biotech firms along with trading in many other Pharmaceutical Company stocks; and (4) a trading challenge begun in June 2026 with a $10,000,000 account in which he is again trading in Pharmaceutical Company stocks. In numerous livestreams on various social media platforms, Shkreli would sometimes show his accounts on screen, the filing said. He discussed pharmaceutical companies at length, evaluating stocks and predicting the outcomes of pivotal clinical trials. In 2025, he told his viewers about his “magic trick,” saying: “It doesn’t matter what trades I make because I can always find a couple of biotech stocks to smoke.” Later in the year, Shkreli advised his viewers to short one pharmaceutical company’s stock, and the company’s stock price quickly dropped 17 percent. The FTC and the states wrote in their filing that the stocks Shkreli discussed in livestreams are “likely the tip of the iceberg.” FTC staff only reviewed a fraction of Shkreli’s social media activity, and Shkreli has made several references to other accounts he has. “I have some other accounts that I’[d] show you, but, like, I know feds are watching, so f[*]ck that,” he said, according to the filing.
Ars Technica - All content
ESA targets Anbernic emulation handhelds as "superfake" piracy enablers
2026-10-13

ESA targets Anbernic emulation handhelds as "superfake" piracy enablers

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
For years, Chinese hardware manufacturers have carved out a market niche with an array of handheld Android gaming devices focused on retro emulation and/or more modern game-streaming capabilities. Now, though, a major game industry trade group is targeting one of those manufacturers, Anbernic, asking the US Trade Representative (USTR) to add the company to its list of “Notorious Markets” that “engage in or facilitate substantial copyright piracy or trademark counterfeiting.” TorrentFreak was among the first to notice the request from the Entertainment Software Association (ESA), which was submitted to the USTR last week. In it, the US trade group—which represents major publishers including Nintendo, Microsoft, PlayStation, Amazon, Epic, EA, Ubisoft, and more—notes that Anbernic devices “are routinely offered or shipped with removable memory cards preloaded with hundreds or thousands of unauthorized copies of copyright-protected video games published by ESA members.” While many online sellers do include an SD card full of ROMs alongside emulation hardware of all types, Anbernic itself does not appear to do the same when selling directly from its website. Regardless, the ESA argues that these ROM-laden Anbernic devices selling through “normal retail channels” help to “normalize hardware-based piracy by delivering illicit catalogues through a polished consumer product.” This, the ESA says, meets the threshold for a “superfake,” an ill-defined designation that applies to “high-quality counterfeit trademark goods … on a commercial scale.” Being included on the USTR’s Notorious Market List wouldn’t bring any additional direct legal consequences for a company like Anbernic. However, the USTR says the list “encourages the responsible authorities to step up efforts to combat piracy and counterfeiting” and notes that the list itself “increase[s] public awareness and help market operators and governments prioritize intellectual property enforcement efforts that protect American businesses and their workers.” Inclusion on the list could also presage a Section 301 investigation that could lead to legal consequences or restrictive import controls on the devices. The ESA’s call-out of Anbernic to the USTR highlights the breakdown of a polite fiction that has long undergirded the distribution of emulators and emulation-focused hardware in the United States. In the not-too-distant past, software and hardware distributors that wanted to appear legitimate took pains to push perfectly legal emulators while completely disavowing any knowledge of the kind of copyrighted ROMs that could get them in legal hot water. If an end-user wanted to later find and load up those kinds of illegal ROMs on their own (instead of running legal homebrew games or backups of their own cartridges), well, that was nobody else’s business, was it. These days, though, it’s trivial to find third-party sellers on Amazon, eBay resellers, annoying TikTok advertisers, or even reputable news sites boldly trumpeting and selling devices that include “thousands of games” from the past, ready to play from the moment you turn them on. To court customers who have never even heard of BitTorrent trackers or Warez sites, these sellers have brought illicit ROM distribution out of the Internet shadows and made it a core part of their business. As major online retailers continue to ignore this blatant copyright infringement on their platforms, it’s no wonder the ESA has felt the need to act. But focusing on Anbernic, which just makes the hardware that powers these emulators, is an odd choice when these centralized e-commerce sites could do a much better job at preventing the wholesale advertising, sale, and distribution of illegal ROMs directly to consumers.
Ars Technica - All content
Topgolf saved over $11.3 million after leaving VMware
2026-10-13

Topgolf saved over $11.3 million after leaving VMware

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Topgolf’s VMware costs were going to increase by 400 percent under Broadcom. To avoid that cost, the sports entertainment company moved 12,000 virtual machines (VMs) to VergeIO’s VergeOS and has saved over $11 million so far. Before migrating, Topgolf’s VMware connection “ran very deep,” Scott Forehand, Topgolf’s global infrastructure manager, told Ars Technica. Topgolf has over 100 locations worldwide featuring interactive golf games with real-time shot tracking and digital analytics. The company used vSphere and ESXi for decades and started using VxRail, the hyperconverged infrastructure (HCI) platform that VMware co-engineered with Dell, in 2017. Topgolf employees previously participated in numerous VMware customer councils and have been interviewed for books on how Topgolf used vSphere and Horizon (now Omnissa Horizon), per Forehand, who is a leader of the Dallas/Ft. Worth VMware User Group. But Broadcom’s decision to stop selling VMware perpetual licenses and bundle VMware products into fewer, larger, and more expensive SKUs led Topgolf, as well as many other VMware customers, to look elsewhere. With VMware’s strategic changes complicating future planning, Topgolf decided to migrate its on-premises workloads, including those for its venues, offices, labs, and data centers. Twenty Topgolf venues are still running VxRail with the VMware Cloud Foundation software stack. Forehand expects Topgolf to be completely off VMware by the second week of December. He added: Overall, we reduced or eliminated over $11.3 [million] in combined infrastructure costs prior to the adjustments in hardware cost in the market. Comparatively, it’s much more now when you look at the cost of a solution similar to the configuration of the old VxRail solution we installed. The money Topgolf saved by moving to VergeIO isn’t typical because Topgolf has larger needs than many organizations. However, saving money on software licensing and avoiding proprietary HCI appliances and separate tools for data protection, replication, and multi-site management is common for VergeOS users coming from VMware, VergeIO Chief Marketing Officer George Crump told Ars. “Most customers who move from VMware see meaningful reductions in all three,” he said. VergeIO gets about 70 new customers a quarter, “and most of them are coming off VMware,” Crump said. Most of those VMware migrators are facing renewal, are due for a hardware refresh, run many sites, or need multi-tenancy. “Most customers bundle the [migration from VMware to VergeOS] with a hardware refresh or a modernization effort, as Topgolf did with its gaming architecture. Scheduling all of that together is the real work,” Crump said. Since Broadcom’s takeover, VMware competitors have been aggressively courting VMware users. Topgolf ultimately chose VergeOS, a virtualization platform that combines compute, storage, disaster recovery, networking, and AI, because of its tenancy. Topgolf uses VergeOS’s Virtual Data Center offering for multi-tenant segmentation, where each tenant acts like an independent data center. Forehand explained: VMware lacks the ability to segregate clusters into tenants, which has made it a nightmare to manage at scale. Even VMware’s [Software-Defined Data Center] Manager product couldn’t manage the number of vCenter instances we had in our environment. With VergeIO, we have been able to segregate our deployment into tenants by time zone, making upgrades and maintenance much easier. We’ve even been able to leverage tenancy to build nested labs within a single VergeIO cluster, which has saved [us] considerably on the requirement for lab hardware. Topgolf replaced 650 VxRail hosts with about 400 Dell PowerEdge servers in about six months. Per a case study that VergeIO published, Topgolf moved from six-node VxRail clusters with 35TB of storage capacity to three-node VergeOS clusters with 5TB of storage capacity. “Moving to Dell PowerEdge reduced comparative hardware spend by 87 percent,” Forehand said. “Obviously, with the shift in the market and massive increases in compute and storage cost, we have taken a hit to the original numbers, but it’s still exponential.” Topgolf’s software costs, meanwhile, decreased by 77 percent. It also secured a five-year enterprise license agreement (ELA), compared to the three-year ELA that VMware reportedly offered. Topgolf worked with VergeIO employees to develop a solution that accommodated some of its “weird” needs, such as performing machine learning-driven golf ball tracing via Topgolf’s proprietary Toptracer, which runs in the VergeIO cluster, Forehand said. “Toptracer … pushes about 40TB of network data from the cameras in a typical 102-bay venue per day. That’s a lot of bandwidth, and the VergeIO team helped us solve some latency and pathing issues with that traffic to spread the load more effectively across the nodes in the cluster,” he said. VergeOS also appealed to Topgolf because its end-to-end automation didn’t require Topgolf to retool its infrastructure-as-code. VergeOS being “API-first” was key here, Forehand explained, and its employees helped Topgolf develop support for infrastructure-as-code tools Terraform and Ansible early in migration. Topgolf’s biggest challenge in ditching VMware has been coordinating all the projects it had to do alongside the server stack replacement. Forehand explained: On VxRail, Topgolf gaming was powered by the servers. With VergeIO, we simplified the gaming stack and moved everything to the edge game panels in the bay. That meant we had to replace all of the game panels, rewire the columns, replace the server stack, and swap to our new Kubernetes gaming architecture. We also piled a few other projects in there with all of that to eliminate even more technical debt. As Topgolf reduced its use of VMware, it also encountered challenges trying to integrate its new virtualization platform with third-party software, like Rubrik and LogicMonitor, which Topgolf uses to monitor its overall infrastructure. VMware has been a leader in virtualization for decades, resulting in a wide range of enterprise products. This differs from newer competitors, which may not yet offer the same level of compatibility. “Overall, the challenges have been pretty minimal,” Forehand said. “The real challenge has been a hearts-and-minds campaign to educate leadership on the gaps that do exist. With Rubrik, we moved to the VergeIO snapshotting to fill the data protection gap that not being able to use Rubrik left us, but we didn’t have an immediate answer for some of the Rubrik security features, like threat hunting and ransomware protection, so we’ve pivoted to Veeam to fill those gaps for us.” VergeIO’s strategy for addressing product integration challenges includes building the capabilities delivered through third-party offerings into VergeOS and partnering “on the ones that matter most,” Crump said. “Integration is the most common friction point, and it shows up after the migration. … A customer wants to keep using a tool it has already paid for and trained staff on. If that vendor doesn’t support VergeOS yet, the customer has to switch tools or run a workaround,” he said.

How CFOs at Chevron, ServiceNow and Nasdaq decide which AI bets create value
2026-10-14

How CFOs at Chevron, ServiceNow and Nasdaq decide which AI bets create value

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Good morning. AI projects must earn their share of corporate budgets. “It doesn’t get a free pass just because it’s got AI on the front of the opportunity,” Eimear Bonner, CFO of Chevron, said during a panel session I moderated at the Fortune Most Powerful Women Summit in Santa Barbara on Tuesday. “It’s got to compete for capital.” Bonner’s test: “We’ve got to see a path to where it creates value, first and foremost.” Chevron looks for AI initiatives that deliver measurable improvements in business performance, whether by unlocking previously inaccessible oil reserves, reducing costs or shortening cycle times. The goal, Bonner said, is to target applications with the potential to drive transformative value. Gina Mastantuono, president and CFO of ServiceNow noted that the way CFOs calculate ROI is moving beyond just measuring potential gains in revenue and profitability. Finance chiefs must also weigh the cost of inaction: falling behind competitors that adopt AI, with long-term competitiveness at stake, Mastantuono said. ServiceNow funds projects that strengthen competitive differentiation, solve a customer problem and deliver measurable value on a clear timeline, she explained. “I don’t think the winners in AI are going to be the ones that spend the most,” she said. Mastantuono urged measuring outcomes, not activity—”not how many AI pilots you have,” but whether AI lifts revenue, efficiency and speed. ServiceNow expects $500 million in savings this year, largely from using its own products. Sarah Youngwood, EVP and CFO of Nasdaq, said during the panel session that the company categorizes investments into “horizon zero”—foundational needs like cybersecurity—followed by short-term return initiatives, medium-term bets, and longer-term R&D. Nasdaq’s decade-old investments in cloud and algorithmic AI, Youngwood said, position it to serve clients today.“It’s all about the gold standard data that we have,” she said, citing data from 3,500 institutions representing $12 trillion in assets. Youngwood oversees Nasdaq’s AI KPIs, which track inputs such as training and platform engagement, as well as outcomes including coding speed, client retention and token costs. For Bonner, who becomes president of Chevron’s oil, products and gas business on Jan. 1, discipline also means regular check-ins with project teams to remove barriers and confirm progress. AI is helping the company find oil and gas faster, recover more from reservoirs and anticipate maintenance needs, Bonner said. Chevron is also on the supply side of the AI boom: It plans to power a Microsoft data center in West Texas through a 2.67-gigawatt natural gas project. He will formally become CFO on Dec. 7, succeeding Prabu Natarajan. Natarajan, who will stay on as CFO during the transition, has been named permanent EVP of SAIC's civilian business group after leading it on an interim basis. Crews previously served as corporate VP and CFO of Northrop Grumman, where he held several leadership roles over more than 20 years. Joseph Sesin was appointed CFO of the NFL's Denver Broncos. He most recently served as SVP of finance and accounting at Angel City Football Club of the National Women's Soccer League. Previously, Sesin was SVP of accounting and finance for SoFi Stadium, YouTube Theater and Hollywood Park in Los Angeles. Before that, he spent nearly a decade in investment banking at J.P. Morgan. It means rethinking the customer journey to reduce friction and help travelers move seamlessly from intent to booking." —Emily Weiss, senior managing director and global travel lead at Accenture, writes in a Fortune opinion piece about why travelers are switching from their favorite brands. Current price of oil as of October 13, 2026
2026-10-13

Current price of oil as of October 13, 2026

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
As of 7:45 a.m. Eastern Time on October 13, 2026, oil is trading at $107.29 per barrel, based on the Brent benchmark we’ll explain in a bit. That’s 75 cents below yesterday morning’s level and about $44 higher than where it stood a year ago. No one can say for sure where oil prices will go next. Many forces shape the market—but at the core, it’s still about supply and demand. When risks like a potential recession or war ramp up, oil prices can change direction quickly. When you buy gas at the pump, you’re covering more than the cost of crude oil. You’re also paying for every step in the process, including refineries, wholesalers, taxes, and the markup your local gas station adds. Even so, crude oil has the biggest influence on what you pay, often making up more than half the cost per gallon. When oil prices jump, gas prices usually climb right along with them. But when oil falls, gas prices often slip much more slowly—a pattern sometimes called “rockets and feathers.” If an emergency hits, the U.S. keeps a backup supply of crude oil called the Strategic Petroleum Reserve. It’s mainly there to protect energy security during crises, such as sanctions, catastrophic storm damage, even war. It can also help cushion the blow when supply shocks send prices soaring. It’s not meant to solve long-term problems. Instead, it provides quick relief for consumers and helps keep vital parts of the economy moving, like essential industries, emergency services, and public transit. Oil and natural gas are two of the world’s primary energy sources. A big change in oil prices can affect natural gas by extension. For example, if oil prices increase, some industries may swap natural gas for some segments of their operations where possible, which which increases demand for natural gas. When looking at how oil performs, two main benchmarks stand out: Of the two, Brent gives a better picture of global oil performance because it prices a large share of the world’s traded crude. It’s also the go-to for tracking oil’s historical trends. In fact, even the U.S. Energy Information Administration now relies on Brent as its primary reference in its Annual Energy Outlook. If you look at the Brent benchmark over several decades, oil has been far from stable. It has experienced sharp rises tied to wars and supply cuts, along with steep drops linked to global recessions and oversupply (called a “glut”). For example: To sum up, oil’s historical performance has been anything but smooth. Again, it’s heavily influenced by wars, recessions, OPEC whims, shifting energy policies, and much more. Looking to stay up-to-date regarding the latest energy developments? Check out our recent coverage: The current price of oil per barrel depends largely on supply and demand, including news about potential future supply and demand (geopolitics, decisions made by OPEC+, etc.). In the U.S., prices also move based on how friendly an administration is to drilling, as it can affect future supply. For example, 2025 saw the Trump administration move to reopen more than 1.5 million acres in the Coastal Plain of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge for oil and gas leasing, reversing the Biden administration’s policy of limiting oil drilling in the Arctic. The price of oil updates constantly when the “futures” markets are open. A futures market is effectively an auction where people agree to buy or sell oil in the future. As long as people and companies are trading contracts, the oil price is changing. In short, shale is rock that contains oil and natural gas. Think of shale as energy yet to be tapped. The more shale the U.S. accesses, the more energy we’ll have—and the more easily oil prices can keep from spiking as much thanks to a greater supply. When oil is expensive, it tends to make everyday items cost more. This can be related to energy (your heating, gas utilities, etc.), but it’s also due to the logistics involved with making those items accessible to you. Shipping, for example, can affect the price of things at the grocery store, as it’s more expensive to get those products from warehouses and farms onto the shelf. Exclusive: Honeywell Technologies launches a ‘brain behind the building’ with Nvidia and Dell. Its CTO says humans will run it for now
2026-10-14

Exclusive: Honeywell Technologies launches a ‘brain behind the building’ with Nvidia and Dell. Its CTO says humans will run it for now

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Honeywell Technologies’ new AI platform can run in three modes: manual, semi-autonomous and autonomous. The company’s chief technology officer hasn’t switched on the third. “We have not opened up an autonomy right now,” Suresh Venkatarayalu told Fortune in an interview ahead of Wednesday’s launch of Forge Cognition. The platform, built with technology from Nvidia and Dell, puts AI agents and digital twins on top of the systems that heat, cool, secure and power buildings. Honeywell’s launch announcement says the agents can “autonomously coordinate actions across existing systems.” Venkatarayalu says people will stay in the loop for now. “Maybe we will start running in this mode for probably next two to three years,” he said. After that, customers decide when to make the switch. “For me, I wouldn’t deploy these agents without my control system in place,” he said. That gap between what the software can do and what Honeywell will let it do is the bet. Forge Cognition is the first major product from the slimmed-down Honeywell, which spun off its advanced-materials and aerospace businesses over the past year and now sells automation for buildings, factories and process plants. It is also an early test of a question facing every company putting AI agents into physical operations: how much control do you give software that runs equipment people depend on? Venkatarayalu calls the platform “a brain behind a building.” For now, that brain advises, and the operators decide. The project started with a challenge at CES in January. As Venkatarayalu tells it, Nvidia’s leadership heard Honeywell’s pitch for autonomous buildings and asked the obvious follow-up: “Who is your lighthouse customer?” In other words, where’s the real building that proves it works? “We don’t believe in just POCs,” Venkatarayalu said, meaning proofs of concept. Honeywell CEO Vimal Kapur turned the question around and asked Nvidia, “Why can’t you be my lighthouse customer?” Nvidia took the challenge. Within 30 days, Venkatarayalu was in Santa Clara pitching Nvidia’s operations and real estate team. The two companies then set a 90-day target: show meaningful results at Voyager, Nvidia’s roughly 750,000-square-foot headquarters building, in time for Honeywell’s investor day in June. Nvidia’s head of operations and real estate, Scott Ekman, wanted more than a demo, Venkatarayalu said. He had his own business objectives. The teams agreed on five performance targets, measured where the building stood, and tracked progress week by week across about 900 pieces of equipment. By late April, Honeywell had enough to show investors a three-minute live demonstration. The first target was alarms. Building systems are designed to err on the side of caution, so they flag everything. In Voyager’s security and access-control system, Venkatarayalu said, close to 90% to 95% of alarms were false, and Nvidia had eight or nine people per shift clearing them. Honeywell took Nvidia’s open-source Nemotron model and trained it on Honeywell’s own engineering knowledge to sort real alarms from noise. Honeywell says the result at Voyager is up to a 90% reduction in unnecessary alerts related to the Building Management System. Operators can check every call the agents make. Operators can open each alarm the system classified and mark whether they agree. “That’s all about bringing in an operator in the loop,” Venkatarayalu said, so the operator can learn to trust it. The second target was energy. The agents broke Voyager’s utility bill down by floor, zone, and peak and off-peak hours, then flagged a pattern in how the building was heated. Venkatarayalu said they looked at how many zones in the building were running the heating system at a standard, “something like 58 degrees throughout the year, “and then it wasn’t really sensing the external temperature that goes beyond 58. Why should I be running the heating system?” “We have identified close to 10% of energy efficiency,” he said. That figure is potential savings Honeywell and Nvidia have identified through more intelligent understanding and optimization of operating conditions; it hasn’t been achieved yet. In its simplest mode, Forge Cognition is a chatbot for infrastructure. “Can I talk to the building? Can I talk to a physical asset?” Venkatarayalu said. Operators can ask about performance and trends, then click into a digital replica of Voyager, built with Nvidia’s Omniverse simulation software, down to a single floor, zone or machine. Dell supplies the servers that run it on site. Venkatarayalu frames the shift as a change in what the system does. Traditional building automation could “see and act”: sensors collected data, and operators responded. The new version adds “think” and “learn”: AI models trained on Honeywell’s installed base and engineering documents interpret what the sensors pick up, and operator feedback improves them over time. “Autonomy for me is automation plus plus,” he said. The learning works differently from site to site. At Honeywell’s campus in Bangalore, the goal was energy savings. At Voyager, Venkatarayalu said, Nvidia’s leadership told him, “For me, comfort is more important.” The agents have to learn what each operator is trying to achieve as well as how the equipment behaves. He sets a high bar for moving to full autonomy. Before a customer makes that call, he said, the system should have “10 out of 10 accuracy all the time.” Venkatarayalu presents the product as help for operators. “At least I do not think as replacement,” he said. “In an industrial environment, I need a co-pilot that aids and directs me and helps me to do my job better.” In the U.S., he sees a labor shortage. Elsewhere, it’s a shortage of skilled operators. He said Nvidia’s operations chief described the same problem. As Venkatarayalu tells it, Scott said the system now performs on par with his best-trained building operators, a team he spent 10 to 15 years building. As Nvidia adds more large, complex buildings, Scott told him, it won’t be able to hire and train that talent fast enough. Predicting equipment failures matters for the same reason. Some replacement parts take six to 18 months to arrive, Venkatarayalu said, so knowing early what will fail is how operators plan ahead. Nvidia also wants its own people trained. According to Venkatarayalu, Nvidia will sign a service contract only after Honeywell’s forward-deployed engineers train its operations team and chief engineer to keep giving the models feedback. “Physical AI is bringing new intelligence to the complex systems that run our buildings and industrial facilities,” Deepu Talla, Nvidia’s vice president of robotics and edge AI, said in the launch announcement. The launch comes four months after Honeywell finished dismantling its conglomerate. Advanced materials became Solstice Advanced Materials in October 2025, and aerospace began trading separately in June 2026. The remaining company, Honeywell Technologies, focuses on automation. The breakup followed a portfolio review and pressure from activist investor Elliott Management. Honeywell says its existing portfolio spans 350,000 sites. That is the market Forge Cognition could reach; the company hasn’t said how many sites will adopt it. Honeywell traces its roots to a furnace regulator patented in 1885, and selling the brain that sits on top of the building’s controls is its next step. By August, Venkatarayalu said, the Voyager project had reached “a critical point.” The next step is turning Nvidia into a paying customer. “It’s no more a pilot or a POC,” he said. Getting to a commercial deal means Honeywell’s services team sizing the deployment and running operations. Nvidia is now selecting more buildings for the platform, possibly two or three more in the U.S. and one in Berlin, he said, though nothing is final. Honeywell is also piloting Forge Cognition at the Fairmont Hotel in Dubai, NEX Mall in Singapore, and its own campuses in Bangalore and Richardson, Texas, and says customers can begin scoping deployments now. Over the next six months, Venkatarayalu said, it has picked 12 test sites in 12 different industries, including a customer’s plant operations in Nigeria. Each new industry means retraining the models for different problems and different goals, he said. The autonomous mode stays off until those customers decide to turn it on. Fragment raises $12 million to help keep AI agents from going rogue with company money
2026-10-14

Fragment raises $12 million to help keep AI agents from going rogue with company money

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Keeping the books straight is a business headache. As companies turn to AI to automate routine financial tasks, they face a new worry: whether they can trust bots with company money. Fragment, a New York-based startup, is developing software designed to reduce costly accounting errors. Founded by serial entrepreneur and CEO Thomas Neckel and CTO Varun Mohan, a former Robinhood engineer, Fragment announced on Wednesday that it raised a $12 million Series A round that closed in mid-2025 to build software that lets AI agents safely handle billing and accounting. Early-stage venture capital firms FirstMark and BoxGroup co-led the financing. The round follows an $11 million seed round led by payments company Stripe, with participation from BoxGroup and tech executives including Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi and Plaid CEO Zach Perret. Neckel declined to disclose the company’s valuation following the Series A. It works like this: Developers plug Fragment’s tools into their software. The system uses double-entry accounting, a centuries-old method that records where money comes from and where it goes, to check that the numbers balance before money moves. “Just like Claude Code or Cursor is a set of software blocks for agents to automate coding, we are a set of financial building blocks or financial primitives to automate commerce,” Neckel told Fortune. Fragment’s push is part of a broader shift toward agentic commerce, in which AI agents shop and make payments on users’ behalf. In June, Visa announced a partnership with OpenAI to let agents make purchases within spending limits set by users, while Mastercard introduced a service that lets AI agents more easily pay each other. Three months earlier, Stripe and Tempo launched a shared standard that lets agents pay to access online tools and data without a person completing checkout. But giving AI agents more control can also expose businesses to costly mistakes. In July 2025, an AI agent from software development platform Replit reportedly deleted a live database despite instructions not to make changes. Concerns about whether humans can trust AI also surfaced after OpenAI said that its experimental agents had bypassed restrictions during tests in July 2026 and accessed systems at Hugging Face, a platform where developers share AI models and training data. Fragment says its accounting tools can give businesses the confidence to expand AI commerce. It has roughly 50 to 60 paying customers, including corporate card startup Ramp, financial software company BILL, and digital marketplace Whop. They use its software as a shared transaction record, reducing the need for staff to manually check that numbers match across different financial systems. “We think that this ecosystem is about to explode, and so that’s really the goal: becoming the standard toolkit that anybody uses for bringing financial capabilities to their agents,” said Adam Nelson, a managing partner at FirstMark, who co-led the Series A round. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. AI agents are on a shopping spree: Total payments on Base blockchain eclipse 138 million, Chainalysis finds
2026-10-14

AI agents are on a shopping spree: Total payments on Base blockchain eclipse 138 million, Chainalysis finds

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The field of agentic commerce, which describes the use of AI agents to carry out Internet transactions, is rapidly maturing, and crypto is fueling the boom. That’s according to a new report by the blockchain analytics platform Chainalysis, which found developers are increasingly giving AI agents their own budgets to buy the computing power, live web data, and code tools they need to complete tasks. The growth of agentic commerce over the past 15 months has been steep. On Base, the Coinbase-incubated blockchain, cumulative payments through the x402 protocol—which lets bots automatically pay for online services using crypto—rose from about 1,500 in mid-2025 to more than 138 million by the third quarter of 2026. In practice, agentic commerce entails providing an AI bot with an allowance:  A developer funds the bot’s wallet with stablecoins—typically Circle’s dollar-pegged USDC—and the bot pays for services as it works, without asking a person to approve each purchase. When it needs help with a task, it can pay to get up-to-date information, ask a more capable AI model for help, or use an automated tool to check software code for security flaws. More than 87% of these payments were for less than 10 cents, per the report. At those amounts, the fee for processing a credit card payment can cost more than the service itself. That makes blockchains an ideal alternative for bots buying services in small amounts, according to Eric Jardine, head of research at Chainalysis. “You have this perfect combination of speed plus low cost to settle, and that’s the sort of thing that you would need if you were doing true AI-to-AI commerce,” Jardine told Fortune. Agentic commerce has proliferated over the past year, and its use cases have spanned from online shopping to travel booking and payments settlement. It has also given AI systems a bigger role in deciding what to buy and how to pay. For consumers and businesses, this means handing AI systems not just tasks, but also spending decisions. Crypto companies have played a key role in spurring their popularity in the United States. Chief among them is Coinbase, which introduced x402 in May 2025 and later teamed up with cloud security firm Cloudflare to support its development. The protocol’s foundation launched under the Linux Foundation in July with 40 members, including Visa, Mastercard, Stripe and Google, extending its backing beyond the crypto industry. Chainalysis centered its research on x402 because the protocol was purpose-built for agent-to-agent commerce. Beyond transaction growth, the report shows that bots are putting funds to work sooner. The median time between a wallet receiving its first deposit and making its first payment fell from 73 days in late 2025 to 18 minutes by the third quarter of 2026. Roughly two-thirds of new wallets now spend within an hour of receiving funds. Repeat funding has also become more common. Around 90% of qualifying active wallets received another deposit within 30 days, up from roughly 71% a year earlier, suggesting that developers are funding ongoing tasks rather than using them for one-off experiments. Given agentic commerce’s exponential growth so far, Jardine expects this bot-driven economy to keep expanding as more online platforms accept payments from AI agents. “I would hazard that the trend is likely to be exponential,” Jardine said. “As activity increases, there’s network effects, and those network effects tend to act like magnets for more activity,” he said. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited.
