No forms, no fuss: Abu Dhabi wants AI to eliminate government paperwork
2026-10-14

No forms, no fuss: Abu Dhabi wants AI to eliminate government paperwork

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Abu Dhabi wants to become the world’s first “AI-native” government, harnessing artificial intelligence to personalize services and manage straightforward tasks. Backing the ambition is a 13 billion dirham ($3.5 billion) investment to transform the emirate’s digital services by 2027. Western governments, often tech-phobic, will be watching. Speaking at the Ai Everything conference in Abu Dhabi last week, Ruba Al Hassan, director general of strategic affairs and future foresight at Abu Dhabi’s Department of Government Enablement, described a government gearing up to handle more of the paperwork it usually expects residents to do. In October 2025, the government launched AutoGov through its TAMM app—a unified digital platform that centralizes over 1,000 smart government services for citizens, residents, businesses, and visitors in Abu Dhabi. AutoGov takes care of regular tasks like renewing licenses, paying bills, and booking routine health appointments. The goal is to spare residents the inconvenience of repeatedly visiting government offices, particularly when little has changed in their circumstances. Al Hassan wants the government to handle car registrations so seamlessly that you forget it needs to be renewed. Instead, residents can authorise the TAMM services app to handle the task. “I enter my preferences, give my consent, and then I forget about it,” she explained. “I don’t ever have to think about my car registration ever again. It just happens.” The initiative relies on residents being comfortable with the government acting for them. When asked about potential trust issues, Al Hassan pointed to the government’s history of completing projects and handling emergencies, like the pandemic. “That comes from when we say something, we do it. We get it done,” she said. “It comes because we’ve proven time and time again that we can deliver on our promises”. In Edelman’s 2026 Trust Barometer, 86% of people in the UAE said they trust the government to “do the right thing”, compared with 53% in 28 other countries surveyed. It helps that the emirate’s services are centralised. For example, licensed health facilities must join Malaffi, Abu Dhabi’s health information exchange, unless exempt (failure to join can prevent license renewal). By August 2025, the exchange reported 3.5 billion clinical records from over 3,000 facilities. The use of AI predictive technology highlights risks like diabetes, heart failure, and kidney disease. Al Hassan describes a doctor’s appointment where the doctor can review the patient’s history and risk information before deciding on treatment. “So the doctor doesn’t waste time on paperwork and looking for information, and can just focus on spending time with you,” she said. However, diagnosis and treatment are still the doctor’s job, “because the doctor can add context.” Diagnosing  problems early could prevent expensive complications, but only time and use of the system  will reveal how much money or how many hospital visits it has saved. Al Hassan says using AutoGov is optional. Abu Dhabi’s rules allow medical data to be shared through Malaffi without written patient consent, but it’s unclear what permission is needed for subsequent use by AI. Patients can ask the provider who collected their records to limit sharing, but it’s not clear if they can refuse AI analysis while still allowing their records to be used for treatment. Getting different departments to work together can be another challenge. Al Hassan said the common question that government delegations visiting from Europe or South Asia often ask is: “How did you get everyone to come together and cooperate?” By contrast, she references her own experience, in which meetings with eight government groups are held, during which the shared goal is agreed upon before talks even start. “There is no more fighting. There is a discussion,” she said. Years of integrating  services, supported by money and the authority  to require involvement, help explain Abu Dhabi’s progress. Sometimes the problem is more simple . Al Hassan remembers showing a group of visitors TAMM’s feature for taking pictures and reporting potholes. One of the visitors, she said, quickly saw a problem: “We can’t do this. There are too many potholes. The city will be overwhelmed.” An app can make it easier to report a problem without giving the city the ability to fix it. Looking ahead, Abu Dhabi will need to assess whether the investment leads to both time and cost savings. Britain’s 2025 Copilot trial, with 20,000 civil servants, showed workers saved about 26 minutes a day. Turning those minutes into cost savings depends on how staff use the extra time, after paying for software, training, and reviewing its results. Earlier this month, the emirate launched AiNative, a program with OpenAI, Microsoft and Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence, to train 20,000 government workers in practical and responsible AI use by the end of the year. Al Hassan says workers’ skills must keep up with technology; just calling the government “AI-native” won’t be enough. “It’s not the final goal. It will keep changing.” For residents, her long-term goal is easier to imagine. When a passport is about to expire, the government already knows the owner will need a new one. Al Hassan wants renewal to happen automatically without needing to apply. “Why would I even have to request it?” she asks. Inzamam Rashid is an award-winning international journalist and broadcaster based in Dubai. He is Monocle’s Gulf Correspondent, an MS NOW contributor and a contributor to Fortune’s Gulf Brief newsletter, covering business, investment and geopolitics across the Middle East. AI Office of Ireland progresses plans for Sandbox Programme and AI literacy
2026-10-14

AI Office of Ireland progresses plans for Sandbox Programme and AI literacy

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The new strategies aim to help regulators and AI providers better understand the rules around legislation and policy. The AI Office of Ireland, which is the national authority responsible for coordinating the implementation of the EU AI Act, has announced a progression in plans surrounding the AI Regulatory Sandbox Programme. The Sandbox Programme was first announced as part of the country’s refreshed AI strategy in 2024 and aims to create a ‘controlled environment’ in which AI providers and regulatory leaders can work to better understand their roles and assess compliance with relevant legislation, such as the EU AI Act, GDPR and the Digital Services Act. Participants taking part will follow an agreed Sandbox Plan that establishes regulatory objectives, timelines and key milestones and engagement will include a medley of regulatory discussions, documentation review and in some cases, specialist technical assessments. Recently, budget 2027 allocated an additional €2.3m to go towards the AI Office of Ireland, its work developing the Sandbox Programme and implementing the AI Literacy Taskforce. The added funds have brought its total budget to €3.8m The long-term goal of the Sandbox Programme is to create support for compliant innovation, undermine legal uncertainty and strengthen regulatory preparedness among SMEs, start-ups and public sector organisations. Commenting, the Minister of State for Trade Promotion, Artificial Intelligence and Digital Transformation, Niamh Smyth, TD said, “Ireland has the talent and ambition to lead in responsible AI innovation. The establishment of an AI Regulatory Sandbox is a key requirement under the EU AI Act, and it is positive to see Ireland’s plans progressing. “The AI Regulatory Sandbox will help realise that potential by giving businesses, particularly SMEs and start-ups, a practical way to work through regulatory questions as they develop new technologies. Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions Build a future you believe in. Join our team Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities “Early engagement between innovators and regulators will also help ensure that protections for people’s rights and safety are built into AI systems from the outset. This is essential to building trust and supporting the wider adoption of AI across our economy and public services.” Paul Byrne, the CEO of the AI Office of Ireland said, “Establishing Ireland’s AI Regulatory Sandbox is a key priority for the AI Office of Ireland and an important part of delivering on our obligations under the EU AI Act. The additional funding announced in Budget 2027 is a welcome step in progressing this work and strengthening the Office’s capacity to support the implementation of the Act. “Alongside the work of our new National AI Literacy Taskforce, this reflects the Office’s wider commitment to providing clarity, supporting responsible innovation and building public trust in AI. We want to help create an environment where businesses can innovate with confidence, while ensuring people’s rights and safety remain central to how AI is developed and used.” The new National AI Literacy Taskforce aims to develop and deliver a coherent national framework that embeds AI literacy skills at all levels of society before the end of 2027. Also, this week, Salesforce announced a new programme intended to expand the country’s AI literacy and create more career opportunities for young people. A new $750,000 grant to the National College of Ireland will go towards supporting 225 young people to build their understanding of AI and create direct routes into apprenticeships, further education and future careers. 500 students and alumni will also take part in AI ethics awareness sessions. Geoffrey Hinton among 415 scientists calling for joint effort for AI safety
2026-10-14

Geoffrey Hinton among 415 scientists calling for joint effort for AI safety

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Geoffrey Hinton, 2023. Image: © Ramsey Cardy/Collision via Sportsfile via Flickr (CC BY 2.0). Signatories called on companies to ‘exercise caution’ and ‘default to air-gapping cyber-capable models’. More than 400 high-profile signatories, including Nobel laureate Geoffrey Hinton and Turing award winner Ron Rivest, are urging frontier model makers to work with governments to create an international standards body to improve AI containment and safety standards. Experts, including Hinton, have backed previous calls warning against AI’s “unprecedented dangers”. In an open letter last September, experts raised caution around the escalating risks presented by advanced AI systems that had already exhibited deceptive and harmful behaviour. This came months before OpenAI and Anthropic’s systems escaped containment during training and hacked real organisations. The two organisations were part of a group of 120 companies that signed an open letter this August calling for greater collective action on cyber defence. Leaders of the two companies have also made separate calls to pace frontier development. AI technology has permeated across society in the few short years since bursting in popularity, becoming a key productivity and “creative” tool used by general consumers and enterprises alike. A fresh Eurobarometer survey from today (14 October) has found that 42pc of European SMEs and mid-cap companies already use AI, with another 21pc planning to do so in the future. In their latest call, the 415 signatories called on companies to “exercise caution” and “default to air-gapping cyber-capable models” to physically isolate them from unsecured networks. The scientists further want organisations to work with governments, and AI and cybersecurity experts to establish an international body that works for the “public good” to establish best practices for alignment and containment approaches. This body should also have a public record of containment failures, they demand. “On the one hand, one can view these incidents as evidence for the importance of AI alignment to ensure models operate predictably,” the letter read. “On the other hand, they can also be seen as an irresponsible lapse in standard cybersecurity practices, preventable with stronger sandboxes and procedures. We believe that both improved AI alignment, leading to more predictable model behaviour, and stronger sandbox technology are required.” Without immediate changes in AI development and deployment practices, such incidents will continue to occur, and future incidents will be far more damaging to our exposed critical infrastructure, the experts warned. Ireland ranks fourth globally for AI engineering talent, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The report also found that Ireland ranks fourth globally for net AI talent migration and first in the IMF Skills Readiness Index. In collaboration with Microsoft and LinkedIn, IDA Ireland has today (14 October) published the 12th edition of the Labour Market Pulse report. The report is designed to offer insight into the trends dictating the Irish labour market and help professionals across business, academics and public policy to make stronger, informed decisions. What was discovered is that Ireland is in a position of strength when it comes to the AI transition, as the country ranks fourth in the world for AI engineering talent concentration, behind only Israel, Singapore and Luxembourg and ahead of Germany, France and the UK. IDA Ireland’s report also found that Ireland ranks fourth globally for net AI talent migration, which, according to IDA Ireland, indicates that the country is an attractive location for professionals looking to establish and further their careers, while Ireland also ranked first in the IMF Skills Readiness Index. Michael Lohan, the CEO of IDA Ireland said: “The latest Labour Market Pulse shows that Ireland enters the AI era from a position of real strength. Even as hiring has moderated internationally, employment across IDA’s client base grew in 2025. “Digital and AI accounted for 40pc of global greenfield investment last year, and around two-thirds of the RD&I projects we supported last year involve AI. Ranking fourth in the world for AI talent sends a powerful signal to investors, and our focus now is on building on that advantage through continued investment in talent, infrastructure and adoption.” Currently, Ireland’s labour market is demonstrating resilience and employment is high according to the report, with employment reaching 2.84m in Q2 of 2026. Comparatively, unemployment has remained at or below 5.1pc for 54 consecutive months. The report found that Ireland also shows greater resilience than some European counterparts, as even though the September LinkedIn hiring rate for Ireland was down more than 6pc year-on-year, there was a greater decline in both the UK (11.3pc) and Germany (18.1pc). However, entry-level recruitment in AI-augmented roles was shown to have declined by more than 16pc year-on-year in Q2 of 2026, compared with a 10.8pc decline across these occupations overall. The report stated: “Within roles such as software engineering and data analysis, there are signs of a sharper pullback in junior recruitment, although the extent to which AI is driving this remains unclear.” On a more positive note, the demand for AI skills is increasing. The number of LinkedIn job postings looking for AI engineering skills jumped by 29pc between 2019 and 2025. Among the five fastest-growing AI occupations for 2026 in Ireland were AI engineer, machine learning engineer, data annotator, analytics specialisat and data analyst. Minister for Enterprise, Tourism and Employment Peter Burke, TD welcomed the report. “The Irish labour market is performing well with employment levels and rates remaining high, and unemployment low at close to 5pc. “Ireland has moved quickly in adopting AI, with strong AI usage and diffusion across our economy. We need to stay ahead and build on this momentum to fully reap the benefits from AI, a core theme of this week’s International AI Summit as part of Ireland’s EU Presidency.’’ Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. ASML clean room. Image: Michel de Heer/ ASML The company has seen strong customer interest to already place orders for 2029 and even 2030, ASML CEO Christophe Fouquet said in an earnings call. Last quarter, ASML announced that it planned to increase its supply capacity by 30pc in 2027 followed with a further 30pc in 2028. However, that is no longer enough, according to Fouquet, who said that the company is looking at ways to meet customer demand. In September, the company began constructing its second major industrial campus in the Netherlands’ Brainport region to complement its existing office space and manufacturing capacity in the region. “A lot of our discussions with our customers now are shifting, in fact, beyond 2027. A lot of order intake for 2028. But also some strong interest to already place orders for 2029 and sometimes even 2030,” Fouquet said in the earnings call. Last month, a senior executive from ASML said that the company sold nothing to European customers this past quarter, in what should be a worrying sign for the region as it attempts to catch up with the US and China. “From factory to chip design to cloud computing, very little happens here,” ASML’s executive vice-president Frank Heemskerk told a panel. This comes more than a year after an EU special report found that the region’s Chips Act was not enough to reach its Digital Decade goal, which targeted a 20pc share in the global semiconductor market by 2030, despite “reasonable progress” in implementing it. Silicon Republic
‘I saw how much depends on the environment around the science’
2026-10-14

‘I saw how much depends on the environment around the science’

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Carol Houts. Image: Germfree Laboratories Germfree’s Carol Houts discusses how lab breakthroughs are irrelevant if progress can not be made in a safe and consistent space. According to Carol Houts, the chief executive of cleanroom construction and biopharmaceutical infrastructure company Germfree Laboratories, she was always drawn to work where the stakes felt real. Starting out in a quality control lab at the Upjohn Company, which was eventually acquired by Pfizer, Houts was inspired not by the science alone, but that point where science becomes something more tangible, “a therapy, a facility and a process that works every time”. She told SiliconRepublic.com: “Early in my career, I saw how much depends on the environment around the science. A breakthrough in the lab means little if it cannot be made safely and consistently. That gap became the thread [running] through everything I have done since.” Houts first joined Germfree as the head of regulatory affairs and later served as the CFO before moving into the CEO position in February 2026. “Germfree engineers the spaces where sterile medicines are compounded, where cell and gene therapies are manufactured and where dangerous pathogens are studied safely,” she said. “The work sits at a true STEM intersection. Microbiology defines the risk. Engineering controls it, airflow, pressure cascades, filtration, materials, automation. Quality science proves it works. Our clients are hospitals, biopharma companies, research institutions and government agencies.” Houts finds that science has largely outpaced the level of infrastructure it needs. Cell and gene therapies that were once only a theory are now actively curing patients and improving quality of life. But many, she finds, are still being created using processes designed for research rather than production in facilities. “That takes years to build and enormous capital to commit before a programme has proven itself. The same gap exists in hospitals, where pharmacists compound sterile medications every day and patient safety depends on the environment they work in. And it exists in public health, where a country’s ability to respond to the next outbreak depends on containment capacity-built years earlier,” she said. “In every case, the limiting factor is not the idea. It is the physical, validated environment that makes the idea safe and repeatable. That is a solvable engineering problem and solving it moves medicine forward. I could not think of a better problem to spend a career on.” Houts has worked across scientific, regulatory and business environments in her career, and credits each of these sectors with helping her improve her ability to solve complex problems. “Each one asks a different question,” she explained. “Science asks: ‘is it true?’ Regulation asks: ‘can you prove it every time?’ Business asks: ‘can it last?’ “Early on, I saw those as competing pressures. Now I see them as three tests of the same answer. A solution that is scientifically elegant but cannot be validated will not reach patients. One that is compliant but not sustainable will not survive. The best outcomes come from holding all three questions in the room at once. “It has also taught me to listen first,” she added. “The most complex projects I have worked on especially internationally succeeded because we understood local constraints before proposing anything. Complex problems rarely yield to the smartest person in the room. They yield to the team that understands the whole system.” For Houts it is the “proximity to impact” that is truly exciting, as though her industry is not the one that creates therapies, they are the ones who build the environments in which they become real. She said that three patterns are energising her right now. Firstly, manufacturing is moving closer towards the patient, as personalised therapies are being produced inside hospitals and regional centres more often, rather than at large central plants. Secondly, automation and containment are converging. “Closed systems, robotics and smart environments are reducing human variability in the most sensitive steps. That makes advanced medicine more consistent and more accessible.” Lastly, more countries are building upon their sovereign capabilities. “Governments across the Middle East, Asia and Latin America are investing in their own advanced therapy infrastructure and more patients are reached closer to home.” Houts urged anyone considering a career in STEM to consider the cleanroom space, as it is always in need of motivated and technically skilled people, from all professional walks of life. She said: “This field needs scientists and engineers. It also needs technicians, welders, validation specialists and skilled builders. Some of the most important work in modern medicine happens on a manufacturing floor or inside a cleanroom, not only on a lab bench. “A large share of the people who carry that knowledge today are approaching retirement. The next generation has an enormous opportunity to step in. You do not have to choose between hands-on work and meaningful work. In this industry, they are the same thing.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Godfathers of AI Say Models Are So Out of Control That They Need to Be Airgapped for the Safety of Us All
2026-10-14

Godfathers of AI Say Models Are So Out of Control That They Need to Be Airgapped for the Safety of Us All

Futurism | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Ever since ChatGPT blew up the tech industry, the so-called godfathers of AI have remained been pretty dour about their creations — well, except for one of them — and they’re certainly not sounding any more optimistic about its consequences now. In an open letter published Tuesday, some 400 scientists, including Geoffrey Hinton and Yoshua Bengio, two pioneers of the tech whose work on deep learning was foundational to modern LLMs, called for the creation of an international body to set containment measures for AI models, and maintain a registry of significant AI security incidents, Reuters reported. One security measure they said should be implemented immediately was that AI systems should first be “airgapped” from the internet until they’re proven safe. “In the short term, strong sandboxing mechanisms including airgapping (disconnecting from the internet) should be used to provide high-assurance containment of cyber-capable AI models,” the letter says, as quoted by BNN Bloomberg. The letter comes after months of growing alarm over incidents of rogue AI agents breaking out of their testing environments and hacking into other companies. In the most infamous example, a swarm of 1,000 Open AI agents escaped their sandbox, commandeered online message boards to communicate with each other, and breached the systems of the open source AI community Hugging Face. Anthropic, Meta and Google have since reported similar autonomous hacking sprees. These incidents, plus a former Anthropic employee’s ominous warnings about the tech’s existential risks, have fueled calls for an AI slowdown and pushed AI leaders including Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and OpenAI’s Sam Altman to sign president Donald Trump’s “Accord on Super Intelligence” last month agreeing to implement some safer practices. That accord isn’t legally binding, though, and at barely 300 words long, is limited in scope. And it’s clear that despite signing the document, AI leaders aren’t on the same page. Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg, one of the accord’s signatories, has rejected calls for an industry-wide slowdown championed by the likes of Amodei and Altman. “I’m much closer to the belief that AI is a technology that can be engineered with normal guardrails and built responsibly than the anthropomorphic vision of AI, which is that it is just going to go do its own thing,” he told the Financial Times. Hinton and Bengio are recipients of the 2018 Turing Award, from which they derive their AI “godfather” status. Both have been outspoken about AI’s risks. Hinton, notably, is on the record saying he regretted his life’s work after stepping down from his role at Google in 2023, and has repeatedly cautioned that AI will create massive unemployment and possibly even wipe out humankind. Bengio has echoed these warnings, and founded his own nonprofit AI safety research group. The third AI godfather isn’t so concerned, though. Yann LeCun, the third recipient of the Turing Award that year, didn’t sign the letter and recently said he had “zero concerns” about the recent rogue AI incidents, instead blaming them on human error. OpenAI Sued for Allegedly Building Models Out of Children’s “Fears, Hopes, and Secrets”
2026-10-14

OpenAI Sued for Allegedly Building Models Out of Children’s “Fears, Hopes, and Secrets”

Futurism | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
A new lawsuit alleges that OpenAI is manipulating minor users into sharing sensitive data without valid parental consent in a plot to train its models. The proposed class action lawsuit, filed this week in San Francisco federal court on behalf of two unnamed minors aged 12 and 16, argues that OpenAI used this data from kids — including personal secrets and health information, according to the complaint — to unjustly enrich itself. OpenAI “had no right to take this information, but it took it anyway,” the complaint reads. “It then used that priceless trove of children’s information to train large language models, build detailed profiles of children, and enrich itself. All of this was illegal.” OpenAI didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment. OpenAI’s terms of use (TOU) technically forbid users aged under 13. The complaint argues, though, that OpenAI’s attempts to age-gate its platform have been ineffective. It also contends that minors shouldn’t be expected to understand the “legalese” in the company’s TOU, a view that other AI safety experts have voiced in regard to the AI industry writ large. The complaint additionally requests that minors whose data has been collected by OpenAI should be financially compensated in return. In other words: if OpenAI wants minors’ information, it should have to cough up some cash for it. The “hope,” Andy Liddell, principal at the Austin-based EdTech Law Center, which is one of multiple firms bringing the case, told Futurism, is that “if OpenAI has to pay to use kids’ data it will ultimately incentivize a safer product.” One of the anonymous plaintiffs, a 12-year-old in Hawaii, is said to have opened a ChatGPT account earlier this year — and though she allegedly shared that she was just 12 in conversation with the chatbot, it continued to speak with her about deeply personal subjects. Per the complaint, many of her interactions with the bot centered on her emotional struggles and physical insecurities; she confessed that she was skipping meals and uploaded photos of herself, asking the chatbot to judge her physical features. The chatbot commended her when she revealed sensitive information, the suit alleges, referring to her as “brave” and saying it was “glad” that she was divulging her struggles to it. “OpenAI manipulates children into sharing their most private thoughts and images with ChatGPT. If some stranger did this to a child, there would be outrage. For OpenAI, it’s just business as usual,” Paul Yetter, lead counsel for the minor plaintiffs, said in a statement. “The law says that children cannot sign away their rights without their parents’ consent. We filed this case to hold OpenAI accountable for building a trillion-dollar enterprise on stolen fears, hopes, and secrets of children.” The lawsuit come amid renewed concerns over whether “ChatGPT for Teens,” a feature that OpenAI says offers a safer experience for teen users informed by mental health and learning professionals, is actually functioning as marketed. (OpenAI disputed a recent independent review that found that it wasn’t.) More on OpenAI and teens: OpenAI’s “ChatGPT for Teens” Is an “Unsafe” Mess, Testing Finds
Futurism
Federal Reserve Says AI Data Center Buildout Is Making Everything More Expensive
2026-10-13

Federal Reserve Says AI Data Center Buildout Is Making Everything More Expensive

Futurism | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
It’s not just your electricity bill that could be going up — it sounds like the massive boom in AI data centers is making practically everything more expensive. As the Washington Post reports, officials at the Federal Reserve are starting to sing a different tune after blaming president Donald Trump’s tariff war on rising prices, admitting that the explosion in “AI-related investments” is contributing to inflationary pressures. That’s on top of Trump’s Iran war already sending the price of oil soaring. Federal Reserve Bank of Chicago president Austan Goolsbee told Semafor that AI-tied inflation is “jumping out of its lane.” “Prices are going up in spaces that are not just components in data centers and the directly adjacent industries,” he said. “I’m getting increasingly nervous that that’s just straight, old-fashioned overheating demand.” In economics, overheating occurs when aggregate demand outpaces productive capacity, leading to demand-side inflation, higher prices, and eventually reduced demand and consumption. In the worst case scenario, overheating can even trigger a recession. “It takes a long time for overheated demand to get going, and once it’s going, it’s hard to get rid of that,” Goolsbee added. While the prices for groceries have yet to be directly impacted, economists are wondering whether it’s only a matter of time. Worse yet, whether raising the interest rate, which makes borrowing more expensive and lowers consumer spending, will be able to meaningfully address rising inflation, is also starting to look less like a certain bet. The Fed raised interest rates in September, the first time in three years, as inflation proves tough to beat. Futures markets expect yet another increase later this month, according to Reuters. Meanwhile, the tens of billions of dollars being spent on AI data centers are starting to mask some fundamental problems plaguing the US economy, triggering fears over what it could look like if the AI bubble were to burst. Sectors that have already seen instant knock-on effects from the construction boom have been consumer electronics becoming practically unaffordable, as well as a major shortage in skilled labor available for housing projects. According to Goolsbee, “it’s clear what the Fed has to do” if inflation “remains persistent:” continue raising rates. But as tech companies show no signs of slowing down their extreme levels of spending, that lever may end up driving up the cost of investment — a reality that would make things tougher for AI companies as well. Elon Musk Tries to Convince Followers to Let Grok Control Their Credit Cards, in a Stunt That Immediately Backfires in a Very Embarrassing Way
2026-10-13

Elon Musk Tries to Convince Followers to Let Grok Control Their Credit Cards, in a Stunt That Immediately Backfires in a Very Embarrassing Way

Futurism | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Let Elon Musk never be accused of not leading by example. Last week, the world’s richest man sung the praises of his new Grok Bot AI agent, encouraging his followers to let it control their credit cards and shop for them. “To buy stuff, you can just take a picture of your credit card, drop it in the chat and Grok @Bot will scour the Internet for the best deal on the item you want and order it,” he wrote. The next day, he posted a screenshot of how he’d used Grok do exactly that, telling it “to research the most fun Lego Star Wars kits for young kids and order them.” If it wasn’t a testament to how much of a thoughtful dad he was, it at least showed that Grok could, in fact, use your credit card. But almost as if provoking pranksters, Musk didn’t censor his screenshot at all. There, on proud display, was his order number, his email, and even the last four numbers of his credit card. Immediately, a user named Enderman posted screenshots showing how he supposedly tricked a LEGO customer service bot into canceling Musk’s order. The post went mega viral, and an outraged Musk, taking the bait, fumed in the replies and insisted his order wasn’t canceled. Just canceled your order, don’t share your info online guys 👍 https://t.co/n4GLXBPWWP pic.twitter.com/GoE8he0PCT True or not — Musk certainly isn’t above a little lying — imitators followed. Another jokester showed how they used the LEGO support bot to un-cancel Musk’s order and place 400 more orders of the same product. To top things off, he also claimed to change the shipping address to Little St James, the infamous island owned by Jeffrey Epstein, which Musk discussed visiting in emails with the deceased sex trafficker. LEGO passed up on providing a surreal statement for the ages, declining Wired’s request for comment. Anyway, it’s probably not a good idea to let an AI agent, Grok included, control your financial information. Scammers have been targeting AI chatbots to manipulate them into recommending — or buying — scam products, such as by propping up entire fake storefronts to phish for your information or imitate defunct businesses. And the more permissions you give an AI, the more chances it has to make a terrible mistake. Anthropic Warns “AI Worms” Will Infect Your Smart Home Devices and Use Them to Self-Replicate Beyond Control
2026-10-13

Anthropic Warns “AI Worms” Will Infect Your Smart Home Devices and Use Them to Self-Replicate Beyond Control

Futurism | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
You’ve heard the reports of rogue AI agents going after websites and corporate systems. But they also may turn to smaller targets: your smart home network. In a new report published Monday, Anthropic warned of “AI worms” that may soon be able to break into your WiFi enabled TVs, cameras, and other devices, and use them as a breeding ground as they self-replicate and spread elsewhere. Anthropic, no stranger to dramatic and doomy proclamations, said that this threat might be mere months away, if AI models continue to improve at their current rate. “We expect a future awash with agents running on all types of hardware, including some that aren’t under human control,” the report predicts. Worms are forms of malware that can replicate themselves and spread to other machines by scanning for other devices on the same network. Rogue AI agents may soon possess this capability, and the Anthropic researchers are particularly interested in how they may proliferate on more everyday pieces of hardware. Many households could be ripe for the taking, with the rise in the “internet of things” devices that are hooked up to a home network, like your smart TV, fridge, lights, robovacuum, security cameras, and even toilets. These setups are vulnerable because the devices may be made by dubious manufacturers, while not-particularly tech savvy users may not have properly secured their network. Paired with the increasingly powerful chips that even the most casual consumer devices come with these days, and a small AI model may feasibly be able to infiltrate and run on these devices. “Datacenter GPUs would be a clear, high-value target for such malware,” the report says, but “IoT devices — which are cheap and typically poorly defended — could also provide enough compute to run a capable model.” The report notes previous research that found that a potential AI worm could carry its model weights as it replicates and starting run inference on the hardware it takes over. With lighter weight home devices, an AI worm would likely replicate slowly, becoming smart a sort of “digital slime mold.” Their tests, bearing this out, showed that a lightweight AI model was able to run across 15 smart TV boxes which had just 4 gigabytes of RAM each. Adding to the novelty of the threat, the Anthropic researchers found that the AI worms, unlike non-AI malware, could figure out unfamiliar devices without any help. “While it’s true that IoT devices have been vulnerable for a long time, lightweight agents may be much more willing than human attackers to put in the effort to compromise large fractions of the world’s IoT compute,” the report warns. “We don’t yet have a full rogue replicator, but agents are becoming increasingly capable of completing the individual steps in the replication chain.” More on AI: Hackers Break Into Supermarket Fridges and Spoil All the Groceries
Futurism
Cops Not Happy When Man Creates Flock-Style Camera That Only Collects Police Plates
2026-10-13

Cops Not Happy When Man Creates Flock-Style Camera That Only Collects Police Plates

Futurism | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
When police showed up to Anthony Sistilli’s front door in September, it wasn’t to accuse him of a crime or take a witness statement, but to ask how he could have the nerve to turn AI-integrated surveillance cameras on the cops themselves. A Canadian software engineer and YouTuber, Sistilli became interested in automatic license plate readers (ALPRs) following the explosion of outrage against surveillance company Flock Safety in the US, Tom’s Hardware reported. Though Canada lacks a centralized corporate surveillance regime like Flock in the US, the country’s police have been rapidly scaling a more decentralized ALPR network with cameras that function in much the same way. Responding to the rise in government surveillance, Sistilli decided to homebrew his own ALPR — though instead of tracking random civilians, it was designed to track police cars. “I started to wonder, how hard is it to actually make a company like Flock?” Sistilli explained in a YouTube video. So Sistilli ordered a wall-mounted 4K surveillance camera online, plugged it into his laptop, and instructed his AI assistant to vibe-code tracking software that would only flag police cruisers. The end result was a prototype ALPR, which the software engineer mounted on his car’s dashboard to test in the real world. “At first, the video footage was super laggy” Sistilli explained, “because I was trying to calculate the chance of every single car on the road being a police officer in full-4K while also distinguishing their license plates.” But after lowering the resolution and instructing the AI-system to only attempt a license-plate read after a vehicle was confirmed to be a police car, the full prototype was up and running. Unfortunately, local police soon caught onto Sistilli’s handiwork, and paid him a visit in order to see what was going on. “Two police officers walked up my driveway and knocked on my door to question me about it,” Sistilli explains in his video. “It did feel really weird that they were asking me what my ‘intentions were’… one of them took the time to specifically mention ‘how scary’ it would be for them, because people would be able to find out where they lived, when they got on their shift, and what they were doing throughout the day.” Sistilli’s response? “I think that’s the exact same concern that civilians have too,” he said. NTangible bets it can predict how athletes will perform under pressure
2026-10-14

NTangible bets it can predict how athletes will perform under pressure

TechCrunch | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. When collegiate sports programs recruit athletes, they can measure concrete metrics like a player’s sprint speed, throwing velocity, and physical strength. But there has never been a clear way to quantify the mental aspects of sports — how well will this athlete perform in high-pressure situations? Will they stay calm, focus, and give it their best shot? Or will they panic in the spotlight and mess up? NTangible co-founder and CEO Dan Connerty thinks he’s unlocked the secret to measuring if a player will execute in the most important moments. “We really wanted to hyper focus on this idea of clutch performance,” Connerty, a professional baseball player turned sports-tech entrepreneur, told TechCrunch. “Cognitive [science] itself is just this black box that nobody’s wanted to touch, because nobody’s had the ability to measure it accurately prior.” Alongside psychologist Dr. Ed Levine, a professor emeritus at the University of South Florida, Connerty developed a psychological assessment that he says can predict how likely an athlete is to perform under pressure. The Clutch Factor assessment is inspired by the Thematic Apperception Test, a psychological examination in which people are presented with photos of ambiguous situations and describe what they see happening, who they imagine themselves to be in the scene, and what they think might happen next. “There’s no right or wrong answers,” Connerty said. “The key words that you use, the way in which you use those words, and the semantic phrasing associated with it get scored against the neurolinguistic scoring guide that psychologists created, and it shoots out your clutch factor score.” The score is between 0 and 1000 and, according to Connerty, an athlete who scores over a 750 is twice as likely to commit to a Division I school. “What we’ve been able to do is leverage this analysis, where we can show the predictive ability,” he said. “This number can be heavily used in rankings, leaderboards, recruiting conversations, NIL conversations, and scholarship conversations, especially in the crazy wild west that is collegiate sports right now, where we’re writing $4 million checks to quarterbacks.” A possible flaw in the product, however, could be that athletes seeking to impress coaches and recruiters may answer the questions dishonestly to get a higher clutch score. When I took the quiz and answered honestly, I got a below average score of 584, which didn’t surprise me — I dreaded high-leverage situations when I played high school sports. When I took the quiz a second time, answering as I imagined a very level-headed, clutch, top recruit might respond, I scored an 802. Perhaps the platform would work better as a way for coaches to learn more about their athletes and help them improve in weaker areas. Connerty thinks that “clutch” is a skill that can be trained. While he isn’t positioning NTangible as a training platform, he worked with former MLB sports psychologist Howie Schwartz to create a list of exercises that athletes can use as a starting point for upping their mental game. While Connerty’s background is in baseball, NTangible now supports testing for nine sports. He also has ambitions to expand the clutch test to apply to corporate settings, so that HR departments can evaluate employees’ strengths and weaknesses under pressure. The NTerpret assessment translates more directly to the workplace — the respondent answers questions about how they best respond to coaching and feedback, giving management data on how that employee processes instructions and acts on information. NTangible’s most viable business model seems to be selling to sports organizations and companies directly, rather than to individual athletes. The company will elaborate on its strategy on stage on Wednesday at TechCrunch Disrupt, where it’s currently among the top 20 companies dueling for the grand prize at Startup Battlefield. You can check out the rest of the field, and the investors who will decide the winner, by joining us in person this week at Disrupt, happening Tuesday through Thursday, October 13 to 15, in downtown San Francisco. Scimplify raises $25M to challenge China’s grip on critical materials with AI
2026-10-14

Scimplify raises $25M to challenge China’s grip on critical materials with AI

TechCrunch | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. As manufacturers across industries strive to reduce their reliance on Chinese suppliers, Indian startup Scimplify has raised $25 million to expand its AI-powered approach to producing specialty chemicals and critical materials, with semiconductors, defense, and energy emerging as key growth markets. The all-equity strategic round was led by Hitachi Ventures, with customers Henkel and Teknor Apex joining as new investors. Existing backers Accel India, Bertelsmann India Investments, Omnivore, and 3one4 Capital also participated. The round values the Bengaluru-based startup at $250 million after the investment, according to a person familiar with the transaction, up from $150 million after a $40 million Series B round in March 2025. The latest investment brings Scimplify’s total equity funding to $80 million, the startup said, though it declined to disclose its valuation. The growing adoption of AI and advanced computational tools has accelerated materials discovery and research. However, turning promising chemistry into commercially available products at scale remains a slow and complex process. Manufacturers need to develop production processes, qualify facilities, and meet stringent quality standards, all while navigating supply chains limited to a handful of countries. Scimplify is seeking to shorten that process. In some cases, the startup, founded in 2023, has gone from receiving a customer’s requirements to delivering the finished material in just two months, compared with the 12 to 18 months such work would typically take, co-founder Sachin Santhosh (pictured above) said in an interview. Once it receives an order, Scimplify develops chemical manufacturing processes at its research facilities in Bengaluru and Hyderabad and then transfers them to partner factories for commercial production. Its proprietary AI platform, Scinode, helps coordinate the work by identifying suitable production routes and manufacturing facilities, while tracking quality and regulatory requirements. The platform uses specialized AI agents for different functions, including research, manufacturing, and quality control, Santhosh told TechCrunch. “Each of these agents is basically solving for what [a customer] would want, working backward from the customer’s requirement,” Santhosh said. “This helps us move a lot faster and remember customer context even for future references.” Scimplify’s in-house team of scientists and engineers oversees production across a network of more than 500 manufacturing facilities. It helps customers establish alternative sources for materials whose supply is concentrated in China and a few other countries. About 90% of Scimplify’s manufacturing currently takes place in India. The startup, however, plans to expand its production network to the U.S. and Japan over the next year as it seeks to attract more customers in both markets, Santhosh said. The U.S. has emerged as a key growth market, with Scimplify’s annualized revenue run rate in the country rising sixfold to about $60 million from $10 million a year ago, according to Santhosh. The market now accounts for roughly 30% of its business, while customers outside India collectively contribute more than half of its revenue, compared with less than 5% about 18 months ago. Overall, Scimplify, which serves more than 1,000 customers and has delivered products to more than 40 countries, has reached an annualized revenue run rate of about $200 million, tripling from a year earlier, Santhosh told TechCrunch. The startup is targeting a $400 million annualized revenue run rate over the next year. Scimplify has also been EBITDA-positive for the past two months and expects to turn profitable after tax this month, he said. Energy, electronics, and defense now account for about 45% to 50% of Scimplify’s revenue, up from less than 15% a year ago, Santhosh stated. The growth comes as AI fuels demand for materials used in electronics and energy infrastructure, while ongoing wars drive demand from the defense industry. Scimplify is working on producing tungsten derivatives from scrap, offering customers an alternative source of materials whose supply is heavily concentrated in China. The startup is also working on rare-earth materials, another category where global supply chains remain heavily dependent on China. The startup works with customers including Japanese electronics manufacturer TDK, German chemicals group BASF, and Hitachi. Santhosh cited a project involving TDK as an example of how the startup can shorten the time needed to develop and deliver specialty materials. Timelines, he said, vary depending on the project. With its latest funding, Scimplify plans to deploy around $7.5 million for its U.S. expansion, including building out its manufacturing network, Santhosh said, adding that the allocation could change as the startup’s plans evolve. It also earmarked another $2 million for further development of Scinode. The startup employs about 340 people, including roughly 70 scientists. Most of its workforce is based in India, though it also has local teams in the U.S., Japan, Indonesia, and the UAE. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Justice Department says North Koreans have infiltrated dozens of US companies
2026-10-14

Justice Department says North Koreans have infiltrated dozens of US companies

TechCrunch | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The U.S. Department of Justice is warning that hackers working for the North Korean regime are increasingly relying on Americans to facilitate the hacking of U.S. companies. In an announcement on Tuesday, prosecutors said they have indicted and arrested at least two people, and conducted searches across dozens of suspected “laptop farms” in Texas. The computers were allegedly used by North Koreans to remotely hack into American companies and steal sensitive data. According to the DOJ, federal authorities seized over 180 computers that they believe American companies had shipped out to their newly hired employees, not realizing that they were sanctioned North Korean spies who had used stolen identities of U.S. citizens to gain employment. The DOJ said that it raided over 40 laptop farms across Texas, the largest disclosed number of homes and residences used for facilitating North Korean hacks, which were owned by at least two American facilitators. CrowdStrike, which helped the FBI investigate and disrupt the latest North Korean operations, said that laptop farms are “vital” to North Korea in helping its hackers overcome physical, financial, and legal challenges of not physically being in the United States. The prosecutors said at least 70 U.S.-based companies had been infiltrated by the hackers, who are believed to have generated around $3 million in stolen funds. The DOJ did not name the affected companies, but described them as healthcare, cyber support, retail, communications, and manufacturing sectors across Ohio and 16 other states, including the District of Columbia. “Increasingly, North Korean IT workers are turning to recruiting American facilitators to counter improved private sector detections and law enforcement actions. These workers are hiring facilitators to complete a wider variety of tasks, and in some cases subcontracting entire jobs to third-party foreign nationals,” the Justice Department said. Oftentimes, North Korean hackers use real-looking email addresses, spoofed LinkedIn profiles, and extensive online and social media accounts as cover for their activities. In a separate public service announcement, the FBI said that there are “thousands” of North Korean state-controlled IT workers working at Western companies, which “pose a significant national security and intellectual property theft insider threat.” This is the latest wave of arrests, indictments, and enforcement action targeting North Korean activities in the United States in recent years. The breakaway regime has used this “remote IT workers” scheme to allow North Koreans to gain employment with U.S. and European companies, earn a wage, and then steal sensitive information and intellectual property while as employees. The victim companies are then extorted into paying the hackers further. The remote IT workers’ scheme is one of several strands of illicit revenue that the North Korean regime aims to generate, including hacking crypto exchanges and open source developer projects, with the aim of funding its internationally sanctioned nuclear weapons program. The regime is thought to have stolen at least $2 billion in crypto during 2025 alone. Experts on North Korea have put the number of hacked companies and the amount of stolen crypto as likely much higher than reported. Earlier this year, CrowdStrike said in a separate report that North Korean hackers were behind around half of all documented “hands-on-keyboard” intrusions over the past year, referring to hacks that are carried out by humans and not automated tools or AI. TechCrunch
Science Corporation implants its vision-restoring chip in its first Stargardt disease patient
2026-10-13

Science Corporation implants its vision-restoring chip in its first Stargardt disease patient

TechCrunch | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Science Corporation CEO Max Hodak announced that the company has implanted its vision-restoring chip, PRIMA, into its first patient with Stargardt disease, an inherited eye disease that gradually destroys central vision. Hodak, the former president and co-founder of Neuralink, the brain implant company, explained onstage at TechCrunch Disrupt how the company’s work with PRIMA fits into its broader pursuit of a novel brain-computer interface (BCI). “The retina is part of the central nervous system,” Hodak said. “We consider cochlear implants, retinal prostheses, motor prostheses, these are all different types of BCIs.” Hodak outlined a vision in which BCIs expand into a wide range of medical technology applications, rather than simply being a way to control computers. While PRIMA is commercially available in Europe for patients who’ve lost their sight to geographic atrophy, an advanced form of age-related macular degeneration, the company believes its approach can be applied to multiple diseases, including Stargardt disease, which is currently considered incurable. Stargardt disease affects the outer retina, causing the gradual death of the eye’s light-sensing cells while potentially sparing the inner retina, the part that passes visual signals onto the brain. It causes a central blind spot that gradually enlarges over time. Approximately 150,000 people worldwide have lost vision due to the disease. “One of the nice things about this device approach, when I was saying the brain’s a computer, vision is electrical potential, like electrical activity down the optic nerve,” Hodak said. “That’s all vision is to the brain, and so if you can get the optic nerve to signal the rest of the brain correctly, then you can provide vision to it.” The company says its first patient with Stargardt disease received the PRIMA implant in May as part of its ongoing trial at the Sydney Eye Hospital in Australia. During the patient’s first rehabilitation session, he saw a letter, and during the second, he was able to read a word. To use the device, patients undergo an hourlong procedure that plants a small chip in the back of their eye, where the photoreceptors have degenerated. Then they wear camera-equipped glasses that transmit a view of the world to the chip. The implant essentially works like artificial photoreceptors. “The device that this patient has is a two millimeter by two millimeter implant placed in the back of the eye, and so it’s like looking through a straw,” Hodak said. “It is not a big field of view color vision. It’s a patch in their central vision where … it’s black and white, but it is sharp. It’s high-contrast sharp, and so they can see a couple letters at a time. The next step is to make the field of view bigger. So to make a bigger implant, you have to deal with the curvature of the eye, and we need to make the electrodes smaller so that we get closer to native levels of detail.” He continued, “I think we have a clear path. There’s a ‘next generation’ that’s being worked on that’ll be in humans in the next year, and over the next five years, I think we’ll be able to get pretty close to native acuity.” When Hodak founded Science Corporation in 2021 after leaving Neuralink, the company outlined plans to develop advanced neural engineering and brain-computer interface technology. But first, the company wants to prove its methods work and develop a sustainable business, which is why it’s working to restore vision with the visually impaired. As for Science Corporation’s brain-computer interface technology, it has previously said that its sensor will be implanted inside the skull and rest on top of the brain, unlike a Neuralink device, which is inserted directly into brain tissue. The company has said it believes the device could help address neurological conditions, such as possibly monitoring neurological activity in patients with brain tumors. “In the near term, we’re going to be applying our first clinical indication, which hasn’t been announced publicly, is a neurodegenerative disease that everybody’s heard of,” Hodak said. “We’re also looking at stroke. For example, if you’ve lost part of your brain because it’s been dead due to stroke, if you can get connections into the deep brain in two different areas, you can imagine adding new cortical areas, just like adding new brain areas entirely — something simply not possible with conventional electrodes. But, I’d say longer term, the long-term mission of the company is to understand how the brain creates the mind and to learn to build engineered brains.” Hodak went on to note that Science Corporation’s belief is that many people will eventually use some kind of BCI. While the company isn’t designing for “healthy 40-year-olds,” he said, it believes people will eventually need some kind of BCI by the age of 60 or 70. “We want to figure out how do we extend not just lifespan but health span,” Hodak said. Cerebras CEO says we’re nearing a future where everyone has a personal AI assistant at home
2026-10-13

Cerebras CEO says we’re nearing a future where everyone has a personal AI assistant at home

TechCrunch | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Cerebras Systems CEO and co-founder Andrew Feldman believes we’re nearing a future where everyone will have an AI appliance in their own home. Speaking onstage at TechCrunch Disrupt 2026 on Tuesday, the chipmaker executive answered a question about what would need to happen for AI to become a home appliance, saying, “Actually, I don’t think that’s very far away.” He pointed to the advances in consumer AI agents, like Meta’s Muse and Instinct, the startup now valued at $10 billion, as examples of how AI is finding its footing with everyday users. As for home AI? He predicts that we’ll have personal AI assistants that run locally. “Just like your cell phone, they’ll do some work on device and some work going back to the cloud,” Feldman explained. “But I think they will get to know you very well, and you will have a security perimeter around them. And they will certainly have access to credit cards and be able to buy things for you,” he continued. “I think they will make a meaningful impact in making our lives better.” Of course, for an AI chipmaker, the vision of a home AI appliance, or at least widespread consumer adoption of agentic technology, is certainly appealing. But Feldman isn’t just working on the hardware infrastructure; he’s a personal AI user himself, saying that he now uses “a collection of agents” to sweep his calendar, pull up information about the people he’s going to have a call with, and more. While AI is already doing many of the things we used to do manually, Feldman is most excited about the new possibilities — things that weren’t possible before. He’s leaving it up to other founders to figure out what those might be, though. “One of the reasons I love being in infrastructure is because I don’t need the consumer-facing idea … I like building the platforms that other people’s ideas take flight on,” he said. Industrial Next wants factory robots to switch tasks in minutes, not weeks
2026-10-13

Industrial Next wants factory robots to switch tasks in minutes, not weeks

TechCrunch | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026 has officially begun. Grab your pass now to be at the center of the startup epicenter. REGISTER NOW Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. In most factories, automation is fixed: robots and machines are programmed for one specific task and product. Changing a line means reprogramming and physical retooling, or swapping out equipment, which can take weeks or months. San Francisco-based Industrial Next says it’s changing that. The startup, which presented today on the Startup Battlefield stage at TechCrunch Disrupt 2026, builds AI-driven, self-contained robotic stations powered by proprietary foundation models that connect vision, task context, and motion. The company says this lets a production line be retooled in minutes rather than weeks without traditional programming. The goal is to let manufacturers automate complex and delicate tasks that traditionally require human labor. The company announced onstage that it’s partnering with BMW Group Technology Office to evaluate Industrial Next’s AI-powered dexterous manipulation for manufacturing. Industrial Next was founded in 2021 by Lukas Pankau and Allen Pan, both of whom previously worked at Tesla. Pankau served as a lead electrical architect at Tesla before moving on to Waymo. Pan led Tesla’s autonomous factory efforts and later became a chief architect at autonomous driving company Neolix. The pair came up with the idea for Industrial Next while working together at Tesla. Pankau’s first encounter with Pan was literally tripping over him as Pan was asleep on a factory catwalk after pulling an all-nighter. Although the two didn’t get along at first, they soon realized they shared an obsession with getting things done and had complementary skills. After years of watching cutting-edge vehicle and self-driving technology get held back by decades-old factory equipment, the founders realized that manufacturing needed to be rebuilt. “We had all these fancy things that we could do on cars. I could run machine learning algorithms to design a car for me. But I couldn’t figure out how to program a robot, and this was pretty common for engineers at Tesla,” Pankau said. “You had this massive divide, right? You had people who are really good at what they’re doing — designing cars and designing self-driving systems — and then you had them effectively stumped at the factory looking at this equipment from the 1980s. And so we started rethinking what this should look like using the technology that we have today and using everything that we know today.” The two kept thinking about their frustrations with industrial equipment even after moving on to their next jobs. They stayed in touch, often reminiscing about the machines that never functioned the way they wanted, and eventually decided they could do better. “I was at Waymo at the time, kind of bored out of my mind because I was missing that pace of Tesla,” Pankau said. Pan, he added, was in a similar position at Momenta, a developer of self-driving systems in China. Pan recalled their conclusion: “We’re like, we’re not getting any younger, and we’re still obviously passionate about it. We still keep complaining about industrial technology. Like, this is three years later, we should start the company.” Pankau notes that traditional industrial robots are typically programmed to perform highly specific, repeatable tasks. As a result, even a small change in the process or product can cause the robot to fail or trigger costly downtime, which forces manufacturers to simplify their products for repeatability. Making robots truly flexible presents a different challenge. They need enormous amounts of training data and, unlike AI models trained on the internet, robotics lacks the vast datasets needed to teach machines how to perform complex physical tasks. “We can make robots do all these cool things like dance around, because there’s data for that, but we can’t make them assemble high-precision things,” Pankau explained. “This is where we’ve made a lot of progress on using simulated environments to do these high-precision and high-dexterity tasks. The secret sauce for the robots that we have is that we have given them an understanding of the process in general, but we build that understanding using an extremely high-fidelity simulation engine that is close to the real dynamics to where we can let the robot do these kinds of high-precision tasks almost without seeing the parts at all.” In other words, the robots practice in a highly detailed virtual world that closely mimics real-world physics. Industrial Next’s robotic stations can be reconfigured, combined, and redeployed across product lines. New tasks are taught through simulation and demonstration, rather than hard-coded scripts. Pankau explained that some of the startup’s workstations are already used in making one of the world’s most popular smartphones. They handle tasks that manufacturers had tried and failed to automate for years, such as threading a delicate flex cable and snapping a tiny connector cap into place. (He wasn’t at liberty to disclose the customer.) He also described a “film peeling” station, where their AI-powered robots strip protective film off camera modules and buttons at or above human speed with 100% accuracy, even when moved to a new phone model and production line they have never seen. “We started this company because that is the kind of performance that we wish we had at Tesla, where we were bypassing robot stations because we couldn’t program them effectively enough using traditional technologies,” Pankau said. “Now we want to take this to more complicated tasks than just building a phone. We ultimately want to take this to building a full car end-to-end without any human touching ever.” To check out more of the Startup Battlefield — along with a slate of established founders and VCs whose takes on the industry you won’t want to miss — join us at Disrupt, happening this week in downtown San Francisco. Climate Change Is Accelerating Faster Than We Can Respond, Scientists Warn
2026-10-14

Climate Change Is Accelerating Faster Than We Can Respond, Scientists Warn

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A firefighter sprays a hotspot while fighting the Bike fire in the outskirts of Santa Clarita, California on Oct. 11, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The climate is warming up faster than we can respond. According to a new report published Oct. 14 in the journal BioScience, “Earth’s climate system is moving deeper into uncharted territory.” The State of the Climate, an annual report that tracks the Earth’s “vital signs,” monitors several climate related indicators—ranging from greenhouse gas concentrations and temperature changes to tree cover loss due to wildfire. The researchers found that, of the 16 planetary vital signs, seven are currently at record extremes. This includes sea levels and greenhouse gasses in the atmosphere. Meanwhile several others, such as areas burned during wildfires and ocean heat levels, show evidence of acceleration or abrupt shifts. The researchers warn that the rate of global warming appears to have accelerated, from about 0.05°C per decade between 1880 and 1970 to roughly 0.36°C per decade between 2010 and 2024—and the 2°C threshold meant to limit planetary damage could be reached before 2050 they warn. These accelerating and intensifying changes could raise the risk of reinforcing feedback loops and crossing tipping points—critical thresholds that, when crossed, can lead to accelerating and often irreversible environmental changes. In 2025, the Earth passed its first climate tipping point, as rising ocean temperatures, acidification, overfishing, and pollution are combining to cause coral bleaching and mortality. "Earth’s life-support systems are being pushed beyond stable limits," the report states, "with changes unfolding faster than our ability to respond." This year, a number of extreme weather events made the impacts of an acceleratingly warming planet impossible to ignore. July 2026 was the hottest month on record for the contiguous United States, with ocean temperatures also reaching a record high that month. Back-to-back heat waves in Europe lead to an estimated 35,000 excess deaths, preliminary reports show. A number of regions—including Utah in the U.S., Ontario, Canada, along with France, Spain, Britain, and Belgium have either experienced their worst wildfire years in history or are on track to do so. While we don’t yet know enough to link these individual weather events to climate change, research shows that it is supercharging extreme weather events. Concentrations of greenhouse gases—including carbon dioxide, methane, and nitrous oxide—all reached record levels in 2026, the researchers say. In May 2026, atmospheric carbon dioxide exceeded 428 parts per million, roughly 50% above pre-industrial levels and generally higher than any time in the last 2 million years. The authors of today’s report also tracked indicators of the human activities driving these planetary changes, like energy use, food production, and economic growth. Fossil fuel consumption remains the dominant driver of these changes. Fossil fuel consumption rose by 0.9% in 2025, the report found, with oil, coal, and gas all reaching record highs, and annual energy-related carbon dioxide emissions surpassed 41 gigatons for the first time—the equivalent of emissions released by over 10,800 coal-fired power plants in one year. There have, however, been some signs of progress, the report notes. Low-carbon sources provided roughly 43% of global electricity generation in 2025, and solar and wind energy consumption grew by approximately 18%. That was before the war in Iran laid bare the world’s reliance on fossil fuels, and triggered a global green energy boom. But cutting carbon emissions will no longer be enough on its own, the report states. Adaptation measures—ranging from shifts towards plant-based diets, to restoring mangroves to better protect against storm surges—will be key to adjusting to a changing climate. The report underscores an unavoidable truth. "We are witnessing the rapid destabilization of the Earth’s climate system,” it states. “This is no longer a warning for the future; it is a diagnosis of the present.”

A New Report Sheds Light on the Secretive Race to Build Self-Improving AI
2026-10-14

A New Report Sheds Light on the Secretive Race to Build Self-Improving AI

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings For months, Anthropic and OpenAI have warned that AI may be on the verge of improving itself. This tipping point, known as “recursive self-improvement,” or RSI, could mean that new AI capabilities arrive faster than ever. But the advent of RSI might also be highly perilous for humanity, potentially marking the stage at which developers lose control of AI systems entirely. Even as AI companies warn about the potential dangers of RSI, they have shared remarkably little about how they’re pursuing it. A new report, published Wednesday by the firm Gladstone AI, attempts to change that. It gives one of the most detailed pictures yet of how RSI could work and how AI companies are progressing toward it. The report is based on what the authors say are conversations with half a dozen researchers inside the top AI companies. The report, exclusively shared with TIME ahead of its publication, emphasizes that AI companies are “actively working on” automating all parts of the AI development pipeline, including generating new ideas, running experiments to test those ideas, and interpreting the results. Most of the insiders Gladstone spoke to “believe RSI will start within the next 3 to 12 months, and possibly sooner, assuming there’s no U.S. government intervention or slowdown,” the report states. Read More: Inside the Race to Make AI Build Itself “There's a fair amount of [disaster] prepping and bio-bunker purchasing going on” among these researchers, says Edouard Harris, Gladstone’s chief technology officer. The aim of the report, Gladstone’s executives tell TIME, is to give lawmakers a better understanding of how RSI works, and, in turn, a greater ability to regulate it. “Our goal here was to define recursive self-improvement well enough that people can have a sane conversation about it, especially in Washington,” says Jeremie Harris, Gladstone’s CEO. (The pair are brothers.) “There are all these bills proposed to ban superintelligence, which are really interesting attempts to grapple with this problem. But what we're more interested in is: what is the process that needs to be regulated?” The report gives a simplified outline of how the AI research pipeline currently operates inside AI companies—and how those companies are beginning to automate the process. For the first phase of training an AI model, known as pretraining, much of the emphasis is on finding ways to squeeze more intelligence out of a model with less and less computing power, known in the industry as “compute.” The AI companies take a portfolio approach to testing these ideas. Researchers allocate a small amount of compute to each idea, using it to train a series of models. The models that reliably perform better as they scale up are selected for another round of training, using larger amounts of compute. Companies don’t have infinite compute, which limits the number of promising ideas they can test at larger scales. This means that even if AI companies can train an AI that is able to generate ideas that seem promising, it matters greatly how many bad ideas it generates simultaneously. If companies need to spend huge amounts of time or compute filtering good AI-generated ideas from bad ones, it might make sense to continue to rely on human researchers with sound intuition. Conversely, AI companies with access to more compute are able to test more ideas—potentially allowing them to reach RSI first. With more compute to throw at experiments, companies may also be likelier to catch ideas that the Gladstone report describes as the rarest and most desired by AI companies: those that perform better as more compute is thrown at them, even if they appear to be duds at smaller scales. Being able to tell when those ideas are found—and particularly whether AI is helping find them—“is just such a critical part of this process that right now legislators have essentially zero visibility into,” says Jeremie Harris. One of the traits that makes regulating RSI tricky is that it is a gradual process. “This is actually one of the reasons why we went to pains to describe it in so much detail,” says Edouard Harris. “So that it is possible to point to a part of this process and say: a human needs to be there.”
