How School Violence Catalyzed Philippine Lawmakers to Crack Down on Kids’ Social Media Use
2026-10-14

How School Violence Catalyzed Philippine Lawmakers to Crack Down on Kids’ Social Media Use

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Filipino teenagers rest at convenient store and play video game on smartphone in Antipolo City, Philippines, on Oct. 3, 2019. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings School shootings used to be rare in the Philippines. But a spate of incidents involving students this year seems to have catalyzed the Southeast Asian nation’s lawmakers to swiftly pass bills cracking down on what they deem to be a cause of the deadly trend: social media. On Monday, the Philippines House of Representatives passed a bill banning children under 13 from social media platforms, while those between 13 and 17 would have limited access, with 287 members voting in favor and only three against the measure. The latest bill follows its Senate counterpart, which passed last week but has a higher age threshold, barring people under 18 from social media platforms and services classified as “high-risk.” The bills must first reconcile their differences before they head to the President for signature. But their passage already follows steps taken by other countries such as Australia, the U.K., and more in introducing age-based restrictions on social media amid concerns over these platforms’ impact on children’s mental health and safety. Philippine President Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr. said that it was not “surprising at all” that Congress took action amid concerns that social media is believed to have influenced children to take part in violent acts. However, Marcos said his Administration would still study whether such a ban could work in the Southeast Asian nation of more than 110 million people. “That’s also something that we’re studying really hard,” Marcos told reporters in Singapore on Sunday. “It has its advantages,” he said, but he warned it could also “simply make children hide their use.” Age-specific social media bans have been up for debate in the Philippines since at least 2019, but it gained momentum earlier this year, following reports of a foiled mass shooting plot by students in February as well as shootings and stabbings in other regions in the months that followed. Education Secretary Juan Edgardo Angara said harmful online influences were a possible factor behind those incidents, saying in July that experts have pointed to “exposure of young people to damaging social media sites, negative influences, groomers, and terrorist groups online actively recruiting (children).” A ban, however, is an escalatory approach in one of the countries from which users spend the most time online. Critics have also questioned whether such bans are effective. Australia was the first country to ban social media for children, but a community-based study published in June 2026 showed “limited implementation, incomplete compliance, and substantial circumvention of social media restrictions” in the three months that followed since the ban took effect in December 2025. The Philippine Senate’s version of the social media bill passed almost unanimously, save for Sen. Risa Hontiveros, a children and women’s rights champion. Hontiveros, in explaining her sole dissenting vote, said she prefers “tiered restrictions” for adolescents instead of a blanket under-18 ban. “Our legislation must reflect the realities of how social media has also benefited young people,” Hontiveros said, adding that a sudden prohibition could drive children’s social media underground. Hontiveros said that, instead of keeping children off platforms, the goal should be to create a safe internet environment. “Taking it away from them all of a sudden without providing adequate ways for them to cope with its absence,” she said, “is not the answer to their needs and it is not the answer to our collective fears.” Sen. Robinhood Padilla, who sponsored the Senate’s version, told a local radio station that the upper chamber’s measure was necessary to curb not only violence in schools but bad influence on youth as a whole. “This measure must be passed immediately because [social media has] caused a lot of bad things to our youth, especially to minors,” Padilla said on Oct. 11, citing instances of bullying, suicides, and school shootings. “All these problems have compounded over time.” The Senate’s approved bill increased the threshold of restrictions from the original version’s proposal of covering those under 16 years old to covering everyone under 18 years old. The passed bill proposes disabling or suspending the accounts of minors and banning them from creating new ones. Platforms will be required to have effective age-assurance measures. The Senate’s bill does not cover email, private messaging apps that do not have public or group features, school platforms, news websites, or online gaming platforms focused on gaming and not social networking. Senate President Sherwin Gatchalian claimed in June that “a lot of young people are addicted to social media and violent games, and studies showed that it has an influence on their mental health.” A clear causal link between violent video gaming and violence has yet to be established. Under the House proposal, the ban would apply to children under 13 years old. Teenagers under 18 could be on the platforms but would be prohibited from accessing “harmful or prohibited” content, advertisements, livestreams, short-form video, behavior profiling, making purchases, and gambling-like mechanisms. Parents must consent to their children’s use of social media, and this consent would be retractable. Several school-related incidents rocked the Philippines ahead of the passage of the bills. In March, Philippine police revealed seven teenagers from the Calabarzon region in Luzon island were allegedly planning a school attack in February. The five boys and two girls, according to police, allegedly shared images and videos of school shootings in other countries. They were turned over to social workers. Then in June, two male students aged 14 and 15 opened fire in their school in Tacloban City, killing three students. Twenty others were injured in the shooting incident. In turn, Philippine authorities temporarily banned GoreBox, a sandbox-style action game that simulates violence and destruction using weapons. In August, a high school student from a campus in the southern Philippine city of Zamboanga shot a fellow student in school before killing himself. The shooter livestreamed the attack using a body camera. Only a month later, a shooting at another southern Philippine high school left two 14-year-old students dead and eight people wounded. The gunman, a teenager also from the school,  fatally shot himself. Upon investigation, police found posts the shooter made in an online community where young people involved in school shootings are idolized. While gun violence is not uncommon in the country, the incidents have forced the Philippines to reckon with a problem it once thought of as unique to the U.S. But the Philippine Mental Health Association argues that a blanket ban on social media is a “non-solution.” PMHA national executive director Dr. Carolina Uno-Rayco said in a statement to the media Thursday: “Preventing violence requires a comprehensive approach that includes mental health support, safe school environments, responsible online platforms and early identification and intervention.” Activist group Akbayan Youth argued that the proposed social media ban penalizes young users by disrupting those who depend on them for education and livelihood and called for redirecting the regulation. “Do not punish the youth,” the organization’s national chairperson Khylla Meneses said in a statement. “Hold accountable the platforms that profit from how social media operates.”

VisionQuest Puts a Frustratingly Conventional End to Marvel’s WandaVision Trilogy
2026-10-14

VisionQuest Puts a Frustratingly Conventional End to Marvel’s WandaVision Trilogy

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The marriage of Disney+ and the Marvel Cinematic Universe seemed, at first, to be a perfect match. WandaVision, the high-concept miniseries that placed Wanda Maximoff (Elizabeth Olsen) and her beloved synthezoid the Vision (Paul Bettany) in a succession of retro sitcoms and challenged viewers to figure out what was actually happening beyond the suburban veneer, kicked off the venture in 2021 with huge audiences and near-unanimous critical praise. But it ended in the same predictable, digitally enhanced battle as every other MCU mediocrity. With a few fun exceptions, like this year’s renewed-then-canceled Wonder Man and the witchy 2024 WandaVision sequel Agatha All Along, the returns have been diminishing ever since. So it’s not particularly surprising that Disney seems to be paring back its small-screen Marvel offerings. To understand what went wrong, you need only watch the first episode of VisionQuest, the final installment of the WandaVision trilogy. It begins with Bettany narrating a summary of Vision’s MCU storyline, from his origin as the creation of sociopathic supervillain Ultron and education in humanity at the hands of Avengers Bruce Banner and Tony Stark to his annihilation by Thanos and resurrection as a projection of Wanda’s mourning, that will only make sense to viewers who’ve followed that saga for more than a decade. Soon, it’s introducing a bunch of anthropomorphized AIs (Orla Brady’s F.R.I.D.A.Y., Emily Hampshire’s E.D.I.T.H., James D’Arcy’s J.A.R.V.I.S.) that have their own franchise backstories. Less than 15 minutes in, Vision is sitting in a cinema, within his own robotic mind, brooding over memories of Wanda. The story is so unwieldy, at this point, it’s impossible to follow without constant reminders of what came before. Yet the VisionQuest narrative is too emotionally simplistic and stylistically conventional, particularly compared to the two previous parts of the trilogy, to merit all that remembering. From left: Orla Brady, James D'Arcy, Johnathan Sayer, and Henry Lewis in VisionQuest Marvel Annihilated for the second time with the collapse of Wanda’s grief fantasia, the Vision has been rebuilt—“same body, different mind,” he tells us, meaning that he no longer has the capacity for love that once bound him to her and the sons, Tommy and Billy, she conjured. In the physical world, he’s in hiding as a human barback at a Scottish pub with relevance to Wanda-Vision romance lore. But his more vivid existence is a life of the (artificial) mind, where he retreats to a sort of Beaux-Arts-meets-midcentury-modern country estate populated by autonomous AIs. While J.A.R.V.I.S. and F.R.I.D.A.Y. lead the virtual household, or Mindspace, “recreational AI” E.D.I.T.H. provides comic relief, crashing through skylights with daredevil stunts. Everyone seems a bit aimless now that Vision is serving beers instead of fighting to save the human race. It gives him time to stew on the loss of his emotions and relationships, to contemplate the extent to which he is and isn’t the same synthezoid who loved his family. His ennui may also be the only plausible explanation for his potentially catastrophic choice to keep Ultron (James Spader) alive, imprisoned in an isolated tower in Vision’s memory palace. Physical reality disrupts Vision’s Tin-Man-esque, if-I-only-had-a-heart wistfulness when a fearsome bounty hunter, Paladin (Todd Stashwick), is hired to apprehend him. It also brings to his attention the existence—and Paladin-related peril—of one Thomas Shepherd (Ruaridh Mollica), a troubled young man in London whom we’re never really given a reason to doubt is a version of Tommy Maximoff. (If you want to know what became of Billy Maximoff, watch Agatha All Along, which remains an ideal Halloween-season binge.) The question is: Are he and the current incarnation of Vision truly father and son? Is this Vision capable of caring for the boy? Somehow, as the season progresses, it all escalates into yet another cataclysm that requires a superpowered champion to save humanity. But wait: Does humanity even want to be saved, or do we subconsciously crave our own extinction? “They’re relentlessly aspirational,” Ultron notes, “but at the same time they wallow in their hardship, misery, guilt.” To illustrate this point, his tower contains a rendering of Hieronymous Bosch’s The Garden of Earthly Delights. Created by Terry Matalas (Star Trek: Picard, 12 Monkeys), VisionQuest loads up on weighty themes—eternal ones like fathers and sons as well as that current ubiquity, AI. (I’m curious how much the story, which dates back to at least 2022, has evolved in response to an increasingly AI-dominated news cycle that has also become an obsession for Hollywood.) Yet, in typical Marvel fashion, it spends more time crafting action spectacles and shifting settings, traversing the physical world as well as infinite MIndspace via virtual backdrops elaborate yet generic enough to resemble video-game levels, than it does developing characters or ideas. Vision is often engaged in tortured introspection, but this is mostly redundant wheel-spinning. The AI handwringing is neither novel nor especially coherent; some undercooked aspects of the latter plot made me long for the next season of Pluribus. Now there’s a character-driven sci-fi drama that knows when to confuse viewers about basics like where a scene is taking place and what’s happening (rarely) and when to give us fodder for more abstract contemplation (often). That’s not to say that VisionQuest is bereft of pleasures. It makes a wonderful showcase for Bettany’s protean acting chops, though it’s hard to explain why without giving too much away. Even if I don’t entirely buy that Vision would hang on to Ultron out of some sense of ersatz-filial loyalty, Bettany and Spader, a consummate cerebral villain, are great together. (One reason it’s hard to get invested in the question of whether these AIs can feel feelings is that the Mindspace characters are so much more vividly portrayed than the flesh-and-blood humans.) And it’s always a treat to see the Marvel monolith plug precisely the right cult performer into a very specific small role. In this case, that’s Diane Morgan, known to fans of British comedy as the fictional, arrogantly ignorant TV presenter Philomena Cunk, as Paladin’s sardonic assistant. There are some cool visual ideas, especially in the way that production designer Will Hughes-Jones conceives of Vision’s Mindspace as a place permeable to outside interference. As a capstone to a stylistically innovative trilogy, though, the show even falls short aesthetically. WandaVision had its Ozzie and Harriet and Brady Bunch riffs. Agatha burned through witchy feminine tropes, from Ouija to catchy original songs in the vein of Steve Nicks. While it periodically calls back to the sitcom realm Wanda created (remember Small Wonder?), VisionQuest is essentially a techy action thriller with a lonely hero who contemplates the nature of his existence. We’ve seen too much of that stuff in 21st century superhero storytelling, and here the monotony is exacerbated by the volume of MCU backstory that must be repeated and referenced in every episode. In theory, Disney+ was a chance for Marvel to deepen its universe, keeping fans’ favorite characters active between movies. But a crucial ingredient has been missing from so much of that content. Ironically enough, it’s vision.

In Defense of the Disney Adult
2026-10-14

In Defense of the Disney Adult

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Disney Adults are so easy to tease. People think they’re immature, embarrassing, cringe. As such, jokes about people who enjoy visiting Disney parks annually (or even multiple times a year) run rampant on social media. Satirized as being mentally stuck in elementary school and shamed for spending exorbitant amounts of money on their Disney fix, “Disney Adult " has become the punchline de jour for someone who needs to grow up and get a life. In their park of choice—whether it’s Magic Kingdom, Epcot, Animal Kingdom, or Hollywood Studios—Disney Adults typically wear some sort of Disney-coded costume or outfit meticulously coordinated with an on-theme Loungefly backpack and mouse ears. Disney Adults are blasted for constantly returning to “the most magical place on Earth,” and their slanderers believe those travel funds should be spent on “real” vacations. Where? Literally anywhere else. The most outrageous of culprits? A Disney Adult who enjoys the Disney bubble… without any children. But in defense of the oft-ridiculed Disney Adult, why put a price on someone else’s joy, especially if you’re not the one bankrolling it? I’ll admit it: I am a Disney Adult. You know what, I’m proud of it. I grew up during the Disney Renaissance era and still never miss a new release, collect merch (especially ornaments), and will dress my dog as Buzz Lightyear from Toy Story this Halloween. When I’m super bored, I check the wait times for rides while I’m on my couch thousands of miles away instead of doomscrolling. I’m confident in enjoying whatever makes me happy, and I choose to invest in my joy. And yes, I am the dreaded Disney Adult without children. For one birthday in particular, I skipped through the park on my way to see the “Happily Ever After” fireworks wearing light-up Tinkerbell wings, accompanied only by my friend of the same age. I agree with the sentiment of many Disney Adults I’ve encountered about why they keep returning. A trip to Walt Disney World feels familiar and welcoming, and it helps people reconnect with their childhood and revisit a time when they felt carefree. And with new attractions and experiences constantly being added, Walt Disney World offers nostalgia with a chance to make new memories every time. In my opinion, Disney Adults are no different than the passionate members of any other fandom. I feel authorized to speak on this from the vantage point of a kid who practically grew up at Yankee Stadium. My dad worked at the home of the New York Yankees for 47 years. He started as a pre-teen selling Cracker Jacks in the stands while growing up in the South Bronx. And when he later worked in finance, he kept his part-time job at the stadium doing inventory, making all of the night and weekend games for as long as I can remember. As a stadium kid (and lifelong Yankees fan), I’ve seen the most diehard Yankees fans spend thousands on season tickets and coveted World Series seats with hopes of seeing the Bronx Bombers win at home. Fans drop real money for endless rounds of pricey stadium beer just to be “in the building.” And for this fanaticism, people barely bat an eye. Throughout my life, I’ve seen these fans praised for their dedication to Yankee Blue and for having the means to own season tickets. When fans show off extensive baseball card collections or take selfies from expensive seats, they get praise, not ridicule. But if a Disney Adult displays a vast collection of limited-edition mouse ears, the collective eye roll feels palpable. As someone who goes to Walt Disney World often (more than twice a year if timing and budget align), I’m used to snarky responses when I say I’m going to Disney: “Again?” Yes, again. Because it brings me joy, and it’s currently hard out here for a whimsical millennial. But unlike being a Disney Adult, there’s a harsh reality to being an avid fan of a specific sports team: the constant possibility of a brutal loss. When purchasing pricey tickets for a sporting event, there’s a chance that your beloved team will lose, that you’ll leave the stadium devastated, and with a lot less cash. When investing in a Disney trip, however, winning or losing isn’t on the line. There’s a high likelihood of having a great experience if a traveler leans into the tried-and-true activities that have historically been fun experiences for them. The guarantee of making happy memories is priceless. Just as there’s no one way to be a sports fan, Disney Adults are not a one-size-fits-all monolith. Yes, some people spend cash on annual passes, endless Lightning Lanes to skip long lines, and enjoy character dining. But there are also so many ways to experience Walt Disney World without even setting foot in the parks. On my last trip to the House of Mouse, I spent a lot of time at the BoardWalk, an area that connects Disney’s Yacht and Beach Club resorts that doesn’t require a ticket. I watched the Magic Kingdom fireworks on the beach with my toes nestled in the sand and far away from crowds. In another effort to avoid large crowds, I enjoyed a paint-and-sip class at Coronado Springs Resort and got a souvenir only I could make. On future trips, I’m hoping to explore a dog-friendly resort where I can bring my Buzz Lightyear pup, and plan a visit tailored for my mother, a senior who wouldn’t be able to walk endlessly in the Orlando heat. We often talk about when she took me to Disney for the first time for my fourth birthday, and all of the changes since then. For her, Star Wars: Galaxy’s Edge will be a must. Perhaps most importantly, I feel safe at Disney. Although I am alert to any dangers, I feel relatively confident that I can walk through the parks without enduring any catcalling or anyone spiking my drink. Which brings me to another plus for going to Disney so much: I can have a blast with a non-alcoholic drink, and no one’s going to judge me. Do I like to travel to places other than Walt Disney World? Yes. I love exploring the Caribbean in particular, where I immerse myself in water activities and learn about the cultures. When I travel to Europe, I tend to take cathedral tours in whatever area I’m traveling in. Despite the stereotype, Disney Adults can have multiple interests. Still, that doesn’t make me feel guilty for the giddiness I feel the moment I step onto the green carpet at Orlando International Airport, knowing that a shuttle is about to transport me into the peace of the Disney bubble. Everyone has their idea of fun that offers a means of escape. As long as it’s safe, why stigmatize someone for it? Disney Adults may be “cringe.” We’re an easy mark. But I can guarantee that not one of us will think twice about returning to a place we love. Life’s tough, and there’s nothing wrong with enjoying some magic every now and then. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Three CTOs Discuss Technology’s Impact on Their Companies
2026-10-14

Three CTOs Discuss Technology’s Impact on Their Companies

TIME | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Chief technology officers gathered for a panel at TIME’s Executives of the Year Impact Dinner: Tech & Data on Tuesday night in New York City and discussed the ways that technology has shifted how their companies operate. Speaking on the panel at the TIME forum were Franziska Bell, CTO and executive vice president of The Home Depot; Fiona Tan, CTO of online marketplace giant Wayfair; and Sam Dozor, CTO of global e-commerce company Rokt, which sponsored Tuesday’s event. Bell emphasized “always being grounded in the consumer experience” when thinking about how to adapt to new and emerging technologies, or what she described as “the art of the possible.” One new AI tool that The Home Depot has deployed is Magic Apron, an “AI empowered home improvement expert,” Bell explained, that can help customers locate items in store, answer how-to questions about products, and respond to images uploaded by users. Applications like Magic Apron, which Bell says has been rolled out in 2,000 U.S. stores, result from Bell assessing the “friction” involved in customer experiences and working to reduce it with technological tools. She did not, however, discount the crucial roles that humans play alongside tech. “I’m a huge believer in human-AI teams,” Bell said. “That’s really where the magic happens.” Tan also spoke about the customer experience at Wayfair and how technology has been leveraged to help consumers figure out what they want from the company’s wide selection of products. “A lot of times people don’t know what they’re looking for, they can’t describe it, and so perhaps we offer you a journey that will resonate,” she said. “We have to also innovate and provide them an experience that speaks more to them.” When it comes to hiring new employees who can tackle consumer experience goals and integrate AI into their work, Tan stressed the importance of “adaptability” in young candidates who can seamlessly adjust to the quickly changing tech landscape. Tan also said that it’s important to pair younger generations with older ones who have more experience at their companies. Dozor said that the moment is prime for companies to invest in technology. “The time is here,” he said. “The time is now, and all of us need to live it.” One of the ways Rokt does this is by expanding the variety of work its employees can do using AI. “It’s amazing to see engineers lean into building product specs and designers writing code,” he said. In that spirit, Rokt, he explained, takes a forward-looking approach to its work, always thinking about what’s next for its employees and what they can accomplish. “Times are changing quickly,” Dozor said, and people can choose to live in the past, present, or future. “We’ve chosen to live six months from now.” TIME Impact Dinner: Executives of the Year: Tech & Data was presented by Rokt.

You do know they're a supermarket chain... right? A supermarket chain with a building full of engineers in Blue Ash, Ohio [app.goo.gl] who do all kinds of IT engineering and software work. Literally hundreds of them. They operate at least 3 data centers, in different regions across the US. They have their own mainframe for merchandising and inventory apps. They have blade servers in each and every grocery store, running thin clients, pharmacy PCs, wireless networks for ePaper shelf tags. They have their own TV studio for filming training videos and doing remote satellite news interviews. They built and run their own loss prevention systems including DVR systems that capture video evidence for legal proceedings from a hundred cameras in each store. They are their own credit card processor, undergoing and passing PCI DSS certification every year because they process literally tens of billions of dollars of transactions. They have their own photo studio, and three different vertically integrated print hubs for doing their own advertising inserts into newspapers. They run their own printing presses. They own stores that sell couches and lumber alongside groceries. They operate a massive pharmacy operation, including a mail-order pharmacy nationwide. They operate literally thousands of fuel stations. They have hundreds of small-footprint stores in addition to their traditional grocery. They build their own mobile apps, with their own pick-up and delivery operations, completely built in-house. They are good enough at collecting order data that they sell the data to their competitors because it's better than what their competitors get. You have no fucking idea what you are talking about. He Was Charged With a Shocking Murder. Now He’s Set to Become Sheriff. What Happened in Between Rocked an Arkansas Town.
2026-10-14

He Was Charged With a Shocking Murder. Now He’s Set to Become Sheriff. What Happened in Between Rocked an Arkansas Town.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The attractions at September’s Lonoke County Fair in central Arkansas felt, for the most part, typical. There was a Ferris wheel, spinning teacups, a livestock pavilion full of award-winning chickens, rabbits, goats, and pigs. For those seeking a slightly more obscure fair activity, there was “Mutton Bustin’,” a rodeo event where kids between the ages of 4 and 8 simulate bull-riding on top of pint-size sheep. Then there was a more peculiar draw, stationed reliably at a table for local and state Republican candidates in this November’s midterm elections: Aaron Spencer, a 38-year-old Army veteran who is running for sheriff of Lonoke County. Spencer was one of the few candidates attending the fair in person, and everyone knew who he was. Even I recognized him from his interviews on CNN and local television, a rugged man with a thick dark beard and “FREEDOM” tattooed down his right forearm. “Oh, it’s actually you!” a woman exclaimed as she approached, somewhat in awe. Spencer offered her and other visitors yard signs reading: “Aaron Spencer. Lonoke County Sheriff.Together WE can build a safer and stronger Lonoke County.” Unless you knew the backstory—and most everyone in Lonoke County does—there was nothing in the candidate’s outward appearance that would have suggested that just four months prior, Spencer had nearly been put on trial for murder. One by one, fairgoers approached, shaking Spencer’s hand. “Bless you,” and “I prayed for you,” and “You’re my hero,” they said. Spencer handed out rows of stickers to the kids, and some people purchased pink campaign shirts. Less than two years earlier, Spencer had been arrested for shooting a 67-year-old man 15 times after there were disturbing charges involving Spencer’s 13-year-old daughter. A shocking series of events had brought him here, standing in front of me a free man on the verge of possibly becoming an elected county official. I wanted to know why people here seemed intent on voting for him, despite it all. When I visited, I learned there was a deeper reason for all of this love for Spencer that I had not yet grasped. Beyond the horror of Spencer’s personal tale, it turned out that Lonoke County had been home to one of the country’s most prolific child predators. And, now, here was Spencer, promising a new kind of lawman. The people liked what they heard. Ask Spencer why he decided to run for sheriff, and he’ll jump straight to the dangers of child sexual abuse, specifically accused perpetrators who are let out on bail pending their trial. “Protecting our children was my big motivation to run,” he told me, explaining the many ways that the system had failed his family. “That’s why in the primary, my slogan was ‘Rape a child in Lonoke County, you can get out of jail for the price of a lawn mower.’ ” The slogan may not have been catchy, but it was based in truth. On the afternoon of July 7, 2024, Sgt. John Ingram and Deputy Brian Duke from the Lonoke County Sheriff’s Office arrived at the Spencer property, a three-acre turkey farm in rural Arkansas about 30 miles from Little Rock. They came to the Spencer home to discuss a report they’d received: that Spencer’s 13-year-old daughter had allegedly been sexually abused by a man old enough to be her grandfather:  Michael Fosler. Fosler did not appear to have a prior criminal record. He was a veteran, a former police officer in Indiana, a widower, and a truck driver. At the time, he was dating a family friend of the Spencers’; his daughter was also friends with the sheriff. Spencer, a handgun tucked into the back of his pants, was displeased and accused the deputies of “creeping around.” His demeanor only worsened as police implied that they were concerned Spencer would take matters into his own hands. Fosler had allegedly met Spencer’s daughter at the home of that family friend, and they had regularly communicated online before the abuse began. This had apparently been going on for months; everyone else was just finding out on this day. Spencer had allegedly found out earlier that day, before the police arrived, and had been out seeking information about Fosler, which prompted the suspicion that he might act as a vigilante. It didn’t help that Spencer began the exchange by asking who had called the sheriff’s office. Ingram appeared to anticipate how Spencer would react. He issued an ominous warning, an exchange which was recorded on his bodycam. “We still don’t live in a country where you can take the law into your own hands, OK?” he told Spencer. The sergeant then posed a “hypothetical” question about a father who might get arrested for vigilante violence. Spencer responded, “Well, hypothetically, maybe that rapist and that father can share a jail cell, and maybe that rapist could be that father’s fucking bitch.” Eventually, Spencer and his wife sat down with the two lawmen for an hour, and Spencer concluded by saying, “I know what I’m allowed and not allowed to do according to the law.” Officers arrested Fosler a few days later, and the indictment listed 43 counts, including, among other things, sex with a child under 15 and multiple counts of possessing child sexual abuse material. Judge Barbara Elmore set his bail at $50,000. Fosler paid the typical 10 percent bond—or $5,000, the cost of a lawn mower—and was released with a vague order not to have contact with children. He received no ankle monitor or other restrictions. Spencer’s fury was evident. On Aug. 11, the turkey farmer posted a disturbing Facebook meme, showing a pen of hogs with the caption: “FUN FACT: A PACK OF HOGS CAN CONSUME AN ENTIRE PEDOPHILE IN LESS THAN 10 MINUTES.” Months passed with Fosler a free man. Spencer remained, as he told police later, “vigilant” because “nobody else is watching him.” Then, on Oct. 8, Spencer woke up late in the night to the barking of his dog. He found his daughter’s bed empty. He suspected that she was with Fosler—it appears he had continued to contact the 13-year-old after bonding out of jail—and decided to go look for her on the country roads while his wife called 911. What happened next, Spencer argued in court, was self-defense. The following facts aren’t disputed: Spencer came upon Fosler’s white F-150 and rammed him, forcing him off the road. Fosler and Spencer had a confrontation in which Spencer fired 16 shots, hitting Fosler 15 times, and then pistol-whipped him in the head. Fosler fell and “rolled like a beach ball,” according to Spencer. His daughter had been in the F-150 throughout. Spencer called 911 and said, “Michael Fosler is fucking dead on the side of the road for trying to kidnap my daughter. I had no choice.” Fosler was actually still alive, however, when law enforcement responded to the scene. The first cops there were from the Lonoke Sheriff’s Office, including Sgt. Ingram, the same man who had gone to Spencer’s home a few months earlier. The officers arrested the now-unarmed Spencer at gunpoint, forcing him to lie on the ground. They cuffed him and put him into the back of the police cruiser with the heat cranked up to make him sweat. Spencer told me that the arrest was particularly humiliating and painful due to a shoulder injury from military service. His first words once he was in the car were, “Fucking piece-of-shit pedophile.” He went on to describe the emergency care provided to Fosler as a “waste of money”: “Don’t waste your time. He’s a pedophile,” Spencer called to the paramedics. “You got the calls, you got the report, yet I’m the only one in handcuffs.” Over the course of the next two hours, which were captured on video, Spencer prayed, complained about his foot and how the car felt “like a coffin,” and struggled against the cuffs.At one point, he began to scream and kick the door. He demanded to see his daughter. In bodycam footage, the officers appear extremely confused. Ingram insisted to officers on the scene that Spencer was “trained” and uniquely dangerous. Another deputy searched both Spencer’s daughter and his then-18-year-old son—who had arrived on the scene after the shooting—at gunpoint. “Please don’t shoot my dad,” his son cried. Upon hearing that Fosler had died, Spencer told the officers, “Evil is hard to kill, but it’s not impossible.” Spencer was charged with first-degree murder, at first, but was quickly released on bond.In November, the district attorney revised the charges to second-degree murder. From the beginning, Spencer’s case was a cause célèbre. People across the country flocked to donate to his defense. Gun Owners of Arkansas wrote about Spencer’s case on their website and fundraised for him, helping him secure an experienced defense attorney. “This incident is the newest example of ‘use of deadly force’ that we believe to be justified,” they wrote. Judge Elmore, the same judge who had presided over Fosler’s case, was initially assigned to Spencer’s case and implemented a gag order barring anyone from speaking to the press. The case was moved to a different judge, Special Circuit Court Judge Ralph Wilson, and the gag order was lifted. This was around the time Spencer began his campaign for sheriff. Arkansas does not allow people with felony convictions to become sheriff, but Spencer could run for office pending trial. Spencer won the Republican primary for sheriff with over 53 percent of the vote in March. The looming trial of a candidate for sheriff threatened to be a spectacle with little precedent. Then something stunning happened: In June, the entire case was dismissed with prejudice. It turned out that the same sheriff’s office Spencer was seeking to lead had botched the investigation of his case so badly that it was unsalvageable. In a scathing order, Judge Wilson blamed the office for a series of errors, either intentional or incredibly stupid, writing that the misconduct was “so egregious” there was no option other than to dismiss the case. Now, here Spencer was, no pending trial, in the midst of a general election campaign at the county fair. “Why are we not having this accountability within our judicial system in order to protect the community?” he asked me, again alluding to his case. “One of the things they tried in the beginning was saying I was a vigilante. I ‘went out and murdered this guy,’ and this and that. And it was more I got stuck in a self-defense situation because the system had failed to do their appropriate job in protecting the community.” Whether or not you believe the former, the latter seems undoubtedly true. At his table, as more people streamed through, the candidate opened bags of candy and poured them into a bowl. “This is a safe place for kids to get candy,” he told one parent. We all chuckled awkwardly. If everyone, even Spencer’s detractors, agreed on something, it was that John Staley, the outgoing sheriff, who has been in office since 2012, had ruined the department. A few people I spoke with mentioned the 2021 shooting death of a teenager named Hunter Brittain, who was test-driving a pickup truck he had been fixing up when Deputy Michael Davis pulled him over and shot him in the neck. (Davis claimed it appeared that Brittain was reaching for a rifle.) Brittain’s death became national news, and his family hired lawyers Devon Jacob and Benjamin Crump, who had previously represented the family of George Floyd. Davis was convicted of negligent homicide and was sentenced to a year in jail. The sheriff has also been accused of fostering an environment in which deputies in the jail sexually assaulted women detainees as a regular practice, and then retaliated against women who complained. According to a civil complaint, women were routinely sexually assaulted and asked to perform sexual favors in exchange for better treatment in the jail. In one chilling anecdote from the lawsuit, a deputy sexually assaulted a woman and then broadcast the audio over the intercom system. Three deputies were fired and two were convicted of felonies in 2024, and they received probation and community service, no prison time. Through both controversies, the sheriff remained in office. The final nail in the coffin for Staley appears to have been the misconduct in Spencer’s case. On the night of the shooting, Fosler had a dashcam in his truck that, according to Spencer’s attorneys, would have recorded the final moments before the shooting. But, in a strange twist, the SIM card from the dashcam was not properly logged into evidence and went missing. It was never found. This was the proximate cause for the prosecution’s dismissal. (Multiple attempts to contact the sheriff’s and prosecutor’s offices for comment went unreturned.) Spencer’s opponent in the sheriff’s race, Larry Benhke, a certified yoga instructor and former Little Rock police sergeant, was in a booth on the other side of room at the county fair. He has been running as an independent, and his signs and T-shirts carried the slogan “Proven Leader. Real Solutions.” Benhke and Spencer studiously avoided each other like divorced parents at a wedding. But even Benhke had nothing good to say about Staley. “One of the reasons I ran for sheriff was because [that investigation] was not properly conducted. The sheriff’s office does not have the resources and the training to conduct a homicide investigation.” He pointed out that the sheriff’s investigators may have thought the case was a “slam dunk”—Spencer had confessed to shooting Fosler on the scene—and failed to acknowledge that they were not trained to properly process evidence in this kind of case, where justified self-defense was a plausible possibility under Arkansas law depending on what that missing dashcam footage showed. “I will tell you this: Nobody’s ever going to know what happened because they bungled the investigation,” Benhke said. After Spencer’s case ended, county prosecutor Chuck Graham gave an interview and asked rhetorically, “Do you really want to live in a society where people just go and kill somebody like the Old West?” Similarly, if less provocatively, Benhke, who is running as the coolheaded, experienced lawman, emphasized the need to discourage vigilante violence and educate residents about how policing works, or ought to work. Due process, in other words. This was not what had happened in Fosler’s case. “I hear it all the time: Dead pedophiles don’t reoffend,” he said. “That’s not a plan.” There is, in fact, a huge rift in the community about whether Spencer is qualified or emotionally stable enough to run an office as troubled as the sheriff’s. One of his detractors is a man named Dean Davis, the second chair of the Lonoke GOP. Davis readily admitted that he did not vote for Spencer in the primary and did not think he was qualified. “Aaron’s a good man. Aaron likes guns. This is not a training.That’s not a sheriff,” he said. On Saturday evening the weekend of the fair, a community group tried to organize a debate between Behnke and Spencer, but Spencer declined. Instead, Behnke answered questions for two hours in a small fire station. I saw Davis there, sitting stone-faced in the back. Behnke made a compelling case for better policing, but the people I met who supported Spencer wanted something different. “People get involved and they get attached to the emotion and the personality and the aura and whatever,” Behnke said when I ran into him at the fair. “That’s just, I guess, politics.” The man next to me at the debate grumbled that he thought it was unfair for Spencer not to show his face at a public event. Spencer has been selective with his campaign events. He spoke to the local gun-rights group. He did an interview with Chris Hansen, of To Catch a Predator infamy, for Hansen’s YouTube channel. My emails to Spencer’s campaign went unanswered; eventually his lawyer emailed me saying Spencer had “other commitments.” But when I spoke to Spencer over the course of standing around his table at the fair for an hour, he did have reasonable answers to questions about how he would run the office. I asked how he would respond to critiques of being inexperienced. “I don’t feel concerned at all,” he said. He opposes Flock cameras. “They used it in Australia during COVID. They were putting people in, I think they call them ‘safety camps,’ ” he said, referring that country’s heavily surveilled quarantine compounds. I asked Spencer what most people wanted to know when they come up to him at events. “What happened,” he said. “I don’t even get questions. They just make statements.” “You’re a hero,” a candidate sitting nearby interjected. “I’d have done what you done.” The events leading up to Fosler’s killing—or “what happened,” as Spencer framed it—hang over the entire election, even though both candidates say that they do not want to talk about it. Yet it’s impossible to avoid. And, as Spencer pointed out to me, it was very personal to many people. “I think there’s a lot of teenagers and kids that have that: ‘I hope my dad is willing to save me if something like that happens,’ ” he said. “And ‘What’s going to happen to my dad if he has to?’ ” Evenings at the county fair are for rodeo. First, everyone stood for a very long prayer as well as a somewhat embellished tale of how Francis Scott Key came to write the national anthem. Rodeo queens—contestants in a pageant focused on horsemanship—were introduced, galloping around the ring in a blur of red, white, and blue sparkles. Afterward, the contestants walking among the crowd handing out little American flags and bubbles to the many children who had come to watch. Little girls stared agog at all the glitter. Then the rest of the entertainment commenced—bull-riding, roping, and other horseback tricks. Men tumbled to the ground, and then got up again. The announcer asked us to applaud for everyone, even those who didn’t perform very well; you could not predict what animals might do. A young girl near me clung to the fence in excitement, wearing pink fringed cowboy boots. In the course of talking to Spencer, I met Walt Lanehart, a self-described “country redneck” running for justice of the peace on the GOP ticket. “I like to go out and blow stuff up in the backyard for fun,” Lanehart told me. In Arkansas, a justice of the peace is akin to a city council member in other jurisdictions. Lanehart is a fireman. “I support everything that Aaron did,” he said. “I mean, I don’t have any kids or a wife, but I’d have done the same thing. I’ve got nieces and nephews. I’d done it for them too. I told Aaron, I said, ‘You didn’t do anything.’ ” But he had to admit there had to be a toll on Spencer’s mental and emotional health. “I know it messed him up a little bit,” Lanehart said. “You’re executing a human being, and that’s not normal.” After his case was dismissed and ended for good, with the district attorney declining to appeal, Spencer issued a statement about “getting back to a normal life.” It’s not clear, however, that this has been possible. A few people I spoke with wondered why he chose to run for sheriff at this moment, rather than wait. Most of the Spencer supporters appeared to identify with his family on a deep, emotional level. I would have done the same thing, or a similar sentiment, was the most common refrain I heard. Implicit in those words, however, is also the acknowledgement of shared trauma. Behnke’s wife, Julie Eckert, a retired sex crimes detective with a pixie cut and funky glasses, told me that dealing with the fallout of a major child abuse scandal is, tragically, not new for this community. Lonoke was home to Jack Walls, one of the worst abusers connected to the Boy Scouts of America. He was, per the local news, a pillar of the community, Lonoke’s “Man of the Year” multiple times, and a Boy Scout troop leader who groomed his victims for two decades and abused an estimated 150 boys. Walls took the boys camping and shooting, plied them with booze and pornography, and abused them. He owned many, many guns, something all his victims knew. He was finally arrested in 1997 after his teenage nephew and victim, Wade Knox, confronted him at gunpoint. A court convicted Walls in 1998 of six counts of rape and he received three life sentences. A full accounting of his victims has never occurred. Walls died in prison last year. Years later, Knox killed himself, and his family opened the Wade Knox Child Advocacy Center in Lonoke. This is now where victims of child sexual abuse, like Spencer’s daughter, are interviewed. Another of Walls’ victims, Heath Stocks, is currently serving three consecutive life sentences for killing his parents and sister. Just a few days before the murders in 1997, Stocks had told his mother that Walls had been raping him for years. Stocks then told Walls about the conversation with his mother, and the Scout leader encouraged the scout to “kill” the problem. He did. At Stocks’ trial, the decades of abuse he endured was not considered adequately. Courts have refused to reconsider his sentence, and the governor has rejected clemency. He remains in prison without much hope of release even though he had been abused by Walls for over a decade, beginning at the age of 9. This history is what led Eckert to suggest that the entire community is genuinely in the thrall of intergenerational trauma. It’s only been magnified by a feeling that law enforcement—the judges, the district attorney, the sheriff—were protecting the wrong people while children were harmed. A middle-aged woman with cropped hair took multiple yard signs from Spencer’s table and told me that she was a former employee of the Lonoke County Sheriff’s Office. When I asked why she supported Spencer, she said, “More people should stand up for their children, to get sex predators. That’s the main reason why. And he was unjustly treated in Lonoke County, as are a lot of other people.” She continued, “He didn’t do anything any other parent wouldn’t do.” Another woman, who arrived with her daughters and granddaughters, all with the same round faces and wide eyes, told me, “If I was him, I would have shot that motherfucker, too.” One of the most haunting conversations I had was with a teen with long hair and face piercings who told me they supported Spencer. “He does not agree with child predators,” they told me hesitantly, going on to explain that the community continued to have a real problem. “Plenty of my friends have their stories. There are literally apps, and there are child predators all over the county. It’s horrible, and people just need to be stricter on it.” They told me that the deep sense of corruption in the current sheriff’s office made victims fearful of reporting. I asked Eckert what she thought about the people who supported Spencer. She said she understood, but thought there had to be a better way. “You can’t shoot them all,” she said. “It’s not a good strategy.” When I spoke to Spencer, he mentioned the people who approached him with their own stories of sexual abuse and their feelings of not being taken seriously. “Some places don’t have somebody who’s standing up and bringing it to everyone’s attention,” he said. And, then, as if reflecting on his own experience, he added, “And then what kind of society are we creating when we can’t trust our police to call them out, to help them?” The election on Nov. 3 will determine whether Lonoke County will put a very different kind of sheriff in office. Perhaps such a change would uncover more cases of abuse here. What would happen after is a question no one could answer. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Did Ilhan Omar Commit Immigration Fraud? Or Is This an Escalation of Trump Prosecuting His Enemies? Let’s Consider the Evidence.
2026-10-13

Did Ilhan Omar Commit Immigration Fraud? Or Is This an Escalation of Trump Prosecuting His Enemies? Let’s Consider the Evidence.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. In the Venn diagram of Donald Trump’s least favorite groups, there is perhaps nobody closer to the middle than Rep. Ilhan Omar. The lawmaker from Minnesota is a progressive, an outspoken critic of Trump, a woman, a Muslim, and an immigrant. Specifically, she’s Somali American, part of a group Trump has targeted virulently. (Among many examples, Trump last week posted on Truth Social that Minnesota should “fight back” against its Somali population.) Trump has repeatedly attacked Omar and suggested she belongs in neither Congress nor the country. That made it all the more alarming when—three weeks before Election Day—federal agents entered the sitting lawmaker’s home as part of what the Justice Department called an “ongoing investigation.” Is this yet another example, even an escalation, of Trump’s efforts to use federal law enforcement to harass his political opponents? Or is there some legitimacy to this investigation? Let’s get into what we know and what we don’t. What were federal agents looking for in Omar’s home? Early Monday morning, agents from Homeland Security Investigations, part of the Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency, surrounded Omar’s home and entered the premises, then left with boxes and other items. The Justice Department said only that the search was related to an ongoing investigation and that the department had obtained a warrant from a federal judge. But ICE’s involvement suggests the issue centers on potential violations of immigration law, as do past statements from multiple members of the administration, who’ve publicly accused Omar or her family of violating immigration law. In September, Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said: “There may be more to this, the whole family migration and possibly some illegal activity for them to come to the United States to begin with,” Mullin said. “All of this we are investigating.” More specifically, CBS news reported Monday that the investigation is focused on Omar’s original immigration paperwork. Is there any evidence that Omar’s citizenship is fraudulent? Omar’s family was granted asylum in the United States in 1995, having spent years in Kenyan refugee camps after emigrating from Somalia due to the country’s civil war. Omar became a U.S. citizen in 2000, under a law that makes minors eligible for citizenship when their parents are naturalized. Omar’s age in 2000 is at the crux of one set of accusations about her citizenship. Omar’s biography lists her as being born on Oct. 4, 1982, but critics claim—without proof—that she was actually born earlier. Omar says she no longer has her birth certificate, and records with the Somali government are unsurprisingly unavailable, given the country’s decades of internal turmoil. Didn’t the Justice Department also investigate Omar during Joe Biden’s presidency? The New York Times reports that in 2024, the department looked at her finances, campaign spending, and “interactions with a foreign citizen.” The investigation stalled out without charges, and we know about it mostly because Trump publicized it in January during ICE’s violent escapades in Minneapolis. So is Monday’s raid part of a political prosecution, or could it be a legitimate inquiry?Taking the least generous position possible toward Omar is illustrative, so let’s suppose for a moment that the critics are correct and that her family gave false information while securing her asylum or citizenship. Omar was a child or preteen during the asylum process and a teenager during the citizenship process. It’s unlikely that, even if fraud occurred, she was the driving force behind any of it. If—and again, that’s far from proven—fraud occurred, it was more likely conducted by her father as he tried to ensure his daughter would not be sent back to the violence and chaos that had engulfed Somalia. Further, if that fraud occurred, it happened more than 25 years ago. At some point, we may see the administration’s full case against Omar, which will give us more information to assess whether there’s any legitimate law enforcement going on here. But here’s what we know right now: The president has put the Justice Department under his personal control, promised to use it to attack his political enemies, and removed top officials who fail to completely acquiesce to his whims. He has made false claims about Omar, and now, decades after these alleged crimes but weeks before Election Day, his administration has staged a high-profile search of her home. On balance, this looks like an alarming escalation of Trump’s abuse of power, and the burden is on his administration to prove otherwise. That was a bit bleak, but obsessing over it won’t change anything. Here are some ways to reset and reenergize, courtesy of my colleagues. Some unironic astrology: To celebrate Slate’s 30th birthday, Slate writer Heather Schwedel asked several astrologers to interpret the magazine’s birth chart. Apparently, the stars can provide a lot of insight into our publication’s essence—and future. In praise of the secret supper club: By design, hidden restaurants tend to fly under the radar. But if you can get inside one, you’re in for a treat. Your meal might involve entering a whimsical Italian restaurant through a bathroom, or eating a 10-course menu of fine Korean food at a former underground barbershop. Noah Lederman sheds light on this unique culinary trend. The end of articles about “the end of reading”: Countless pundits, teachers, and researchers have warned that we are in the middle of a literacy crisis. With yet another treatise on this topic having been published, Slate book critic Laura Miller declares that it’s time for the discourse to evolve. Ten years of the “pussyhat”: One of the most memorable—and controversial—symbols of the resistance to Trump’s first term in office was a knitted pink hat made to look like cat ears. The pussyhat was intended as an outlet for feminist knitters to express their politics, but it soon became associated with “performative activism that is, at best, cringe, and at worst, intentionally exclusionary.” ICYMI host Kate Lindsay unravels the hat’s legacy. Thanks all from us. Thanks for reading and have a great night. Ian will be back tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Trump Is Spitting on Lindsey Graham’s Grave
2026-10-13

Trump Is Spitting on Lindsey Graham’s Grave

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. If it’s possible for a dead man to roll over in his grave, Lindsey Graham must be doing just that. In the last few years of life, he sold his soul, abandoned his principles, and betrayed his best friend, all for the sake of getting close to Donald Trump and swaying him to embrace Ukraine and punish Russia. Yet now, after a few months of resting in peace, assured that the bootlicking had been worth it, he sees the president emphatically embracing Russia and punishing Ukraine, so intently that he probably had his fingers crossed all along. Back on Earth, among the living, Graham’s former Republican colleagues should learn the lesson while they can. It’s farcical to believe that cuddling up to this president will earn them favors down the line. Trump will push them under the bus on a whim, or—if they’ve already shuffled off this mortal coil, as Graham did in July at the age of 71—he’ll spit on their tombstones. Not long after Graham died, a bipartisan majority of both houses passed a bill stiffening sanctions against Russia for its continued aggression and war crimes in Ukraine. They named the legislation after Graham, and Trump signed it. But this past Friday, Trump announced that he’d struck a deal in which Vladimir Putin sells 300,000 metric tons of Russian diesel fuel to the United States and any other country that wants it—a deal that violates not only the Graham bill but also a bill, passed soon after the 2022 invasion of Ukraine, barring the import of Russian energy supplies. Trump’s goal in striking the deal was to lower fuel prices, especially for truckers and farmers reliant on diesel fuel, and thus win back their support in the upcoming midterm elections. (It’s unlikely prices will decline quickly enough to make much difference, but Trump is desperate to try.) Speaking to reporters afterward, Trump not only publicly thanked Putin for the gesture but condemned Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, citing his attacks on Russian oil and gas facilities as the cause of high oil prices—when, in fact, the main cause is Trump’s war in Iran. He even called on Ukrainians to “get a new president.” Graham should be spinning in his grave at double speed, since he not only touted Zelensky but supported Trump’s war—even urged the president to hammer Iran hard. (The world reaps what Graham sowed.) Trump may not get away with the diesel deal. It is, after all, illegal, and while that hasn’t stopped him from doing dreadful things in the past, he has this time aroused opposition, even fury, from not only Democrats but a fair number of Republicans. The Wall Street Journal editorial page, normally an agile apologist for the president, denounced “Trump’s Diesel Insult to Lindsey Graham.” Maybe the resistance will swell with unlikely collaborators. The larger question, though, is whether many—or any—Republicans will rethink their calculations on the costs and benefits of bending their knees so strenuously to Trump’s demands. Soon after Graham died, the New York Times’ conservative columnist Bret Stephens wrote that he’d “spent years publicly ridiculing Graham” for having “flipped from being Trump’s most brutal Republican critic to his most ingratiating minion.” Now Stephens wondered if he’d been too harsh. As a result of his toadying, Graham had worked a “steadying influence on the president,” culminating in Trump’s support of the bipartisan sanctions bill against Russia. That wouldn’t have happened, he argued, “if Graham had stuck to his anti-Trump guns, keeping up a steady fire of rhetorical barbs.” Would the world be better off if Graham had stuck to his guns? “Two years ago, I would have answered … with a loud yes,” as “freedom mustn’t be secured abroad while abetting demagoguery at home.” But now Stephens had changed his mind, seeing “a nobility” in Graham’s sucking up to Trump—or, as Stephens put it, his decision not to “let his moral or intellectual self-respect get in the way of advancing” his belief in “big things,” like Ukrainian democracy. I look forward to reading Stephens’ future column re-asking the question, now that it’s clear Graham’s influence on Trump wasn’t so “steadying” after all. I hope he urges his fellow conservatives—in the media, in Congress, and inside the administration—to reconsider their own illusions about the virtues of kowtowing to this president. Cisco, Nvidia team up to build fatter, faster pluggable
2026-10-14

Cisco, Nvidia team up to build fatter, faster pluggable

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable AI datacenters are a rats nest of fiber optic cables stitching together thousands of AI accelerators, NICs, switches, and everything else required to train and run the latest and greatest modes. Cisco hopes to untangle this mess with a new pluggable optic developed in collaboration with Nvidia that will pack between four and eight times the bandwidth of existing pluggables into a single port. Announced at the Open Compute Project (OCP) global summit this week, the ultra-dense small form factor pluggable (USFP) promises to pack up to 32, 400 Gbps lanes into a pluggable roughly the size of existing OSFP modules. Each of these pluggables would boast 12.8 Tbps of Ethernet bandwidth, as much as a high-end enterprise switch just a few years ago. To put that in perspective, the most sophisticated pluggable optics today cap out at around 1.6 Tbps using eight 200 Gbps links. Such a pluggable has the potential to significantly simplify cabling across AI datacenters where it isn't uncommon for each GPU to be paired with an 800 Gbps and soon 1.6 Tbps NIC. A single USFP pluggable could consolidate eight GPUs worth of cabling into a single fiber optic bundle. Meanwhile, on the switch side, a future 204.8 Tbps switch would only require 16 USFP ports to support 128 1.6 Tbps endpoints. Cramming all that connectivity into a single pluggable won't be easy. Today a high-end pluggable optic might pull on the order of 20 watts. Cisco's USFP spec calls for up to 100 watts per pluggable. There are also design constraints that have to be taken into consideration. Four times as many links per pluggable means 4x as many electrical contacts that have to be perfectly seated. Because of this Cisco says USFP modules will employ a two phase insertion mechanism in which the pluggable is first inserted into the cage and then a second mechanism is actuated to make the electrical connection. Speaking of reliability, USFP would have a bigger blast radius than conventional pluggables. A single bad laser or digital signal processor (DSP) has the potential to take out far more than a single GPU, but entire systems. While that might sound bad, it's nothing compared to co-packed or near-package optics where one failure could drop an entire rack's worth of accelerators from the network. Nvidia's participation in the development of the USFP standard is notable as the Ethernet networking titan has largely moved away from pluggables in its most recent set of switches. Many of its Spectrum-X and Quantum-X switches now use co-packaged optics and for good reason. Cutting out the pluggables on the switch side nets a roughly 30 percent power savings. But while Nvidia's latest switches may use CPO, its NICs still rely on pluggables in part because it offers integrators greater flexibility. Not everyone is building 100,000-plus GPU clusters for training. For racks utilizing a top of rack switch, lower power direct attach copper cabling can be used without much issue. Given Nvidia's investments in CPO we can only speculate on its interests in USFP, but it likely comes down to densification. The AI infrastructure giant is rapidly moving toward 600 kW racks packing 144 GPUs on board. First teased in early 2025, Nvidia's Vera Rubin Ultra platform promised 115.2 TB/s scale out bandwidth per rack or about 3.2-6.4 Tbps of bandwidth per GPU package depending on whether they're counting bidirectional bandwidth or not. It just so happens that Cisco's initial USFP spec calls for 16, 400 Gbps links per pluggable, or about 6.4 Tbps of bandwidth. While Cisco and Nvidia may be leading the charge on the development of an ultra dense optical pluggable, like most things Ethernet, USFP will be an open standard anyone can use in their designs. Alongside Cisco's latest tie up with Nvidia, Switchzilla also revealed that Microsoft is now using its Silicon One P200 routing chips to distribute workloads across multiple datacenters. Nvidia and others have taken to calling this kind of multi-site computing: scale across networking. We discussed Cisco's datacenter-scale routing platform at length late last year, but the silicon competes directly with products like Nvidia's Spectrum-XGS and Broadcom's Jericho 4-based offerings. Cisco touts the P200 as the first 51.2 Tbps systems "deployed in a production environment by leading hyperscalers." With enough qualifiers you can be the first at anything. As we reported at the time, Microsoft was among the first major hyperscalers to embrace scale-across networking as a means of overcoming local power constraints with its Fairwater clusters in Wisconsin and Georgia. And it seems we finally know the hardware good old Redmond is using to stitch them together. Speaking of hyperscalers, one thing you probably won't find in a hyperscale network is Cisco's Nexus network operating system (NOS). Hyperscalers like Microsoft typically employ vendor agnostic NOS platforms to avoid vendor lock-in and make managing networks across multiple Ethernet vendors cheaper and easier. Originally developed by Microsoft and later open sourced, SONiC is among the most popular and widely deployed of the hyperscale NOSes. SONiC support, we should note is nothing new for Cisco which contrary to popular belief is more than willing to sell you any one piece of its networking stack, assuming you've got a big enough purse — the hyperscalers certainly do. As such Cisco is one of the biggest contributors to the SONiC project. Instead of forcing customers to use its software stack, Cisco's approach to SONiC and the hyperscale is to extend the NOSes capabilities to better take advantage of ASIC's unique capabilities and allow for integration with its services. At OCP this week, Cisco announced support for its Live Protect platform in SONiC to make it easier for customers to keep up with security patches in the wild. This capacity is expected to roll out next year. ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' US tech giants warn Europe that ditching US tech giants could cost €115B
2026-10-14

US tech giants warn Europe that ditching US tech giants could cost €115B

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans Some of America's biggest tech companies have a warning for Europe: curb your reliance on US cloud providers, and it could cost you €115 billion. That's the finding of a new study commissioned by CCIA Europe, a lobbying group whose members include Amazon, Google, and Meta, companies that have plenty of reasons to want European governments to keep buying American cloud services. The research, conducted by the European Centre for International Political Economy (ECIPE), claims the European Commission's proposed Cloud and AI Development Act (CADA) could saddle Europe with €115 billion in costs over the next decade, more than six times the Commission's upper estimate of €18 billion. The report argues that restrictions favoring European cloud providers would leave governments and businesses facing hefty bills to migrate workloads and rewrite software. It puts direct public-sector costs at €33.3 billion, with a further €80 billion potentially falling on private companies in sectors including banking, healthcare, energy, and transport. However, a rather large asterisk is attached to that €80 billion figure. It assumes the Commission extends sovereignty requirements beyond public procurement to cover 25 percent of cloud workloads in essential private-sector industries. In other words, it's a modeled scenario, not a bill Brussels has already handed European businesses. The study also comes with some baggage of its own. CCIA represents several US technology giants that dominate the European cloud market, including Amazon and Google. Both have spent years selling European customers cloud services while more recently developing sovereign cloud offerings designed to address concerns about foreign ownership, data access, and regulatory control. Amazon, for example, has been developing its AWS European Sovereign Cloud, while Google markets a range of sovereign cloud solutions. Apparently, sovereignty is an attractive proposition when it comes with a hyperscaler logo attached. Meta, another CCIA member, has its own interests in European AI infrastructure, although it is not a commercial cloud provider on the scale of AWS or Google Cloud. The report specifically targets Article 31 of the proposed legislation, which it says could allow the Commission to extend sovereignty requirements into essential private-sector services. CCIA argues that forcing businesses to move away from existing cloud infrastructure could trigger expensive software redevelopment projects, with code rewriting and re-architecting accounting for 69 percent of its estimated costs. Luxembourg would apparently take the biggest hit, with the study putting the cost at €1,711 per worker over ten years. Denmark (€905), Austria (€900), and Finland (€864) also face hefty bills, as does Ireland (€856), which hosts the European headquarters of several US tech giants. CCIA is urging EU governments to overhaul the proposed legislation, limit sovereignty restrictions to sensitive workloads, and abandon requirements based on corporate origin or personnel nationality in favor of what it calls risk-based technical safeguards. "Member States cannot rely on the Commission's incomplete estimates when deciding CADA's strict sovereignty requirements for cloud and AI providers," said Mitchell Rutledge, CCIA Europe's policy manager. "Taxpayers and businesses will foot the bill, yet the official assessment missed 70 percent of CADA's true impact." For AWS and Google, offering European customers greater control over their data also helps retain business that might otherwise migrate to European competitors. It's a rather convenient arrangement: sell customers sovereignty on one hand, while an industry association representing your interests warns governments against imposing too much of it on the other. Neither company responded to The Register's questions. Whether Europe's sovereignty ambitions will prove as expensive as CCIA predicts is unclear. What's easier to establish is that the companies warning Brussels about the cost of reducing its dependence on US technology have plenty to gain if Europe decides to keep buying American. ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?'
