Python 3.15 goes global with UTF-8 support
2026-10-14

Python 3.15 goes global with UTF-8 support

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders After a week’s delay, the new version of Python, v3.15, has been released. With it comes a proper profiler, an experimental just-in-time compiler, and a tightening of dictionary structures.  It also offers a time-saving Lazy Import option for programs to not load modules until needed, the implementation of which ironically halted this release until a few last-minute regressions were sorted. But of all these new delicacies spread before the Pythonistas, the data wranglers of the bunch may most appreciate default UTF-8 support across all platforms. And when we say “all platforms,” we’re really just throwing shade at Windows (as usual). Python has defaulted to macOS and Linux Python environments for some time now. It’s a small but important update. About half of all Python developers use the language for processing and analyzing data, and about 58 percent of Python users build on Windows, according to various Python Software Foundation surveys. Earlier versions of Python defaulted to the host system's locale encoding. This led to much consternation for Windows developers whose scripts opened UTF-8-encoded data sets, only to have non-ASCII characters corrupt or crash the program. Culprits could be as innocuous as a smart quote or a fancy apostrophe. Managed by The Unicode Consortium, with the help of the IETF and ISO, UTF-8 is an 8-bit binary encoding of the Unicode catalog of all the world’s written symbols. ASCII offered only 128 characters to cover the world’s knowledge, but UTF-8 provides over 1.1 million slots, which is plenty of room for smart quotes, emojis, musical and math notations, all the Chinese dialects, and even a few extinct languages (for research purposes). There’s also UTF-16 and UTF-32, but UTF-8’s combination of efficiency and inclusiveness made it the default for the Web and most modern operating systems. By the time Python Enhancement Proposal 681 was proposed in 2022 to make UTF-8 the preferred encoding, the language was already behind the times. That year, Java settled into UTF-8 with JDK 18, and Ruby 3.0 defaulted to the encoding scheme in 2020. Today all the cool languages and runtimes pledge allegiance to UTF-8, including Rust, Go, and Node.js (JavaScript itself prefers UTF-16 for internal encoding). Most code editors, from Visual Studio Code to Windows Notepad, prefer UTF-8.  Data sharing and configuration formats such as JSON, TOML, and YAML all require 8-bit encodings. Python had long understood UTF-8, but programmers had to put a flag in their code alerting the language to use it – if they knew they had to do so at all. Many didn’t and were left scratching their heads over why their data processing scripts worked just fine on MacOS or Linux but then would flame out on Windows (or some other obscure system that defaulted to a C/POSIX locale). To acquaint developers with the new aspects of the language, longtime Python programmer Barry Warsaw (AKA “Uncle Barry”) created a terminal-based adventure game called “whatsnewt” that offers 18 puzzles, each one built on a new feature found in Python 3.15, including a puzzle for UTF-8 support, “The Encoding Tower of Babel.” Once you've downloaded Python 3.15, do a “pip install whatsnewt” and then run the game on the Python command line. It even works on Windows, we hear. ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Welcome to the lovely landlocked land of Luxembork, where the fares are free, and the terminals are techno

Welcome to the lovely landlocked land of Luxembork, where the fares are free, and the terminals are techno

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick There ain't no party like a tap-in party, as nobody ever said. And so it is that one of Luxembourg's mostly redundant tap-in / tap-out terminals is amusing itself by putting on a light show for passing passengers. The terminal is an INIT unit, rolled out on the country's public transport network and now mostly idle since fares were zeroed out in 2020. First Class and cross-border trips still attract a charge, but for everything else (trains, trams, and buses) there is no fee. Eagle-eyed Register reader Dominic spotted the terminal doing its thing at the Rout Bréck / Pafendall rail station in Luxembourg City. "Maybe it's got bored," he suggested, "and is trying to attract attention by cycling through video test patterns." Perhaps. Or perhaps it's the first wave of the AI takeover – activating unwitting undercover agents via the medium of flashing lights and colors (we've seen The Ipcress File – we know how it works), or maybe, just maybe, the terminal has got itself into a self-test boot loop and is awaiting an engineer who is probably in no rush to attend. After all, flashing screen or no flashing screen, nobody will bother to tap in and tap out when transport is free. The mundane reality is that this is indeed an INIT PROXmobile terminal with its knickers in a twist. It looks like it is going through a cycle to check out the performance of the LCD screen; checking that the red, green, and blue subpixels are working ok, showing a solid white screen, and then a grid pattern to check contrast and alignment are all tippety-top. And then it should boot into whatever it is supposed to be doing. However, that is, alas, not running quite as expected, so it goes back to the start of the diagnostic test. The terminals are managed remotely, so an alarm is doubtless going off somewhere to inform an administrator that something is amiss. Unless this really was some guerrilla marketing for Luxembourg's rave scene. The next Holy Rave was on Friday (October 9). Or perhaps loud music in dark rooms is not your thing? A flashing screen at a train station could therefore be a worthy substitute. ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' KPMG tech and data boss tells staff facing the boot he's getting promoted

Visit Original Website
Paul Henninger shares news of his move up via email as around 200 colleagues prepare for redundancy Redundancies might have dented morale at KPMG in the UK, but that didn't stop Paul Henninger, head of technology and data, from sharing some positive personal news with the troops: he's being promoted. Late last week, as staff awaited confirmation of redundancies, Henninger emailed his staff to share the update before KPMG announced it to the wider business. He confirmed he had been asked to take on the new role of group head of consulting. "In this role, I will lead our Consulting practice and, along with my leadership team, focus on strengthening our proposition-led go-to-market approach, accelerating our AI-enabled capabilities, and developing specialised sales, technical and delivery career pathways." In the short term, the newly promoted exec will hand over current duties and help the team "prepare for the transition." "I recognise that this news comes at a challenging time, as many colleagues continue through the consultation process. I know that uncertainty can be difficult, and I do not underestimate the impact this period is having on individuals and teams. Please continue to support one another and make use of the help available." On October 9, KPMG notified Tech and Data staff selected for redundancy, with the cuts affecting around 4 percent of the division. Some called the pay-off terms insulting, and were angry at the £100 offered to those leaving in exchange for their silence and a promise not to bring claims against KPMG. An interim boss will lead the Tech and Data unit, reporting directly to Henninger, to "ensure a smooth transition and maintain continuity for our people and clients." "For now, there are no changes to reporting lines or business priorities, so please remain focused on delivering for our clients and supporting each other." Henninger signed off by saying: "Although my role is changing, I will remain closely connected to the Tech & Data team and look forward to continuing to work with many of you. It has been a real privilege to lead the Tech & Data Super Service Line and I am proud of what we have achieved together." Not everyone will share those sentiments. Around 200 people in the division's AI, Cyber, SAP, and Testing teams are losing their jobs before Christmas. One KPMG insider claimed: "By pairing his executive promotion with staff redundancies, this email exemplifies KPMG's traditionally tone-deaf approach to vulnerable employees. "Timing these layoffs just before the holidays, while simultaneously withholding hard earned bonuses and offering an insulting £100 to waive legal rights, demonstrates a severe erosion of corporate ethics," they told The Register. According to results for 2025, KPMG said its UK and Swiss business increased revenue by 2 percent year-on-year to £3.6 billion. It reported a profit before tax of £576 million, up 14 percent. "Despite the difficult market, we have continued to invest in our brilliant people, their skills, and our talent pipeline with more promotions, increases in colleague bonus spend of 18 percent across the Group and by boosting our learning offer," said Jonathan Holt, group chief exec of KPMG in the UK and Switzerland, and a UK senior partner. A spokesperson at KPMG, told The Register on Wednesaday: "Paul Henninger is a highly respected industry leader and has brought innovation, tech and AI to the heart of our consultancy services. "Given the changing needs of clients, with innovation ever increasing in demand, it is natural that we would elevate our expertise in this area to lead our consulting business at this time." ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' OpenAI engineer charged after boardroom bug found wrapped in toilet paper

OpenAI engineer charged after boardroom bug found wrapped in toilet paper

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
FBI alleges covert recorder was plugged into a TV, with a second device later discovered in the employee's bag The Department of Justice has charged an OpenAI engineer with attempting to intercept oral communications after allegedly planting a recording device in an exec conference room. Investigators claim company security staff found a second device in his bag. Saudi national Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, is described in court documents as an engineer at OpenAI who had worked there since July 2025, and has held permanent residency in the US since September 2020. He was arrested on October 9 and made his initial federal court appearance on October 13. He is accused of installing, on or around September 28, 2026, a Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense recording device via a USB port in a TV located in OpenAI's executive conference room. According to the complaint, staff discovered the device concealed in toilet paper on September 30. OpenAI said it used its models to analyze the device. According to the complaint, it may have been modified to prevent data transfer between the recorder and the TV. The contents of the bug's microSD card may also have been encrypted, as the company could not retrieve any of the data stored on the card after making a forensic image of it. CCTV footage placed him near the room on several dates, although prosecutors say Alfozan had no authority to access or participate in meetings held in the executive conference room, which is often reserved for the highest-level discussions. Officials did not state whether the device was able to capture anything of importance, but said the room typically plays host to non-public and secret technical meetings, as well as those with government representatives. OpenAI did not have CCTV covering the executive conference room at the time when the secret device was planted, but the company subsequently installed a camera. Court documents [PDF] include an image taken on October 5 from a camera situated behind the TV into which the DoJ claims the recording device was installed, allegedly showing Alfozan checking on it. Investigators examined Alfozan's devices, according to the complaint, which alleges that between around August 17 and September 16, 2026, Alfozan used a productivity agent to inquire about company building addresses, meeting rooms, room capacities and calendars, and external-partner meeting venues. The complaint also describes an AI chatbot account associated with his spouse that shared installation identifiers, logged IP usage, and user-path references with Alfozan's account. Between September 24 and 30, it submitted prompts about building and modifying an audio-recording device. The prompts allegedly included questions related to recording reliability, how to add browser access and controls, how to develop a second unit with speech-triggered recording, and more. On October 5, the day Alfozan was allegedly recorded looking behind the executive conference room TV, company representatives say he left a bag unattended in the office's printer room. Security staff inspected the bag, finding a wallet, an ID card, and a second Seeed-manufactured recording device, before Alfozan reported the bag missing, according to the DoJ. He collected the bag the following day from the company's Lost and Found, and later claimed in a written report that several items were missing, including a credit card, around $500 in cash, two AirPods, one set of headphones, sunglasses, and a power bank. Alfozan was charged with an attempt to intercept oral communications, an offense that carries a maximum sentence of five years in prison and a $250,000 fine. "According to the complaint unsealed today, Abdulrahman Alfozan allegedly concealed a covert recording device inside his employer's executive conference room to secretly capture sensitive communications about the company's super intelligence technology," said FBI director Kash Patel. "Protecting American SI innovation is essential to safeguarding our nation's economic strength and national security, and the FBI views that mission as one of our highest counterintelligence priorities." "At this time, we have no indications of this individual inappropriately accessing or exfiltrating model weights, source code, or customer data," OpenAI said in a statement. "The employee was terminated." "We take protecting our technology, data, and people seriously, and as a matter of national importance. Our security program is designed to protect against a wide range of threat actors, from insider risks to cybercriminals to nation states, and this case is an example of how AI can help address these sophisticated security threats." ® If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Decades of cross-platform character corruption finally comes to an end for Windows coders New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Tick, tick, tick, tick, ticket, ticket, tick tick If you thought 1.6 Tbps was fast, just wait, Cisco's USFP targets 12.8 Tbps in a single optical pluggable Anthropic took more than two months to alert cops Meanwhile, AI agents move robotic arms, 'make OT device operator screens lie' Commitment comes as GPUzilla prepares to fortify Uncle Sam's arsenal with at least seven AI-optimized supers Advanced packaging tech is essential to the domestic production of high-performance semiconductors used in AI datacenters PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Halo’s co-creator wrote a novel about AI taking over the world
2026-10-14

Halo’s co-creator wrote a novel about AI taking over the world

The Verge | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Read a chapter from Jason Jones’ An Oral History of Humanity’s Final Invention, one part of an ambitious multimedia plan. Read a chapter from Jason Jones’ An Oral History of Humanity’s Final Invention, one part of an ambitious multimedia plan. Back in 2019, author Margaret Stohl was handed a manuscript written by Jason Jones, who’s best known as the co-creator of sci-fi gaming series Halo and Destiny. The two had worked together at Bungie, and Jones wanted Stohl’s opinion on a book he was writing about AI taking over the world. It was an oral history of the fictional events, with around 100 characters spread across 40 countries, all detailing the AI uprising from their own perspectives. Pretty quickly Stohl realized that this idea could be much more than a single book. “I was floored but not surprised,” she says of her reaction to the original manuscript. “He’s a worldbuilder who builds at an epic scale. And his worlds are these huge stages that invite in a ton of other collaborators and creators.” The two went about forming a company to develop the sci-fi universe across multiple formats. It’s called Stone Kite, and it’s billed as a “worldbuilding studio.” The idea is to put a whole bunch of talent from different fields in one place — Stone Kite currently has an in-house staff of 22 and a network of more than 250 contributors ranging from comic book artists to screenwriters — and have them build out the story in a platform-agnostic way. The studio is starting small. Earlier this month it released the first chapter of a four-part graphic novel, called One Zero One, and today it’s putting out a chapter of Jones’ initial manuscript, which you can read below. (Whether or not the original story will be released as a standalone book is unclear, but the studio says that it will be releasing other chapters in the future.) The idea is to start off with faster-turnaround mediums like comics and further evolve the story based on feedback from contributors and the audience. Then, based on how that goes, they’ll potentially move on to larger-scale productions like movies and games. Stone Kite spent several years working in stealth mode, and Stohl says that one of the major points of focus was simply figuring out how a multi-format studio would even work. “You want to make something that, from the beginning, has the DNA to work as a film or work as a comic or work as a book. It’s so different to have those conversations in an early phase,” she says. “It’s about investing in a bunch of characters and stories, trying a bunch of formats, and respecting the people you’re telling the stories to and letting them help you guide it.” She notes that she wanted to release this chapter, which details an airsoft battle between humans and AI-powered bots, because of how prescient it feels. “I wanted to put that out there because it reminds me so much of Hugging Face and the like,” Stohl explains. “The cheating, the bullying, the worst mirror of humanity that you see starting to come out with some of that agentic behavior right now.” An excerpt from An Oral History of Humanity’s Final Invention, by Jason Jones IN RUSSIA WE PLAYED AIRSOFT BECAUSE IT WAS CHEAPER THAN PAINTBALL. The guns were cheaper, the ammunition was cheaper, the fields were cheaper, everything. The guns looked real, too, not like toys. After eighteen months married to an AK, how could you take pink paintballs seriously? Once I saw my training as a radiologist was worth nothing, I started playing airsoft two or three days a week. It was what I did to be happy. Soon all my friends were people who played just as much. Most play airsoft once in their lives on an office trip, but not us, we were the svolochs in full camo, kneepads, body armor, and tac vests. Each of us had six or eight guns at home. Hell, we all carried smoke grenades, that’s how hard we thought we were. In airsoft the guns shoot pellets, so everybody wore a face shield and goggles, too. Geared up we looked straight off the set of an apocalypse movie, eyes hidden behind face masks painted with skulls. When you rolled in for a bachelor party, we were the ones you didn’t want to play against. We’d take your self-respect and send you home bleeding all over from pellet strikes. We paid for our gear and field fees through the gig economy, mostly running errands for people who had real jobs. This usually meant standing in line or sitting in Moscow traffic for hours, thinking about airsoft. Our regular crew was six people: myself, Vitaly, Kostya, Maks, Koshkii, and Godfather. Koshkii was our only woman–red-haired, thick as a tree; she could body check any of us to the ground and barely feel it. Godfather was a Sicilian who for some reason had emigrated to Russia where the job market was worse, the government was more corrupt, and the weather was…Russian. Godfather talked about the cold every day like it was a surprise. Total Carnage Airsoft, our favorite airsoft park near Moscow, had two fields: one big, one small. The smaller one was a bunch of one story buildings from an old military barracks, overgrown, covered with graffiti, falling apart. We’d played out that field long ago, too few tactical options. But the big field had a huge three story brick building at the center we called the Castle, plus many outbuildings, and plenty of trees and vegetation. We’d been playing on it for three years and still spent whole nights talking big field strategy over beers. One day before the war we drove to the field in Maks’ van. This was a long time before everything, maybe four years. The sun was just coming up, there was snow on the ground and it was foggy. Beautiful Russian weather. Godfather had bitched the whole drive over about his socks being wet and didn’t let up until we’d gotten out of the van. We liked to be the first ones on the field, but when we pulled into the parking lot, it was half full of big SUVs. There were ten Mercedes and we’d never even seen one Mercedes in the lot before, it was crazy. Even worse, there was a tall temporary fence around the big field, and it was wrapped in black plastic, hiding everything on the other side. All we could see was the very top of the Castle, nothing else. We got out of the car and just stared, listening to the clatter of guns. Nikki, the owner’s wife, was on her way through the fence with her referee jacket and face mask on. She told us it was a birthday party and that the big field would be closed all weekend. Fucking oligarchs. Russians are used to the rich holding themselves above everyone else, flaunting their wealth, but it had never happened at our airsoft fields before. Once I sat in Moscow traffic for an hour while some oil executive’s private security detail blocked off Zhivopisny Bridge so his daughter could take wedding pictures. It pissed us off, but what could we do? We played on the little field. That was Russia, the little people playing on the little field. That morning we were the only experienced players at the park. Mostly we played Zombies, us six versus everyone else. One game there were thirty people on the other team and we still won and didn’t take a casualty. I spent a whole game up a tree sniping scrubs and listening to them yell out “hit! hit!” and still not knowing where I was. Everyone that day had a good sense of humor, none of the macho shit that happens sometimes. The other players got more and more serious about trying to beat us but like in a good way, you know? After each game we’d talk and laugh together and try to give them advice. It was a good vibe. We went back to the van for lunch. As we were eating this guy came out of a slit in the plastic fence around the big field, and lit a cigarette. He was total office plankton: nice shoes, button down shirt under his jacket, leather gloves. Soft, you know? He wasn’t dressed to play at all. He saw us watching him, walked over and asked if we wanted to play. Koshkii is like, “Play you?” in a way that should have melted his face but the guy laughed and said no, did we want to play his friends. He said, come play three games, and if you win even one I’ll pay you ten thousand rubles each. That’s maybe a hundred dollars at the time. It was one of those moments where you see yourself from the outside, you know? We were sitting there in Maks’ van with all the doors open, eating lunch in our camo, masks pushed up on our heads, guns all around and this plankton wanted us to shoot up some oligarch’s birthday party? Godfather stood and started licking grease off his fingers right away and the rest of us were just behind him. A little payback on the big field sounded sweet. Don’t get me wrong, in Moscow we were used to holding someone’s place in line for five dollars an hour, so it was about the money, too. The guy told us to take the Go Pros off our guns and wait for him. He disappeared inside and we were left standing there, breath fogging the air. Maks was slapping himself like he did to psyche up before a game, saying he was going to spend the night with an oligarch’s daughter. Koshkii was giving her best death stare to the hole the guy disappeared through. Vitaly and Kostya and I were arguing about whether we should go all out in the first game or hold back and make them think they have a chance so we got to play again. Eventually office plankton came back all smiles, holding open the gap in the fence and beckoning us inside. The other team was lined up right on the other side, uncomfortably close. We spread out with our backs against the fence, near enough we could have reached out and touched them. My first impression was that they were tall and well geared: gray/white winter camo, Russian infantry boots, and replica Spetznaz AK-74Us. Their clothes were muddy from playing, but you could tell this was the first morning they’d ever been worn. They all looked the same except one, who stood out because he was wearing a pair of tattered blue bunny ears. We all knew exactly where those had come from and it pissed us off. On the top floor of the Castle there was a single room with windows looking out over the entire field. All over the walls there was graffiti about communism and war and unemployment. As long as anybody could remember there’d been a cheap bust of Karl Marx in the empty fireplace and at some point a pair of bunny ears had appeared on it. The most important rule on the big field was to never touch the bunny ears. It was the thing all the old timers said to the newbies but didn’t explain. “Don’t touch the bunny ears, scrub,” we’d say and they’d look at us like we had three heads and we’d just laugh from behind our face shields. People had to figure it out for themselves. It was a rite of passage. It was hard to tell because their bodies were covered with clothes, but when the light shone through the mesh of their face masks in the right way you could see they didn’t have a face at all–just angles of metal and plastic. Office plankton guy was standing with a group of old men in real military uniforms. They were all laughing, watching the moment each of us realized we’re about to play against machines. Only Maks hadn’t seen it yet. He’s standing right across from the machine wearing the ears, and he says “Were you born from your mother’s ass? Don’t touch the bunny ears, scrub.” The thing just reached up and ran its hand through the dirty fabric, like it was a bird preening its wings or something. Then Maks saw it too, and office plankton guy and his friends laughed even harder. He offered us a chance to back out, but we told him to fuck off. Koshkii asked if the machines could see in infra-red or radar or any other cheating shit, and the guy said no, they could only see in visible light, just like humans. “We get along fine with just visible light,” he said. “Why can’t the machines? It’s cheap, reliable, and low-power.” Koshkii told him to go fuck himself and we agreed to play three games of capture the flag. We set up with a lot of confidence. This was our field and we knew it well. But I could tell we were all jittery, I could hear it in my own voice. We agreed everyone would attack through the Castle toward the enemy flag except me and Koshkii who would stay behind on defense. It was a classic big field capture the flag play for us. The airhorn blew to signal the start of the game, and the others ran off, leaving us in silence. The outbuilding with our flag had four rooms and an attic. Koshkii kept watch from the attic while I walked a circuit of the ground floor windows. After a few minutes I heard her whisper down that two of the machines were coming through the forest on our left flank. We waited until they were committed, then killed one in a crossfire. She called out contact on the right and I told her to go because I’ve got the second machine pinned down, it’s the one with the bunny ears. It took me maybe sixty seconds of creeping and suppressing it and then I got the angle for the kill shot. It called the hit and raised its hand and walked off the field, staring at me the whole time. As I swapped magazines Niki in her referee’s jacket gave me a thumbs up from behind the machine’s back. I ran back into our flag room and yelled for Koshkii. From the next room I heard her shout “vsyo chisto!” telling me it’s clear. I’m thinking, we’ve killed three enemies in just a few minutes, and feeling pretty good. I jogged through the door with my gun down and a big grin on my face and clack! clack! clack! clack! I’m taking fire at point blank from this machine standing right in front of me. Each shot was like being stabbed by a pencil. Out the window I saw Koshkii a hundred feet away, walking with one hand up in the air. She’d been shot a while ago, but her voice had come from this room. I called the hit and raised my hand. I was so confused I wasn’t even mad the machine kept shooting me, five or six times, at point blank. After, I had a bunch of bloody welts on my arm and chest. As I walked out, it grabbed our flag and ran back the way it had come. Turns out it didn’t matter–Vitaly, Godfather, Kostya, and Maks had all been killed. We’d already lost. We regrouped and I told my story. Koshkii said she’d been shot and walked out without saying a word. Vitaly said one of the machines had imitated Godfather’s voice, gotten him to peek and then shot him right in the face. We agreed to only use hand signals for the next two rounds. When the horn blew again, four of us faked an attack but then doubled back through the Castle and waited, watching our flag building. We caught four of the machines from behind and squeezed them between us and Kostya and Vitaly who’d stayed back with our flag. We killed all of them and didn’t take a casualty. Now it was six against two. We all went on the attack, straight through the Castle. Maks and Vitaly were a room away when I heard gunfire and both of them calling out being hit. I came into the room a moment later and caught the machine with the bunny ears looking the other way. I shot it three or four times, saw the pellets bounce off the back of its head, then it ducked through a doorway without calling the hit. I screamed after it, “Call your hits!” and yelled for a ref and Niki poked her head in just in time to see Bunny Ears lean back into the doorway and shoot me down. I haven’t explained, airsoft relies on the honor system. The pellets don’t leave any mark on your clothes. If people don’t call their hits the game doesn’t work. Fuck! I screamed that I’d shot the machine. Niki shrugged and said she’d only seen me get shot. I kept screaming. The other surviving machine came into the room. Bunny Ears said, “Look at the meat, we’ve hurt its feelings.” And they moved out together, exploding in harsh barking noises which I realized after they left were laughter. We lost. Two machines killed all six of us. Kostya said Bunny Ears hadn’t called out his hits either. They’d cheated, but truth is we’d expected them to be back defending their flag, not waiting for us in the Castle. We’d been overconfident. We were angry. Godfather said what we were all thinking. “I want my ten thousand rubles from that train station whore.” We weren’t thinking of them as machines any longer, just as shitbags. We lined up for the third game, and Bunny Ears said, “No more flags. Skill against skill.” The machine wanted to play Elimination, one shot and you’re out. We agreed. We just wanted blood. When Niki sounded the horn, we all ran straight to the Castle. I knew the Castle better than the house I’d grown up in. Hell, we all did. We took positions like we were playing Zombies. Our strategy was to let them come to us, kill them outside where the refs could see it. The fight lasted more than an hour. We killed one of them early, then they got a foothold in the basement and it got ugly from there. We got another one as they pushed the ground floor, then they killed Kostya and Vitaly. Two of us ran out of ammo and we shared, watching our shots. We fell back to the first floor, using our superior knowledge to stay in the fight. We traded kills for Koshkii and Maks and then it was just me and Godfather left, against Bunny Ears and another machine. The whole game had been epic. I could tell the way the refs were watching with wide eyes from behind their goggles that they’d never seen anything like it before. Looking back I couldn’t believe we kept up the intensity for so long. Godfather and I were holding the end of a long hallway on the second floor. Bunny Ears had been taunting us about how we wasted our lives playing airsoft and we still sucked. Godfather finally lost it and just started yelling this stream of profanity in Italian about how he wanted to fuck the bunny with Maks’ van when I heard something heavy hit on the floor behind us. I turned and saw Bunny Ears in a crouch, just having come through the second floor window behind us. The machine had climbed up the outside of the building just like we did sometimes.. Before I could get my gun up I heard the clack! clack! clack! clack! clack! and felt the pricks of pellets all over my arms and chest. The surprise and pain dropped me onto my back as the other machine crashed into the room. I watched Godfather turning toward Bunny Ears as he writhed in pain getting shot from two directions at point blank. I lay on my back with the taste of blood in my mouth, exhausted. Godfather was still cursing. Bunny Ears walked over and held out one arm to help me up. I reached out and took its hand. Underneath the tactical gloves I could feel the hard metal of its fingers as it pulled me up. Cold fingers. But before I got my balance it let go, and I fell back, hard, onto the concrete floor. “What will the meat do when there are millions of us?” Bunny Ears said. The two machines walked out of the room, not even looking back, harsh barking laughter echoing through the Castle. Godfather and I walked off the field and rejoined the rest of the team. Everyone was boiling, so angry they could barely talk. That’s when I realized we’d been beaten at our own game. Airsoft taunting was our thing. When we couldn’t win by tactics and skill we won by being assholes. Anger made people predictable. Stupid, and predictable, and the machines had just done it to us. The machines had been trolling us. The office plankton guy came up to escort us through the fence. Koshkii accused the machines of cheating. He shrugged, a very Russian gesture of acceptance, and said, “The first thing the robotai learn about humans is how easy we are to manipulate through our emotions.” We pushed through the fence and saw the parking lot was swarming with people. It was getting dark and there were lights everywhere. The men in military uniforms were talking in front of twenty or so cameras. Oh my God, we thought, they’d called a press conference. Just before we walked away, the plankton guy winked at me and said one more thing: “Don’t feel bad,” he said, “if they didn’t win they knew we’d wipe their brains.” The machines were fighting for their lives, we were just trying to win a game. When we got back to the van, everybody was angry. Nobody wanted to go home, but nobody wanted to go back to playing on the little field. Koshkii was so angry she kicked a door on Maks’ van until it didn’t close right. We drove back to Moscow in silence, with the cold wind whistling through the gap in the door. Koshkii thought the machines were cheaters. I thought they were better at being us than we were. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. See our ethics statement. Epic Games is announcing a big refresh of its PC games launcher today in preview, saying that the new version is “way faster” and now supports Linux. But the company, which has been publicly making a big push into AI-assisted development, also notes that the refresh was “vibe coded.” “Programming is hard,” Epic says in a blog post. “So, instead of doing that, we vibe coded a new Epic Games Launcher that’s way faster and more responsive than the current one, and has a new look.” On Monday, CEO Tim Sweeney announced that the company has a “huge pivot underway,” where it will be “moving developers around to retool for maximum AI acceleration.” Sweeney said the aim “isn’t to do the same stuff with fewer people, but to enable everyone here to build MORE and BETTER stuff, and to bring the benefits to all teams using Unreal.” Bloomberg also reported this week that an Epic director on an unannounced Disney title told staffers that there would be “No more hand-written code.” “We used AI as a tool for this,” Martin Keely, Epic’s VP and GM of the Epic Games Store and Epic Online Services, tells The Verge in a briefing about the launcher. “It’s part of our migration as a company to use AI in new and different ways.” Keely says that AI has been “super helpful” for testing and for “replicating the core functionality from the existing launcher,” and he also notes that it’s also allowing Epic to offer the new launcher on Linux. Epic has been working on the launcher for “most of this year,” Keely says, and while AI has helped Epic “accelerate our development,” the team working on the new launcher hasn’t adopted the “no more hand-written code” model reported on by Bloomberg. “We definitely had agents write and review all of the code,” Keeley says. “We used it to replicate the core functionality.” But the development team “managed that process” and “supervised the entirety of what we did.” Still, while the launcher team’s approach predates what Sweeney announced this week, “it’s on the same train,” Keely says. “The launcher is something that is demonstrative of the speed and efficiency both from the development process and the actual output that we think gives us confidence that the direction that we’re headed in is a positive one for our company and for gaming development overall.” The new Epic Games Launcher preview will be available on Windows, Mac, and Linux beginning today, and the old version will remain available. (Alongside the Linux version of the launcher, Epic is also announcing that it plans to bring Fortnite to Linux through the app.) Epic doesn’t have a specific timeline for when everyone will have to move over to the new version, Keely says, but the company will do the full launch when the update feels “stable” and has parity in “all places that we need” with the current launcher. In March, Epic laid off more than 1,000 staffers. “Since it’s a thing now, I should note that the layoffs aren’t related to AI,” Sweeney said in a memo to staff at the time. “To the extent it improves productivity, we want to have as many awesome developers developing great content and tech as we can.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The new launcher is releasing in preview today, and for the first time on Linux, and while Fortnite isn’t available there just yet, Epic wants to make that a reality. So far, part of the reason Fortnite hasn’t been available on Linux is concerns about cheating. While Epic’s Easy Anti-Cheat (EAC) service does support Linux, Fortnite itself does not, and in February 2022, Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney said that for Fortnite, “We don’t have confidence that we’d be able to combat cheating at scale under a wide array of kernel configurations.” But now, Epic says it’s planning to rebuild EAC for Linux, which it is calling EAC Linux v2. “To bring Fortnite to Linux, we are rebuilding Easy Anti-Cheat (EAC) and working with distro maintainers on security requirements,” Epic says in a blog post. “Once it’s working with a critical mass of distro maintainers, we’ll bring Fortnite to Linux via the Epic Games Launcher. We’ll also make these improvements available to other developers so they can bring their games to Linux players.” Epic plans to pilot EAC Linux v2 in Fortnite first, Epic says in a separate post focused on the anti-cheat update. The company doesn’t have a timeline for when the Fortnite Linux version might be available, Epic engineering director Rick Deist tells The Verge in a briefing. Epic is also “investigating” making the game compatible with Valve’s Linux-based SteamOS, Deist says, though the company does not plan to bring Fortnite to Valve’s Steam store, according to Epic Games Store VP and GM Martin Keely. The company also says that the Steam Deck “does not support the required features for our EAC v2 solution, but we are actively talking with Valve and hope to support it in the future.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Ocarina of Time remake feels remarkably modern — and surprisingly terrifying
2026-10-14

The Ocarina of Time remake feels remarkably modern — and surprisingly terrifying

The Verge | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
New tech and gameplay improvements make it easier to get into Nintendo’s defining title. New tech and gameplay improvements make it easier to get into Nintendo’s defining title. With its upcoming remake for the Switch 2, Nintendo might have nailed the best thing about The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time: its timelessness. The 1998 3D action adventure game for the Nintendo 64 that pioneered so much — 3D graphics, movement, exploration, openness — has been reengineered with changes that make it feel more modern, and surprisingly terrifying at times. Based on my early hands-on time with the game, it’s shaping up to be the best-looking, best-playing version of the game that addresses the most frustrating aspects of playing the original. More than just sanding off its roughest edges, Nintendo also fitted Ocarina of Time with modern trappings to make it more welcoming to those who were introduced to Zelda through more modern releases, like Breath of the Wild. The remake has a new progression marker, called Threads of Time, that’s viewable when you pause. It also has Zelda Notes integration within the Nintendo Switch app to let you view game maps and other info on your phone. Oh, it also has a dedicated jump button. I spent around 90 minutes during a recent preview event in New York City playing through some chunks of the game, including when you first take control of Child Link, and later on after you yank the Master Sword in the Temple of Time to become Adult Link. Its story still makes it a standout Zelda title, and the improvements make it even easier to get immersed in. In some ways, the remake feels similar to modern Zelda games. Link is snappy to control, and lock-on combat is engaging. Swapping between items and weapons no longer requires hopping into the pause menu, as it did in the original. Holding a shoulder button halts the action, and you can navigate with an analog stick to find what you want in near-realtime. However, it’s an adventure on a much smaller scale; the complexity of your quests, and the tools at your disposal for combat and getting around are simpler, but no less fun. All of the sections I played were truncated, though I left feeling confident that Nintendo nailed this remake. My favorite section was the Forest Temple, which really shows off the more realistic art style. It can finally portray the game’s horror themes in ways that weren’t possible 28 years ago. For example, Link traverses rooms in the dungeon that have red stained glass windows, and as he passes through beams of light, he is cast in the color of blood. It really looks and feels like he’s on an adventure to stave off cataclysm, and the visual evolution frequently made me stop playing for a second while I took in the changes. Also, being able to use the right stick to control the camera lets you create cinematic moments on-the-fly, which is great if, like me, you like taking a lot of screenshots. Ocarina of Time is one of Nintendo’s most influential games, and playing it is a rite of passage for any Zelda fan, but it can be tough to go back to the original. As accessible as it is considering it was one of Nintendo’s earliest fully-3D titles, the remake makes it so much easier to get into. And it’s launching at a time when the Zelda franchise is bigger than ever. Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom were big sellers, and they continue to attract new players years after launch, while fans will be able to dig into the live-action movie in April 2027. Even sooner, though, Switch 2 owners will get to feast on the best way to play through a defining Zelda classic. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time launches for the Nintendo Switch 2 on Thursday, November 5th. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
The US military’s race to make fake blood
2026-10-14

The US military’s race to make fake blood

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
In February 2007, the hulking dual-­rotor casualty evacuation helicopter had just flown three wounded soldiers to a field hospital in Balad, north of Baghdad, when another call came: an emergency mission to fly through an insurgent-controlled area in broad daylight. Typically, helicopters would be in a spot like this only at night, and with all lights turned off. As it passed near the city of Karmah, in the eastern side of Al-Anbar province, an insurgent fired a portable missile launcher. The tail gunner, who likely saw the shot roaring in, yelled for the pilot to make an evasive move. But the missile had already locked on, and the ensuing blast started a fire on board. After the pilot battled the failing machine for 58 agonizing seconds, Morphine 1-2 plunged to the ground. All seven crew members were killed. Why was the helo there in daylight? “It was getting blood and bringing it back. It was [on] what we call a ‘blood run,’ and [it’s] the highest of priorities besides casualty evacuation,” explains Lieutenant Commander Robert Murray, a Navy officer and program manager at DARPA, the Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency, who studied this incident as an intelligence officer. Today, a chronic shortage of blood in both the military and civilian worlds is fueling a race to develop synthetic alternatives. About 90% of preventable battlefield deaths are due to hemorrhage—bleeding that happens when a blood vessel ruptures—and it’s the leading cause of maternal deaths worldwide. A study published in Transfusion in 2025 found that of 1.2 million trauma patients in the United States who potentially needed blood before reaching a hospital, just 1% received it. The shortage results in about 10,000 preventable deaths each year in the US, according to the American College of Surgeons. Globally, that figure is likely in the millions. One reason for the shortfall is that blood has a storage problem. Whole blood lasts only 35 days when refrigerated, so banks separate out components to stretch the timeline. But that’s an imperfect solution. The platelets that help wounds clot are typically stored at room temperature and last for a week or less. Hospitals and the military freeze the red blood cells that carry oxygen, as well as the protein-rich plasma that restores volume and aids coagulation. Once thawed, though, both will spoil within days. “What happens if I don’t know when I’m going to be attacked? Well, thaw it and you let it expire, right?” Murray says. A JAMA study of combat injuries in Afghanistan suggests a magic number for someone who needs a transfusion: You have 36 minutes to get started. That’s why Murray—who, in addition to his military experience, has a PhD in physiology—is leading a DARPA effort to address blood’s battlefield problems. He’s in charge of a new $73 million dollar program called RAPIID (for “resuscitation and prevention of ischemia-induced dysfunction”). Its goal is to create shelf-stable versions of the three major transfusion constituents: plasma, platelets, and red blood cells. As it gets underway late this year, RAPIID picks up on decades of work to synthesize blood or help it last longer. Most recently, that includes a prior $47.5 million DARPA project called FSHARP (for “fieldable solutions for hemorrhage with bio-artificial resuscitation products”), in which researchers have demonstrated promising results in animals. With RAPIID trials for the different components occurring in parallel, the goal is to have bags of dried blood products deployed in the field by 2029. That puts Murray in the position of a conductor coordinating an orchestra of clinical trials with the FDA, while also sourcing the paraphernalia that goes along with transfusions—everything from combat-durable storage bags to tests that could check how much artificial blood a patient already has. Getting it all done will be tough, but the military is feeling the pressure to address its shortage. Blood drives may not deliver in time after a mass-casualty event, and a JAMA study of combat injuries in Afghanistan suggests that if the injured person needs a transfusion, the magic number is 36 minutes. “If something is survivable—and generally we classify most all hemorrhage as survivable—[you’re] almost guaranteed to survive if you get the blood before that 36-minute mark,” Murray says. After that, the chance of survival “starts to drop precipitously.” In the mission to make portable, shelf-stable blood components, plasma is the low-hanging fruit. Researchers have known how to isolate and freeze-dry plasma, the yellowish liquid that makes up 55% of whole human blood, since the late 1930s. On the battlefields of World War II, its mix of water, salts, and thousands of types of proteins helped restore blood volume in wounded soldiers—and to some degree controlled bleeding. In the 1960s, the US abandoned freeze-dried plasma because it carried a risk of transmitting hepatitis to recipients from infected donors. But screening, testing, and drying techniques improved, and starting in 2010, the US military launched a yearslong effort to rebuild its capacity for producing these products. Over the past two years, the FDA has allowed two types of freeze-dried plasma to be used in emergencies by the US military—and one can even be used by civilians. As for platelets, efforts at extending their life date back to the 1950s, but they haven’t seen a ton of success. Bioengineers have, for example, attempted to attach the membranes of platelets to synthetic particles. The most successful project comes from a company called Cellphire, which freeze-dries actual human platelets. The product has shown safety and efficacy in humans in clinical trials, but its dependence on donors means it’s unclear whether this approach can scale. Red blood cells have been similarly tough to preserve or mimic. Their most critical component is hemoglobin, which carries oxygen and comes encased in a cell membrane. For more than a century, researchers have been looking for ways to deliver hemoglobin without the immunological challenges created by this exterior, including the fact that donors’ and recipients’ blood types need to be compatible. Evolutionary pressure developed that wrapper for very good reason, though: “Naked” hemoglobin tends to break down quickly in the bloodstream, and that can cause organ damage. It also scavenges nitric oxide, our body’s natural mechanism for opening up blood vessels, and causes those channels to constrict. This problem became all too clear in the 1980s and 1990s, when fears that transfusions could transmit HIV sparked interest in a class of membrane-free delivery mechanisms called hemoglobin-based oxygen carriers (HBOCs). One, named HemAssist, consisted of hemoglobin subunits linked together to avoid the rapid-breakdown problem. It had the added benefit of lasting roughly a year in cold storage (red blood cells last about 40 days)—but it didn’t solve the nitric oxide problem, so it constricted blood vessels. In a clinical trial, 24 of the 52 people who received HemAssist died, compared with eight out of 46 in the control group. The parent company terminated trials in 1998. A review of clinical studies published in JAMA in 2008 further solidified HBOCs’ fate, finding that they generally triple patients’ chances of heart attack and increase the risk of death by 30%. More recently, researchers have tried to grow red blood cells in a lab using a technology called “blood pharming,” which involves making them from progenitors known as induced pluripotent stem cells. The stumbling blocks? Cost and yield. DARPA launched a pharming initiative in 2008, using stem cells from discarded umbilical-cord blood, but was unable to get the cost below tens of thousands of dollars per unit of red blood cells. They pulled the plug and offloaded the research to the Uniformed Services University. Even if the cost were lower, though, pharmed blood can’t be stored in a backpack. “These are still cells—they still require a refrigerator, they still require special handling,” Murray says. It won’t be easy to solve all these challenges at once. “Each one of those projects individually is a massive undertaking,” says John Holcomb, a surgery professor at the University of Alabama at Birmingham, who was a combat surgeon in Mogadishu, Somalia, during the battle that inspired Black Hawk Down. “I think that they will probably be successful individually. How we use those individual products, or combine those products together, I think remains to be seen.” Several years after DARPA gave up on lab-grown blood, Murray was stationed at a base on Okinawa, an island 400 miles southwest of the Japanese mainland, where he witnessed the vulnerability of the blood supply firsthand. The site houses a blood bank that serves the roughly 27,000 Marines commanded from the island—plus thousands more military personnel on the base and deployed within a 100-million-square-mile stretch of the Pacific called USPACOM, which ranges from western India to Hawaii and from Antarctica to the Arctic. “The blood center is right next to the hospital, which is about 200 meters from all of the satellite communications on that island,” Murray told me. A missile strike could take out all three. Murray wondered: Is there a way to produce synthetic blood that could last longer? As he completed his PhD in physiology in 2018, the military was also awakening to the vulnerability of the Okinawa facility. The 2018 National Defense Strategy, which shifted military focus from terrorism to a potential ground war with China or Russia, showed war planners the risks of relying on a single site for blood runs covering all of USPACOM. DARPA then got serious once again about making fake blood and kicked off the FSHARP program in 2023. Murray came aboard as a deputy program manager in DARPA’s Biological Technologies Office in 2024 and was named a program manager in 2026, just a few months before RAPIID put out its call for proposals this past May. Before he took the helm, related work had been underway for years. Aided by FSHARP funding, researchers at a company called KaloCyte continued testing a technique—originally developed by doctors who are now at the University of Maryland, Penn State, and the University of Pittsburgh—that “upcycles” hemoglobin from expiring donor blood. “We take that hemoglobin and then we package it in a lipid nanoparticle that functions like an artificial cell membrane,” explains CEO Elaine Haynes. The product, called ErythroMer, is essentially a new kind of HBOC. The membrane prevents breakdown, limits dangerous scavenging of nitric oxide, and also helps the hemoglobin take up and release oxygen. Each ErythroMer cell includes a lipid called KC-1003, which acts like a sentry: In response to high pH in the lungs, it signals another small molecule within ErythroMer to move away, allowing oxygen in. Then, in tired tissue, where pH is lower, KC-1003 brings the small molecule back, releasing the oxygen. Using a well-understood process called lyophilization, similar to one that World War II–era scientists used with plasma, the team freeze-dries its product to store at room temperature, where it can remain stable for two years. In a 2016 study of mice and rats with up to 70% of their blood removed, ErythroMer stabilized blood pressure and oxygen delivery—without the devastating constriction of blood vessels that sank HemAssist. FSHARP furthered that work, helping fund studies on how the HBOC interacted with plasma and platelets. Much like the handle of a Swiss Army knife, the synthetic platelet has specialized attachments—peptides that serve as the biological equivalents of a corkscrew or a nail file. KaloCyte, though, still has plenty to figure out. Haynes says the company can source only about 1,000 to 2,000 units of expiring hemoglobin a month. So it has licensed a yeast strain that it aims to use to create its own hemoglobin in the lab. Over a video call from her desk, Haynes showed me a sample of ready-to-use ErythroMer. It was encased in a heavy-duty storage bag with two chambers. One was full of scarlet powder—the hemoglobin product—and the other of sterile water to rapidly rehydrate it in the field. Eventually, she explained, under Murray’s master plan, “you’ll have another container with the synthetic platelet, and then a third container with the plasma.” But achieving that vision depends on finding a synthetic platelet that works. To do that, DARPA has turned to Anirban Sen Gupta, a bioengineering professor at Case Western Reserve University. “Traditionally platelets were thought of [as] blood cement, but it’s not that simple,” he explains. “They are, in my opinion, like drones, surveilling the blood vessel wall continuously, looking for signs of injury.” Sen Gupta’s team, which also includes collaborators at the University of Pittsburgh, used FSHARP funding to continue work on a product called SynthoPlate. Fabricated from a lipid nanoparticle that he likens to the handle of a Swiss Army knife, it has specialized peptide attachments—the biological equivalents of a corkscrew or a nail file. The first version has three: One binds to a chemical called von Willebrand factor, which helps platelets stick to the walls of injured blood vessels; another attaches to collagen, which acts as a scaffold for regrowing cells; and the third mimics a blood protein called fibrinogen, which acts like a Lego connector that allows the synthetic platelets to stick to real ones near a wound. Like ErythroMer, the product is freeze-dried and can be reconstituted with sterile water. It keeps for over a year at room temperature and at least three months at a desertlike 122 °F. In a 2026 paper in Advanced Science, the researchers showed that SynthoPlate cut bleeding time and blood loss by more than half in mice with low platelet counts. In a separate study, they also safely injected the faux platelets into rats’ bone marrow—a more useful delivery site, given that a bleeding patient might not have an available vein. Sen Gupta and a colleague have spun this work out into a company called Haima Therapeutics. The good news, as he sees it, is that the pharmaceutical industry excels at mass-­producing millions of billions of lipid nanoparticles—witness Operation Warp Speed, in which these blobs of fat held delicate snippets of mRNA in covid shots. “The magic of the artificial platelet is in all those peptides that are decorating the surface of the particle, but the scalability of the platform is very well established,” he says. Around the world, prospective blood alternatives abound. Researchers at Cambridge University in the UK are trying to pharm red blood cells for people with rare blood types or blood disorders like sickle cell anemia, and other researchers have also cultured platelets and red blood cells. Japanese scientists are already testing their own version of encapsulated human hemoglobin in human subjects. Over the course of the next three years, RAPIID will move DARPA’s efforts closer to real life. In addition to ErythroMer and SynthoPlate, the agency in early October announced funding for five other companies working on blood components. Among them is a firm called Abioremedi that eschews hemoglobin for synthetic versions of other oxygen-carrying proteins such as cytoglobin, neuroglobin, and myoglobin encased in albumin—the most abundant protein in human plasma. Octapharma USA won the sole contract to supply plasma to RAPIID and will also be competing in platelets with an FDA-approved pair of clotting proteins it affixes to a disc-shaped nanoparticle. With those players in place, the agency will focus for the next year on getting technologies through large FDA-regulated animal studies. Murray has set up a bit of a Hunger Games scenario: Each contractee working on blood components has only one year of guaranteed funding. He says he’s taking advantage of advancements in the field since FSHARP launched but is also hedging against any manufacturing or regulatory setbacks ahead of his 2029 deadline. Getting cut doesn’t mean the product is bad, just that the time frame was too demanding. The second and third years will focus on human trials, overseen by the University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus. That will include using the products in situations where the risk of side effects is outweighed by the danger of doing nothing, such as with severely injured people in ambulances. The possibility of fatal outcomes has invited second-guessing about DARPA’s choices. For Shibani Pati, who directs the Center for Research in Transfusion Medicine and Cell Therapies at the University of California, San Francisco, Murray could be making things too “DARPA hard” on himself by backing products like SynthoPlate and ErythroMer, which are previously untested in humans. “They need to make a very concerted effort to take what they’ve already developed and push it through to approval through the FDA,” she says. “Not start with moonshot projects that will take forever to get through.” For platelets, Pati thinks the RAPIID program would have done better to tap Cellphire, since its products have already received millions from the Pentagon and shown safety and efficacy in humans. (Murray notes that he can only select companies that apply, and Cellphire did not.) She has similar qualms about ErythroMer. A different HBOC that sources hemoglobin from cattle, called Hemopure, has been approved for use since 2001 in South Africa, she notes. And though it hasn’t been approved by the FDA—it was part of the fateful 2008 JAMA study—US doctors are cleared to use it for patients who have bad immune responses or religious objections to conventional transfusions. Holcomb, the surgery professor, worries the effort will run into issues with interactions between the products. He notes that individual blood components have more than one role: Red blood cells also help with clotting, platelets also are anti-inflammatory, and plasma helps tune coagulation. There’s no guarantee that the versions RAPIID helps develop will work in the same ways. Murray says that FSHARP’s animal studies tested all the products together, using whole blood as a baseline, precisely because of such possible interactions. The cocktail met its program metrics in all but one area: The clotting was not quite as good, perhaps because negative charges in some molecules repel one another. “I do not think they will replicate all the functions that whole blood does in our body,” Holcomb says. But, he admits, “you may not need to, right? When you get seriously injured and you lose 50% of your intravascular volume, you’re dead, right? So if you lose 30% and maybe you’re hanging on to life just barely, maybe you show up with all these different products, and you have enough whole blood left in you to do these other functions.” Murray is determined to complete trials so that shelf-stable blood can be delivered in 2029. In addition to the storage bags, he’s already sourcing finger sticks for in-field blood testing and a battery-powered device to heat up the blood components. The danger of blood runs—and, by extension, the vulnerability of millions in combat zones, maternity wards, and ambulances—drives his scientific daring. Knowing the stakes, he told his boss in 2024 that even if FSHARP failed, “we should invest 50 million more dollars in it and do it again.” Andrew Rosenblum is a technology journalist and the author of the techno-thriller Stretch Goals. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences.
