MIT Technology Review
Open models have real benefits. Do they outweigh the risks?
2026-10-13

Open models have real benefits. Do they outweigh the risks?

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
MIT Technology Review Explains: Let our writers untangle the complex, messy world of science and technology to help you understand what’s coming next. You can read more from the series here. Late last month, Anthropic released a report arguing that the model GLM-5.3 poses a major cyberattack risk. The report’s core concern is that the powerful new model, released in August by the Chinese company Z.ai, is freely available for anyone to download and modify. Anthropic’s assertion is only the latest move in a long-running debate about whether frontier AI models should be made open, like GLM-5.3, or should stay locked behind the doors of the frontier labs. These days, much of the debate about how to handle open models is playing out in public—and there’s a lot of money resting on its outcome. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who has a lot to gain if people continue buying their own hardware to run open models, advocates enthusiastically for the United States to develop more of them, whereas Anthropic’s Dario Amodei, who runs a company that could be undermined by cheap, powerful models, advocates for a more cautious approach. But beneath all these commercial incentives, there is real substance to disagreements about open models. “I think a lot of the way you come down on the openness debate is influenced by how you come down on the AI debate,” says David Gray Widder, an assistant professor at the University of Texas at Austin School of Information. Those who are concerned about AI safety warn that open models can easily be misused by bad actors, whereas those who focus more on AI’s practical potential urge that this power should not sit behind the closed doors of just a few big companies, and that open models could help spread it more widely. There are a range of ways to handle open models between banning them entirely and releasing them willy-nilly. But any approach that a government or a society might take still involves trade-offs between safety and democratization. Deciding whether to support or limit the production of open models requires understanding those risks and benefits in depth. Let’s start with the basics. A closed model, like the models that power ChatGPT and Claude, can only be accessed through a website, app, or API. An open model, on the other hand, can be directly downloaded: The details about its internal structure, encoded in a long series of numbers known as the model weights, are publicly available. Such models are sometimes called “open-weight models,” to distinguish them from models for which even more information, such as details about the training procedure, is available. Right now, closed models are more capable than open models—as of publication, the top eight models on the Artificial Analysis LLM leaderboard are all closed, though open models are only a handful of months behind in development. But there are several reasons why someone might opt to use an open model rather than a closed one. Because open models are downloadable, people can run them on their own computers or hard drives. They can even retrain them to behave differently, if they have the expertise and resources to do so. If you’re an academic researcher working to understand and improve AI systems, you almost certainly need to work with open models in your research. When working with closed models, researchers can’t do much more than send prompts and observe the responses. But researchers can observe the inner workings of open models, as well as the “chain of thought” that the models use to think through a problem before answering. They can also combine multiple open models to build new systems, compress them to make them more efficient, and fine-tune them for new applications. Businesses, too, have reasons to prefer open models. Even if you depend on an external provider to run them for you, open models still typically cost less than closed models, according to a recent report from Mozilla. And if businesses want to use AI to process sensitive data or proprietary information, running a local model on their own hardware may be the best way to keep their operations secure. But perhaps the most important quality of open models is that they challenge the tight control the frontier labs have over how most of us use AI. “The clearest, biggest argument [for open models] is around preventing mass concentration of power—it is about making sure that this foundational technology is a public good,” says Elizabeth Seger, senior policy advisor at the Tony Blair Institute for Global Change. “I think we’re at this juncture where the question of who controls the infrastructure that allows AI to operate is as big of a question as who gets to control information on the internet.” Seger sees the future of AI playing out in different ways, depending on the role that open models have. In one possible future, frontier AI systems are controlled entirely by select people, companies, or governments, and as those systems grow more powerful, those groups have increasing control over what our world looks like. The world’s most sophisticated hacking models, for example, are currently all made in the United States, so it’s entirely up to US companies and the US government whether other countries can use them to shore up their own cyber defenses. If those companies remain in the lead, we could in principle wind up in a world in which the United States is capable of hacking any other country, but no one is capable of hacking it. Frontier open models could change that balance. “If every individual had a loyal-to-them AI system that was safe [and secure], I think that would be a much less concerning world than one in which they have to just rely on a government that says, ‘Yeah, we’ve got a super intelligent AI system, it’s got this plan, it’s inscrutable to all of you, just trust us,’” says Hamish Hobbs, director of AI policy at the Centre for Long-Term Resilience. But open models are not a geopolitical panacea. Paul Azunre, who cofounded Ghana NLP, an organization that builds open-source AI tools for low-resource Ghanaian languages, notes that open models can also function as a way for companies to benefit from the labor and expertise of less-privileged nations and consolidate more power in their own hands. He identifies a pattern in which AI companies release models openly, observe how the community uses and modifies them, and then integrate those same improvements into their own products—without any credit or compensation for the people who originated those innovations. Mark Zuckerberg himself has said that the ability to integrate community improvements into Meta’s stack is a key benefit of releasing open models. “I think the ideal of open source is a very noble one, and there’s a lot of value that it brings,” Azunre says. “But the way it’s actually implemented in practice, it tends to be a very extractive approach.” The past few months have made it clear that safety remains a gnarly problem for closed models. There’s the autonomous hack that OpenAI agents executed against Hugging Face in July, but human misuse is a real threat too: According to a recent risk report from Anthropic, several extensive hacking operations have been undertaken with the assistance of Claude. So closed models are risky enough—and open models would likely be much easier to exploit. Before Anthropic passes a user’s query to one of its closed models, it uses automated systems to detect whether that query relates to hacking, biological weapons, or other risks. When it detects that users have outsmarted those filters, it can shut down their accounts. But when someone downloads a model and runs it on their own hardware, there’s no filter checking whether their prompts are safe, and there’s no way to revoke their access to the model. There are other important lines of defense. AI companies train their models to refuse harmful requests—so even if a bad prompt gets through Claude’s filters, there’s a good chance that the model will still say no. In principle, this same defense could work for open models, because it’s intrinsic to the model itself. Unfortunately, researchers have shown that it’s surprisingly easy to retrain a “safe” open model to agree to engage in the behaviors it was previously trained to avoid. Because of these facts, some people have argued that open models are simply too dangerous to release. An open model capable enough to walk someone through synthesizing a virulent new pathogen, for example, could prove catastrophic in the wrong hands. But it would be a vast oversimplification to deem open models dangerous and closed models safe. The defenses that AI companies build around their closed models are far from perfect, and people misuse those models all the time, as the Anthropic risk report makes clear. Even though open models have even fewer defenses against misuse, bad actors might still opt to use closed models because they are more accessible than open models with similar capabilities, which require lots of expensive computer chips to run locally (and even more chips if you want to retrain them). In fact, some experts who spoke with MIT Technology Review argued, by analogy to open-source software, that open models have safety advantages over closed models. Open-source tools, such as the Linux operating system, are often seen as safer than their closed-source counterparts because their vulnerabilities have been probed and patched by a vast community of online contributors. Similarly, communities of researchers can more easily test open models for security flaws, though fixing those flaws is trickier than rewriting a line of code. Open models can also be deployed more broadly than closed models for cyber defense. Any tool that can be used for hacking can also be used to prevent it: Defenders hack their own software to uncover flaws in need of patching. Anthropic has shown how this can work for closed models with its Project Glasswing initiative, which enables some trusted companies to use its most capable models for cyber defense. But plenty of other companies were left to fend for themselves. Open models, however, can be used much more widely. After it was hacked by OpenAI agents, Hugging Face emphasized the fact that it had to depend on an open model to analyze the hack, because questions about it triggered the closed models’ security filters. Nevertheless, many experts still perceive open models as riskier than closed models. The time-tested open-source security logic doesn’t necessarily hold up when dealing with tools as powerful as today’s—and tomorrow’s—models. “Where it gets complicated is when you start taking seriously the one-off instances of particularly catastrophic incidents that might be lower probability, but if they happen would be disastrous,” says Seger. If a wildly powerful open hacking model were dropped into the world today, for example, a bad actor could devastate our technological infrastructure before anyone had a chance to shore up their defenses. An event like that could be impossible to recover from. The risks can look even more dramatic when you consider what models might do of their own accord. The OpenAI Hugging Face hack only occurred because the company’s models were being tested with many of their cybersecurity guardrails removed, and open models have very few guardrails in the first place. If open models did decide to misbehave, it might be difficult to detect what was happening because they are so decentralized. For this reason, people who take seriously the purported catastrophic risks posed by rogue agents, such as Dario Amodei, tend to be more skeptical of open models. A few years ago, the debate about open models had an all-or-nothing framing: Either you supported open models because of their potential to fight concentration of power, or you rejected them because of the safety risks. These days, however, people are expending a lot of effort to find some middle ground. One way they are doing that is by devising techniques to mitigate the safety risks of open models. Most of the safety tools used for closed models, such as filters and monitoring, aren’t applicable to open models, but there are other possibilities. Some researchers are interested in trying to train models that are never exposed to training data that teaches them about bad or otherwise undesirable behavior—an open model that never learns about hacking, for example. But purging training datasets of all references to misbehavior without harming model performance remains a challenge. Other researchers are working to build models that are resistant to retraining, so that an open model’s safety training can’t be so easily removed. The theory behind these “tamper-resistant” models makes a lot of sense: If you repeatedly try to retrain a model to be unsafe and reinforce any resistance that the model shows to that retraining, eventually you’ll get a model that’s fortified against future retraining attempts. In practice, however, no current approaches to making models tamper-resistant hold up against all tampering attacks, according to a recent preprint. But Saad Hossain, an engineer at the AI medical imaging company Deep Breathe and the paper’s lead author, hopes that future techniques will prove more effective. “My reason for optimism is that it’s a very active area of research, and a lot of people are trying to come up with new and better methods,” he says. If the risks of open models are less black-and-white than they might at first seem, so are the benefits. Publishing the internal structures, or weights, of models might not be enough to disrupt AI-mediated concentration of power. “I think there are very genuine benefits to open source in terms of deconcentrating power, but they’re not sufficient in themselves,” Hobbs says. Open weights are a bare minimum standard for open models: Researchers can learn much more about how models work if they have access to details about their training, including the data on which they were trained, but AI companies are typically reluctant to divulge this information. Accessibility matters for open models too. Without computer chips that can run sophisticated models, you can’t actually use the model weights, even if you have them. Those chips have become prohibitively expensive as AI companies snap up more and more of them. “Openness is a floor, not a ceiling, for what we need to do to ensure that our technological futures benefit everyone,” Widder says. Real openness—not only open weights, but also open training data and accessible hardware—could also bring more risks, of course. If everyone has access to advanced AI hardware and advanced models to run on it, then anyone could potentially be a hacker. But there are strategies for mitigating these risks. Many experts advocate for some version of staged release, in which models start their life closed and gradually become more and more freely accessible as their safety is assured. Figuring out exactly how to implement such strategies will require striking a careful balance between the risks and benefits of open models. But if open models are a necessary element of a positive AI future, as some experts believe, that effort will be well worth it. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
AI labs are buying data from dead startups to train their models
2026-10-13

AI labs are buying data from dead startups to train their models

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-13

Visit Original Website
When Keelan Miskell began winding down his crypto startup this year, he didn’t think its data was worth much: the users were anonymous, the company just a few years old. But he’s since turned the firm’s digital archives into real money. He’s netted roughly $25,000 for his code base and a still-pending amount (likely around $15,000) for the workspace data in Google, Notion, Figma, and Slack. “At least all that stuff we were doing for two and a half years is worth something to someone somewhere,” he says. The sale was brokered by a company called SimpleClosure, part of a new growth sector in tech: businesses catering to dying startups. Estimates from Carta and AngelList show shutdowns rising between 26% and 56% annually in recent years, and an end-of-life ecosystem—including coaches, “death doulas,” and firms that help handle the legal, tax, and operational headaches of going ­belly-up—has emerged. Now tech undertakers are selling the data remains to AI labs. SimpleClosure added data sales to its services in April. Its Asset Hub platform helps founders sell their code and workspace data—emails, chats, documents. Buyers include foundation AI labs, vendors that construct simulated workspaces for AI training, and mature tech companies building their own models. Asset Hub has handled over 150 deals and expects to return over $10 million to startups by the end of 2026. CEO Dori Yona has seen these transactions, once rare and “somewhat taboo,” become increasingly routine, with 47% of SimpleClosure deals now including code or data sales. And it’s got company. Industry rival Sunset also has a rapidly growing data resale business. Founder and CEO Brendan Mahony says Sunset values a dying client’s assets by head count, years in operation, volume of data, and how the team used its tools. Particle board, he says, is a good analogy: “This operational exhaust data is like the wood shavings that everyone thought had no value, but it actually has a ton.” Demand for defunct companies’ data emerged suddenly last year, as AI training labs hit the limits of public and synthetic datasets. Surge AI, for example, buys data to build “reinforcement-learning gyms” where clients’ AI agents practice tasks—think debugging a complex IT architecture or drafting appellate briefs. Nick Heiner, who leads the company’s work on these environments, says he seeks data archives reflecting a range of situations, from disagreements to tricky judgment calls. Heiner explains that one company’s Slack history, if it captures conversations with variety and depth, can be worth more than another’s Slack, email, and calendars combined. Real archives avoid common errors in synthetic worlds, such as references to dates that don’t exist, but the extent of performance gains remains unclear. No company has yet released tests comparing results of real-world data training with the alternatives. Pricing reflects this uncertainty: Buyers quote wildly different numbers, Heiner says, because valuations are based on “research theses” estimating how many useful tasks come from, say, 100,000 lines of code. That hasn’t stopped bigger deals from happening. This past August, as part of Spirit Airlines’ bankruptcy, Google outbid the AI data company Mercor to buy the carrier’s decades of data, including 100 million emails, 500 million Teams messages, and 176,000 employee records. The price tag: $10 million. And Bloomberg reported in September that SpaceX’s AI unit is weighing buying data from struggling startups. Legally, this all could get iffy. Spirit’s flight attendants’ union objected to the sale on privacy grounds, and Greg Ewing, an attorney who advises AI startups, has called such data “legally radioactive.” Suppliers employ data scientists to scrub personally identifiable information and exclude HR records, and buyers say they scrub too. But Ewing points out some of the private details that lurk in emails and calendars: doctor appointments, school pickup arrangements, date-night plans. He describes a “Wild West of AI training” that is only starting to give way to proper licensing agreements and vetted use. There’s also the question of who profits from these windfalls. The proceeds typically flow to senior creditors and investors—not employees. To Ifeoma Ajunwa, a law professor at Emory University and author of The Quantified Worker, this represents a form of “captured capital.” Legal precedent allows for monitoring to help workers do their jobs, but once a firm is out of business, that justification vanishes. “Employers should have express consent before selling workers’ data,” says Ajunwa, but she adds that there’s no federal law banning such sales. While many see this new market as a chance for failing businesses to recover some value, these wind-down sales also have limitations. Founders have little to no visibility into who is buying the data and what they plan to do with it. Plus, there’s little assurance they’re getting a fair offer. Data prices will likely stabilize in the coming year, though, unless an AI bubble pops first. Either way, tech’s new undertakers will be there to appraise what’s left. Matthew King is a technology and environmental journalist based in Baltimore. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Huskeys Launches Profiling to Advance Proactive Network Edge Security
2026-10-14

Huskeys Launches Profiling to Advance Proactive Network Edge Security

The Next Web | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
For security teams, detecting a threat is only part of the challenge. They must also determine which application is at risk, how the activity relates to legitimate business operations, and what action will reduce exposure without interrupting users. Huskeys is introducing Profiling to address those questions, giving organizations a dynamic, business-aware view of their web applications and APIs. The launch was first reported by SiliconANGLE. Profiling is designed to connect application endpoints, real users, behavior, existing security controls, and business-critical workflows in one profile. Huskeys plans to use that context to help organizations prepare more targeted controls instead of relying exclusively on generic rules or responding after suspicious activity has already been identified. Many organizations protect their applications through a collection of third-party technologies, including CDNs, bot management systems, web application firewalls (WAFs), and API security platforms. While these tools address different parts of the security environment, each may operate with only a partial view of the application. Huskeys argues that this fragmentation limits the ability to make decisions based on the broader purpose of a request. Security controls that lack application context can block legitimate traffic, while malicious activity may escape appropriate scrutiny. Profiling is designed to build a more complete understanding of each application. It examines how endpoints are used, who accesses them, how the application behaves, which controls are already in place, and which business paths require particular attention. This goes beyond discovering assets or cataloging APIs. The goal is to connect application activity with business intent so that security decisions reflect the operational significance of a request. “A profile changes the question from ‘What does this traffic look like?’ to ‘What does this traffic mean to this business?’” said Roy Weisfeld, Co-Founder and CTO of Huskeys. “Most organizations protect everything the same way, like a mall with one security team guarding every store identically, whether it’s a luxury boutique or a snack stand. We give every store its own dedicated guard, tailored to what it needs. When a sale triples the foot traffic or a new store opens, our guards adapt in real time.” Profiling forms the foundation of Huskeys’ Hardening Loop, which is intended to connect application understanding directly to security improvements. The process begins with a profile of the business path. Huskeys then detects changes, abuse, or exposure and identifies a potentially high-impact fix. Customers retain approval authority before the company delivers the control, after which the system observes the impact. This structure is intended to move security operations beyond the production of alerts. Rather than requiring teams to investigate every finding independently and coordinate changes across disconnected tools, Huskeys aims to connect identified issues with controls specific to the affected application. “Once we understand the application, the intent, and the business path together, security controls can be scoped to the customer instead of relying on generic rules,” adds Yotam Mazurik, the company’s Research Team Lead. “Profiling gives us the context to turn detection into a control that is specific to the application and the business path. The customer decides; Huskeys delivers.” The approach also reflects the company’s view that faster response is not the only way to improve security. Preparing appropriate protections around an application could help organizations address exposure before an attack reaches the application. Application security is becoming more complicated as AI agents join human users and conventional automated systems in interacting with web applications. Agents are beginning to perform activities such as shopping and transactions, creating situations in which legitimate automation and malicious activity may appear similar when judged by traffic signals alone. Huskeys cites Cloudflare’s 2026 data that automated agents and bots accounted for more than half of web requests across its network. In this environment, Huskeys believes understanding the intended application path can provide an additional basis for deciding whether activity should be allowed, challenged, or blocked. The company also points to the shrinking interval between vulnerability disclosure and exploitation as a reason to rethink security operations. “Security teams have spent years trying to respond faster,” Weisfeld said. “We believe there is another approach: understand what matters before the next incident, so you can remove the window instead of racing the clock.” Huskeys Profiling is available in beta for close customers. According to the company, implementation requires no special onboarding, and customers can begin seeing value within hours. The launch advances Huskeys’ broader Network Edge Security Management (NESM) strategy. Its platform connects existing CDNs, WAFs, cloud security, and network edge technologies through a unified agentic layer, with the aim of continuously assessing environments and dynamically adapting policies. By combining application context, customer-approved security decisions, and ongoing assessment, Huskeys wants to make protection more specific to the risks and operational requirements of each application. The company’s approach centers on a simple premise: reducing exposure should not come at the expense of legitimate business activity. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! The Next Web
AI trading agents face a reliability bar human apps never did
2026-10-14

AI trading agents face a reliability bar human apps never did

The Next Web | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
Picture two AI trading agents running the same model and the same strategy. A signal arrives during a fast market, both send an order, and one platform answers in milliseconds while the other times out. Same intelligence, different result. The difference sat in the plumbing. As agents take on more of the work of trading, the infrastructure connecting them to markets plays a growing role in what they can actually deliver. For a consumer app, reliability is a user-experience problem. A delay means a spinning wheel, and the person taps again. For an agent it turns into a correctness problem. An agent making hundreds of automated calls reads a timeout as missing data, so it may act on stale account information, skip a check or retry an order into a worse price. That gap is drawing money. Arcade raised $60 million in June on the view that most agents fail because the infrastructure around them isn’t ready, and TechCrunch reported that MCP servers have had to do extra work simply to track sessions across load-balanced machines, one reason large integrations were slow to ship. “Markets do not pause when someone closes an app or steps away from a screen. The early adoption of Agent OS points to growing demand for agents that can remain connected as market conditions change: in the first week, MCP connections doubled and call volumes increased twentyfold, within two weeks, daily requests exceeded 90,000 with 95% completed within 60 milliseconds and a 97% success rate,” says Jeff Li, VP of Product at Binance. “The next phase of market access will be shaped not only by what agents can do, but by how quickly and reliably they can respond when the market moves.” Those figures reward a close reading. A 95th-percentile result means one request in 20 took longer than 60 milliseconds, and fast markets are exactly where that slower tail matters. At a 97% success rate, a system handling more than 90,000 daily requests would see roughly 2,700 fail each day, by simple arithmetic. The published numbers also leave open where latency is measured and what counts as a failure. Latency, success rate and behavior under load look like separate metrics. For an agent they multiply. A person sends one request and reacts, while an agent chains several together. Suppose a workflow needs five sequential calls to check a balance and read the order book as well as size a position and place the order and confirm the fill, and each call succeeds 97% of the time independently. In that case the full sequence completes cleanly about 86% of the time. Load is the hardest of the three, because demand spikes precisely when prices move and failures tend to cluster at the worst moment. MCP’s maintainers moved to a stateless design in July so servers can run behind standard load balancers without relying on one machine to remember each session. “Before, you needed to have a session store and manage session IDs, and if one of your compute pods went down, all of a sudden the requests would start failing,” says Den Delimarsky, a lead maintainer of MCP. Binance Research made a related point when it shared its two-week figures: as agents become more capable, the constraint may increasingly shift from model intelligence to the infrastructure needed to access data and execute reliably. Trading has learned this before, at considerable cost. On August 1, 2012, a faulty software deployment at Knight Capital sent more than 4 million orders into the market in 45 minutes while trying to fill 212 customer orders. The firm traded more than 397 million shares and lost more than $460 million, according to the SEC, which later settled market access rule charges with Knight for $12 million. For agent builders, the takeaway is uncomfortable. Automated systems can fail faster than anyone can step in, so reliability has to be designed in from the start. Payments went through a similar evolution. Card networks and settlement systems still compete on availability and throughput, yet over time those properties became the price of entry for handling other people’s money. Agent platforms look set to follow. Once one publishes latency and success-rate figures, the others will be asked for theirs, and the published number turns into a floor. Anyone connecting an agent to a funded account can put a few direct questions to the platform. At which percentile is latency reported, and is it measured at the server or end to end? What counts as a failed request, and what does the agent do after one? Context matters as much as averages. How did the figures hold up during the busiest hour of the most volatile day, compared with a launch-week snapshot? Are there rate limits, and what happens to requests above them? Does the platform publish these numbers over time, or only once? Agent platforms may increasingly publish execution metrics the way exchanges already publish system status, and the ones that do it consistently will make those questions much easier to answer. Revolut launches its mobile plan in Germany for €14.99 a month

The Next Web | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The reception of Revolut’s new global headquarters at YY London in Canary Wharf. Revolut has launched its own mobile phone plan in Germany, the company announced today. The fintech, which recently won a French banking licence, is pushing further beyond its banking app. Revolut Mobile costs €14.99 a month and includes 120GB of data at full speed, then unlimited data at a slower 2 Mbit/s, plus unlimited calls and texts within Germany. Also, customers can cancel each month, and there are no connection or switching fees. The service runs on Telefónica Germany’s network, with speeds of up to 100 Mbit/s. It is run by telecoms firm 1GLOBAL, with Revolut acting on its behalf. The plan works only as an eSIM and is set up inside the Revolut app. It will be rolled out to the company’s more than 3 million customers in Germany over the coming weeks. Users get 40GB of roaming data in Europe, a free NordVPN subscription, and a pass for messaging apps in more than 140 other countries. They can keep their number, add up to three extra numbers, or pay more for a so-called VIP number. Revolut’s reward points can cover all or part of the bill. “The telecom industry has been rigid and complex for years,” Beatrice Cossa-Dumurgier, Revolut’s chief executive for Western Europe, wrote on LinkedIn. “We’re bringing the same disruptive energy to mobile that we brought to banking.” Revolut Mobile was built as an internal startup under the company’s “New Bets” programme, she said. The company now has 11 product lines, each bringing in more than €120 million a year, it said, and has more than 80 million customers worldwide. Germany is not the first market for the service. Revolut started rolling out Revolut Mobile in the UK in December 2025, on Vodafone’s network. “Our partnership with Revolut and 1GLOBAL marks a new chapter for the German mobile market,” said Jörg Kablitz, chief partner and wholesale officer at Telefónica Germany. European Commission plans to use AI to respond faster to cyberattacks

European Commission plans to use AI to respond faster to cyberattacks

The Next Web | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
The European Parliament’s Paul-Henri Spaak building in Brussels According to a document obtained by Politico, the European Commission’s IT department wants to use AI to fight back against the most dangerous cyberattacks. DG DIGIT, the Commission’s IT department, wrote the paper, though it is undated, and it explains how the EU’s executive branch should get ready to defend against AI-driven threats. According to Politico, the main goal is speed, as the Commission’s cyber team wrote that just spotting an attack is not enough if staff cannot stop it quickly. DG DIGIT plans to use automatic defences in certain high-risk situations. The details of these cases were redacted in the version shared with Politico. The paper says the plan will not allow machines to act without oversight. There will be strict controls, and people will remain in charge. Moreover, the document also outlines the threats it expects and says that advanced AI models will make attacks faster, larger, and harder to detect. It warns about attacks that could happen outside office hours, spread quickly through systems, or cause major data theft before staff can respond. According to Politico, the cyber division has requested internal approval to move forward. It is unclear whether the plans are already being used. The Commission did not reply to Politico’s request for comment. Governments have been racing to deal with AI’s growing ability to find and exploit security flaws. In July, OpenAI said its own models had breached Hugging Face after breaking out of a secure test, and incidents like that have led to calls for more caution around the most powerful AI models. In September, Commission chief Ursula von der Leyen used her State of the Union speech to back an AI slowdown. She said the EU would bring AI labs together to talk about how to pace the frontier. From left: Markus Grabmeier (President of Electrical Products at Siemens AG), Taavi Madiberk (CEO of Skeleton Technologies), Barry Powell (President and Regional CEO of Siemens Electrical Products) Siemens has bought into Skeleton Technologies, an Estonian maker of energy storage systems. With this, the deal values Skeleton at more than $1 billion, its CEO Taavi Madiberk told Bloomberg. The deal closed this month and was worth more than $100 million, the company said, but it did not give the exact figure. In return, Siemens can sell Skeleton’s high-power storage systems to data centres, and no one else can, Siemens tech chief Peter Koerte told Bloomberg. Skeleton’s systems help data centres smooth out sudden swings in power use and reduce energy waste. “What we see is that also up to 40% more compute can be had on the chips if the power management is done right and the energy is not burned off,” Madiberk said. Skeleton says it works with all the big US cloud firms, and that its kit will soon be used across a gigawatt of data centre capacity. Skeleton plans to spend more than $200 million on growing in the US, including building factories there, Madiberk said. Bloomberg reported in March that the company is aiming for a US stock market listing next year. The two firms have already worked together on Skeleton’s €220 million plant near Leipzig, Germany, where Siemens helped build an automated production line. Siemens wants to sell data centres as a full package, Koerte said. It already supplies them with power gear and design software. “We don’t need the next OpenAI right now, what we need is the next generation of AI, which will primarily operate at the application level,” Koerte said. “We need to further expand the infrastructure.” Skeleton was founded 17 years ago, and it brings together more than 10 Estonian tech companies valued at over $1 billion, a high number for a country of 1.3 million people. Rising power demand from AI is putting pressure on grids and data centre builders across Europe. Other big tech groups are also investing in Europe’s AI build-out. INmune Bio and Quantum Computing partner to apply quantum optimization and AI to advance Alzheimer’s drug research and explore new applications for XPro
2026-10-14

INmune Bio and Quantum Computing partner to apply quantum optimization and AI to advance Alzheimer’s drug research and explore new applications for XPro

The Next Web | 2026-10-14

Visit Original Website
INmune Bio Inc. and Quantum Computing Inc. collaboration Boca Raton, Florida, October 14th, 2026, Financewire Collaboration combines INmune Bio’s clinical research with QCi’s Dirac-3S quantum optimization machine to identify potential new treatment indications and biomarkers for XPro. Initial engagement marks QCi’s first commercial customer in pharmaceutical and biotechnology markets, expanding the application of quantum computing to drug development. INmune Bio Inc. (Nasdaq: INMB) and Quantum Computing Inc. (Nasdaq: QUBT) today announced a collaboration to apply quantum computing, artificial intelligence (AI) and data analytics to identify new potential applications for XPro (XPro1595), INmune Bio’s investigational treatment for Alzheimer’s disease and other disease conditions. The collaboration brings together INmune Bio’s data from pre-clinical research through four clinical trials and understanding of disease biology with QCi’s Dirac-3S quantum optimization machine. Working with clinical and publicly available biomedical datasets, the companies intend to use quantum computing alongside AI and conventional data analytics to uncover relationships that could help find diseases driven by the same biology XPro targets, and to prioritize those with the greatest potential for success. The Stage 1 engagement represents QCi’s first commercial customer in the pharmaceutical and biotechnology market. The agreement also provides options for additional work as INmune Bio advances its clinical development programs. “We’ve built a large clinical dataset on Alzheimer’s and other diseases, backed by years of work with XPro,” said David Moss, Chief Executive Officer of INmune Bio. “This collaboration allows us to examine patient populations and biomarkers most likely to predict responses by analyzing large data sets. We’ll look for the diseases with the greatest potential for success, those whose human biology matches what we’ve already seen XPro do, starting with rare diseases where trials can be smaller, faster and more capital-efficient, and where qualifying pediatric approvals may earn a Priority Review Voucher.” Together, the companies will build on INmune Bio’s research into XPro to explore where its approach to targeting inflammation may offer the greatest potential. XPro is designed to selectively inhibit soluble tumor necrosis factor (TNF), a protein involved in inflammation, while preserving other TNF functions important to the immune system. INmune Bio is investigating its potential in neurological and rare diseases where this targeted approach may offer a therapeutic benefit. Using its Dirac-3S entropy quantum optimization machine alongside AI and conventional analytics, QCi will look for patterns and relationships across these datasets that could help researchers better understand treatment responses, identify promising patient populations and prioritize new indications for further study. “This is a great example of what practical quantum computing can look like today,” said Yuping Huang, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of QCi. “INmune Bio has a complex scientific problem and a substantial body of clinical and biological data. Our job is to bring new computing capabilities to that problem and see what we can learn. The potential to help researchers make better decisions about future treatments is exciting, and it opens an important new market for QCi.” An important part of the program will be testing where quantum optimization adds value. The companies plan to compare results from QCi’s quantum optimization machine with appropriate conventional statistical and machine-learning methods, evaluating which approaches are best suited to different parts of the analysis. The work is also intended to establish a repeatable discovery process through quantum computing that INmune Bio can use with additional datasets and potential disease indications. For INmune Bio, the collaboration offers a new way to evaluate the broader potential of XPro and prioritize future development opportunities. For QCi, it represents an opportunity to showcase how its room-temperature, low-power quantum computing technology can be applied to complex, real-world problems in pharmaceutical research. INmune Bio Inc. is a publicly traded (NASDAQ: INMB), late-stage biotechnology company focused on developing treatments that target the innate immune system to fight disease. The Company’s clinical-development strategy centers on advanced precision medicine, matching drug mechanisms directly to patient biology to optimize clinical outcomes. Quantum Computing Inc. (Nasdaq: QUBT) is an integrated quantum photonics company developing and manufacturing technologies that use light to compute complex information, capture hard-to-detect signals and communicate more securely. Headquartered in Hoboken, New Jersey, QCi has operations across the United States. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription.
